Tools to Design a Biomimetic Humanoid Robot Actuated
by Pneumatic Artificial Muscles and a Synthetic Neural Network

PDF PAGE 3
ii
Abstract
Biomimetic robots offer a platform for studying neuromechanical control through
experiments that would be impractical or unethical with living subjects. This dissertation
advances a long-running effort at Portland State University to build a humanoid robot,
from the trunk down, actuated by pneumatic artificial muscles and controlled by a synthetic
nervous system. The isometric force of Festo braided pneumatic actuators (BPAs) was
characterized over a wide range of resting lengths, contractions, and pressures using a
custom test jig. The analysis produced novel equations for the maximum isometric force
as a function of resting length and a three-coefficient nondimensional force surface that
predicts isometric force from relative strain and relative pressure. The actuator model was
carried to the joint level through isometric knee torque experiments on two test stands: a
simplified pinned knee and a biomimetic knee with a migrating instantaneous center of
rotation. This approach isolated the sources of prediction error, revealing constant length
offsets, series compliance in brackets and artificial tendons, and a loss of usable actuator
length when BPAs wrap around the joint. The biomimetic knee driven by optimized BPA
placements was shown to meet or exceed human isometric knee torque over most of the
range of motion. Tools were created for curating relevant neuromechanical research, as
well as simulating neuromechanical models based on human and robot biomechanical
models.

Acknowledgments
This work was funded by the Department of Mechanical and Materials Engineering
at Portland State University, the National Science Foundation (NSF) grant for NeuroNex:
Communication, Coordination, and Control in Nervous Systems (C3NS) #2015317, NSF
grant #1943483, and the NIH BUILD EXITO program (grant numbers 5RL5GM118963-06,
5TL4GM118965-06, 5RL5GM118963-07, and 5TL4GM118965-07).
I would like to thank my advisor, Dr. Alexander Hunt, for his guidance, patience,
and the freedom to pursue this project, and my committee members — Dr. Bin Jiang, Dr.
Radhika Reddy, and Dr. David Turcic — for their time and thoughtful feedback.
I am grateful to my collaborators and labmates: Mohammad Elzein and Lawrence Pang
for their work on the actuator characterization experiments; Lindie Burgess and Connor
Morrow for their work on the knee torque experiments; Cody Scharzenberger for building
the Muscle Mutt platform and for his assistance proofreading; Alex Steele, who designed
the initial biomimetic four-bar knee linkage used in the torque tests and generously shared
solid models and prototype experience; and Jasmine Bradley, whose work reworking the
figures improved their aesthetic quality and accessibility for readers with color vision
deficiencies.
Finally, I thank my family for their support throughout this long process.

1 Introduction
1.1 Motivation
Biomimetic robots provide a platform for investigating how mechanical structure and
control interact to generate movement, offering complementary insights to simulation-
based studies [Andrews, 1985, Millard et al., 2013, Arnold et al., 2010]. Wheeled robots
remain the platform of choice in structured environments, where their speed, efficiency,
and payload capacity are difficult to match; in the unstructured terrain of the natural and
built world, legged machines hold the advantage, and biomimetic legs that reproduce
human force capabilities have the potential to excel where wheels cannot [Frey et al., 2026,
Ha et al., 2025]. Musculoskeletal humanoids and limb systems driven by tendon-like
actuators have been shown to support biologically meaningful experiments on motion
generation, internal force transmission, and neural control hypotheses [Asano et al., 2017,
2019, Hitzmann et al., 2018, Liu et al., 2018, Chen et al., 2019]. The dual perspective of
using robots to learn about biology, and using biology to design more adaptive robots,
has been emphasized in both biomimetic robotics and biomechanics [Ijspeert, 2020, Asano
et al., 2017]. With such robotic systems, experiments can be performed that would not be
practical or ethical with human test subjects, such as removing a specific muscle from the
system or blocking a sensory feedback pathway. Experiments of this kind allow direct
testing of how the components of the neuromechanical system interact, and they offer new
opportunities for testing neural control theories that are faster and cheaper than human

PDF PAGE 25
2
observation studies [Shin et al., 2018, Goldsmith et al., 2021, Schilling et al., 2022].
Artificial muscles are a key enabling technology for this class of robot. Among the
available actuation technologies, McKibben-style braided pneumatic actuators (BPAs)
combine low mass, high force-to-weight ratio, and intrinsic compliance with a force–length
curve that qualitatively resembles that of skeletal muscle [Liang et al., 2020, Hunt et al.,
2017a]. BPAs have been used in quadrupedal robots [Aschenbeck et al., 2006, Hunt et al.,
2017a, Scharzenberger, 2019], musculoskeletal humanoids [Asano et al., 2017, Hitzmann
et al., 2018], and dexterous manipulation systems [Chen et al., 2019]. However, BPAs also
differ from biological muscle in important ways: their maximum tensile force occurs at
resting length rather than at an optimal fiber length, their maximum contraction ratio is
substantially lower than that of human muscle, and their force output is a highly nonlinear
function of internal pressure, contraction, and loading state [Hunt et al., 2017a, S ´arosi
et al., 2017, Liang et al., 2020]. The author’s experience with 10mm actuators made this
gap concrete: manufacturer specifications overstate the force and contraction available at
the short resting lengths a leg design requires, and designs based on those specifications
delivered less range of motion and joint torque than intended. Nor could the published
empirical models repair the discrepancy, because each is fitted to measurements at a single
resting length [S ´arosi et al., 2017, Martens and Boblan, 2017, Hunt et al., 2017a]; none
accurately predicts how force changes when a designer chooses a different actuator length.
Because muscle resting length is one of the primary degrees of freedom available to the
robot designer, the absence of a force model valid at arbitrary resting length blocked
rational actuator selection. Designing a biomimetic robot around BPAs therefore requires
quantitative, predictive models of the actuator and of the joint torques it can produce.

PDF PAGE 26
3
Actuator force alone, however, does not determine the torque a joint produces. The
robot knee transmits BPA force to the skeleton through mounting brackets, artificial
tendons, and paths that wrap over the joint, and each of these elements departs from the
rigid-body idealization: brackets and fixtures deflect under load, tendons stretch, and
a wrapping actuator loses usable length as the knee flexes. At the start of this work it
was unknown how much these transmission effects reduce the torque delivered at the
knee relative to the rigid-body prediction, and no method existed for correcting that
prediction in a form that would transfer between joint configurations. The biomimetic
knee’s optimized muscle routing [Morrow et al., 2020] compounded the problem: it had
never been validated experimentally or compared against human isometric torque data,
so even the nominal torque envelope of the robot leg rested on unverified calculations.
The remaining gaps concern the controller. No simulation pipeline existed for closing
the loop between a musculoskeletal robot model of this kind and a biologically realistic
neural controller. The neuromechanical modeling literature provides the ingredients,
including two-level spinal central pattern generator architectures [Rybak et al., 2006,
McCrea and Rybak, 2008], neuromechanical simulation frameworks [Markin et al., 2010],
and synthetic nervous system tooling [Nourse et al., 2023], but it does not provide their
assembly for a BPA-driven bipedal robot with human-like muscle routing. Wiring realistic
feedback into such a controller exposes a fourth gap, this one in the literature rather than
in methods: the physiological properties of the group Ia, group Ib, and group II afferents
that should inform the feedback design are scattered across hundreds if not thousands of
primary papers and reviews [Van de Crommert et al., 1998, Windhorst, 2007, 2022], with
no structured resource from which a robot designer or an animal researcher could draw

PDF PAGE 27
4
them.
Four gaps thus stood between the existing robot design and a robot that can walk under
neural control: no actuator force model valid at arbitrary resting length, no quantified
correction for the torque lost through brackets, tendons, and wrapping, no closed-loop
simulation pipeline joining the robot’s musculoskeletal model to a biologically realistic
neural controller, and no consolidated resource for the afferent data that such a controller
requires.
1.2 The Biomimetic Humanoid Robot Project
This dissertation is part of a long-running effort at Portland State University to build
a biomimetic humanoid robot from the trunk down, actuated by pneumatic artificial
muscles and controlled by a synthetic nervous system (SNS). The project is deliberately
iterative. Knowledge from biomechanics and neuroscience guides the design of robots
intended to perform complex tasks in unstructured environments with improved efficiency,
balance, adaptability, and resilience. Those robots then become physical models with which
biological hypotheses can be tested, validated, and refined through controlled changes
that would be impractical or unethical in living subjects. The resulting biological insight
informs the next generation of robot design, and lessons from both directions can be
translated into improved prosthetic and orthotic devices.
The present work builds directly on a series of studies by the author and collaborators.
In the author’s master’s project, the kinematics and dynamics of a biomimetic robot leg
were matched against the Gait2392 OpenSim model, artificial muscle attachment and wrap-

PDF PAGE 28
5
ping locations were determined for the robot legs, and MATLAB tooling was developed for
evaluating muscle lengths, moment arms, and isometric joint torques over the full range of
motion [Bolen, 2019, Bolen and Hunt, 2019]. That work showed that simply transplanting
human muscle attachment locations onto a BPA-actuated robot does not reproduce human
torque profiles; the placement optimization that closes this gap was completed during
this dissertation and is summarized in Section 7.1. In parallel, a biomimetic four-bar knee
linkage with a migrating instantaneous center of rotation was designed and validated by
Steele [Steele et al., 2017, Steele, 2018, Steele et al., 2018], and a canine-inspired quadruped
platform, Muscle Mutt, formerly called DoggyDeux, was developed with antagonistic BPA
pairs and synthetic nervous system control [Scharzenberger, 2019]. That platform extended
an earlier AARL demonstration of central pattern generator control of a pneumatically
actuated robot [Hunt et al., 2017b].
1.3 Research Objectives
This dissertation addresses the first two gaps experimentally, resolves the fourth by consol-
idating the afferent literature into the Sensory Afferent Database [Bolen et al., 2023, Bolen
and Hunt, 2024], and develops the tools and framework that close the third. The objectives
are:
1. Characterize the isometric force of Festo BPAswith uninflated diameters of 10 mm
and 20 mm over a wide range of resting lengths, contractions, and pressures; de-
rive improved maximum-force and normalized force–strain–pressure models; and
compare them against existing models (Chapter 3).

PDF PAGE 29
6
2. Validate and improve the prediction of isometric knee torqueproduced by BPAs
on pinned-joint and biomimetic four-bar knees; identify and model losses caused by
constant length offsets, series compliance, and actuator wrapping; and compare the
resulting torque envelopes with human capabilities (Chapter 4).
3. Consolidate the sensory afferent literature into a structured afferent resource, the
Sensory Afferent Database, by auditing and curating the published record of afferent
properties across animal species and their role in motor control, and by evaluating
AI-assisted literature triage for expanding the corpus (Chapter 5).
4. Develop tools and a framework for neuromechanical controlof the humanoid
robot by converting its OpenSim musculoskeletal model to MuJoCo, implementing
an SNS-Toolbox two-level spinal CPG with proprioceptive and heel/toe contact
feedback, and establishing a staged tuning and verification workflow (Chapters 6
and 7).
1.4 Dissertation Organization
Chapter 2 establishes the technical and scientific context for the dissertation. It reviews
biomimetic and musculoskeletal robots, robot actuation technologies with an emphasis
on braided pneumatic actuators and published models of their force–length–pressure
behavior, and the human lower-limb biomechanics and OpenSim benchmark models
that serve as design targets. It then covers the computational neuroscience of locomo-
tor control, from two-level central pattern generator architectures and sensory afferent
feedback to the reference circuits from which the dissertation’s controller architecture is

PDF PAGE 30
7
drawn. Two further topics frame the simulation work: neuromechanical simulation as a
concept, and the tooling used to execute it, from OpenSim and its SimTK ecosystem to
MuJoCo, SNS-Toolbox, and AnimatLab. The chapter closes with the ethical case for testing
neuromechanical hypotheses on robots rather than living subjects.
Chapter 3 develops and validates the actuator force models. It presents the isometric
force characterization of Festo BPAs with 10mm and 20 mm uninflated diameters over a
wide range of resting lengths, contractions, and pressures; derives improved maximum-
force and normalized force–strain–pressure models; and compares them against the manu-
facturer’s tool and published models. Later sections examine the effects of resting length,
the implications of the validated models for real-time control and simulation, and the
biomimetic analogy between braided pneumatic actuators and skeletal muscle. The chap-
ter’s text and figures are adapted from the author’s publication of this work [Bolen et al.,
2026].
Chapter 4 carries the actuator models to the joint. It describes the robot leg model, the
pinned-joint and biomimetic knee test stands, and the equipment used to collect isometric
torque data; formulates the hybrid torque calculation method and the improved torque
model whose correction terms account for a constant length offset, series compliance, and
actuator wrapping; and documents the optimization algorithms used both to identify
those terms and to redesign actuator routes. The chapter then reports the measured torque
on both knees, discusses the physical sources of prediction error, presents the follow-up
identification and the redesigned routes evaluated on the Gait2392 robot-body model, and
compares the resulting torque envelopes with human capabilities, before closing on the
transmission-design implications of mono- and bi-pulley routing.

PDF PAGE 31
8
Chapter 5 presents the Sensory Afferent Database, the structured afferent resource
built to answer the afferent-literature gap. It describes the audited corpus and its curation
coverage, in which papers carry structured curation notes, afferent classifications, and
notes translating each sensory pathway toward robot or simulation use; the packaging of
the corpus with its citation graph as a self-contained laboratory tool; and the evaluation of
AI-assisted literature triage for expanding the database, before closing by mapping the
corpus onto the controller’s sensory channels.
Chapter 6 builds the tools for neuromechanical simulation, with a stated goal: a
closed-loop environment in which a synthetic nervous system controls the experimentally
characterized robot model and supports the lesion, stimulation, and sensory-deletion
experiments that cannot be performed on living subjects. It opens with the preliminary
AnimatLab bipedal walker results, whose hand-tuned two-layer CPG produced suspended
stepping, with neither weight-bearing walking nor any role for afferent feedback in weight-
bearing motion demonstrated; that outcome set the verification-first approach the tools
follow. The tools themselves are the OpenSim-to-MuJoCo conversion of the robot’s muscu-
loskeletal model with its synthetic BPA knee, the Simulink SNS library built to prototype
reflex topologies, the spiking-neuron mirrors, and the cross-platform verification protocol
that ties the implementations together. Each tool is demonstrated on the fundamental
exercises that establish what it does and does not show: cross-engine reproduction of
the contact-driven rhythm core, headless re-runs of the archived AnimatLab reference
models, the BPA-actuated walker in MuJoCo, a gait-validation sweep against a locomotion
reference library, and a comparison of the production walker variants. The stimulus and
deletion experiments complete the demonstrations, and the chapter closes by discussing

PDF PAGE 32
9
the five-stage training curriculum, the spiking-neuron mirrors, and the new tools the
program leaves other researchers.
Chapter 7 discusses the work as a whole. It opens with the outcome, that improved
first-pass robot design is now achievable, and attributes it: the design factors that remove
the rigid-body idealization, the improved BPA characterization, the tiered attachment
and routing optimization, the sensory afferent database, and the new neuromechanical
simulation tools. Its remaining subsections scope the future work these results enable for
other researchers, from BPA characterization and routing expansion through database and
simulation tooling to real-time neural control of the physical robot. Chapter 8 consolidates
the principal contributions of the dissertation. Figure 1.1 summarizes the flow.
Appendix A documents the muscle force formulations and their cross-platform com-
parison, the BPA force model implementation, the muscle-path length and moment-arm
calculations, and the compliance and wrapping-loss implementations. Appendix B pro-
vides the neuron and synapse equations, spiking and non-spiking alike, used by the
synthetic nervous system tools, relates those equations to their SNS-Toolbox implementa-
tion, and records the spiking-mirror calibration and the connectomes of the spinal-network
models. Appendix C records the static BPA pathways, joint transformation matrices,
bracket reference frames, stiffness and compliance parameters, adopted correction-term
values, and the redesigned extensor route geometry.

2 Background
This chapter reviews the established knowledge against which the dissertation’s contri-
butions are defined: biomimetic and musculoskeletal robotics, the actuation technologies
available to them, the human lower-limb biomechanics they imitate, the neuroscience
of locomotor control they implement, and the simulation and ethical context in which
they are tested. Work from the author’s own program that goes beyond this background,
including the prior AnimatLab bipedal walker, the Sensory Afferent Database, and the
neuromechanical simulation pipeline, is presented as dissertation work in Chapters 5 and 6
rather than repeated here.
2.1 Biomimetic and Musculoskeletal Robots
Biomimetic robots offer many potential applications, ranging from assisting in factories
and environmental exploration to serving as physical models of human movement. By
developing high-fidelity biomimetic robots, we can perform tests that help improve our
understanding of both biomechanics and the underlying neuromechanical systems that
control movement [Shin et al., 2018, Asano et al., 2019, Ijspeert, 2020, Goldsmith et al.,
2021, Hunt et al., 2017b, Schilling et al., 2022]. A few such biomimetic robots exist, such
as the impressive Kengoro and Kenshiro robots of Asano et al. [2017] and Asano et al.
[2019], which have proportionally human bodies, anatomically correct musculotendon
arrangements, rolling-sliding joints, and joint ranges and output power comparable with
human-measured values; their artificial muscles are implemented with brushless DC

PDF PAGE 35
12
motors, wires as tendons, gears, and integrated tension measurement. Kengoro, the more
recent of the two, stands 1670 mm tall, weighs 56.5 kg, and carries 174 degrees of freedom
when the end effectors are included [Asano et al., 2017]. A dog-like robot developed
by Aschenbeck et al. [2006] and actuated with Festo BPAs was designed from a planar
kinematic model scaled to an adult greyhound, with three sagittal degrees of freedom
per leg driven by antagonistic BPA pairs; its front-leg pair alone lifted a 13.5kg payload,
almost double the complete robot’s predicted mass. That platform was used to show
how central pattern generators provide robust locomotion patterns that are adaptive to
perturbations in ground speed [Hunt et al., 2017b].
Robots built with artificial muscles thus provide opportunities to investigate how
muscles are controlled to produce desired motions; however, artificial muscles do not
exactly replicate muscle properties, and existing robots do not capture all of the degrees of
freedom and joint torques of the animal limbs they mimic. The biomimetic leg developed
and tested in this dissertation sits squarely in this lineage: a human-proportioned skeleton,
muscle-like actuators, and the goal of human-matched joint torques.
Figure 2.1 illustrates the human-scale skeletal structure and tendon routing of this
approach.
2.2 Robot Actuation Technologies
2.2.1 Overview
A wide range of actuation technologies have been explored in robotics, including electric
motors, hydraulic systems, smart-material actuators, and artificial muscles [He and Gao,

PDF PAGE 36
13
Figure 2.1: Kengoro human-mimetic musculoskeletal humanoid in neutral and upper-limb
poses, illustrating its human-scale skeletal structure and densely routed tendon-driven
musculature [Asano et al., 2017].

PDF PAGE 37
14
2020, Liang et al., 2020]. Electric motors are widely used due to their controllability and
maturity, but they are limited by thermal constraints, gearing requirements, and reduced
compliance compared to muscle-like actuators [Veale and Xie, 2016, Suzumori and Faudzi,
2018]. Hydraulic actuators offer exceptionally high power density and have enabled highly
dynamic legged systems, but they remain relatively heavy, less efficient than biological
muscle, and require a dedicated fluid infrastructure [Suzumori and Faudzi, 2018].
Among smart-material actuators, dielectric elastomer actuators (DEAs), a class of
electronic electroactive polymers, contract by a different mechanism: an applied voltage
squeezes a soft polymer film between compliant electrodes through electrostatic stress,
and the thinning and radial spreading of the film produces strains that can exceed bio-
logical muscle [Madden et al., 2004]. Comparative studies report high specific work and
good bandwidth for DEAs [Liang et al., 2020], but kilovolt-range driving voltages, elec-
tromechanical instability, and the compliant-electrode requirement have kept them out of
legged robots. Thermally activated artificial muscles reach the most extreme performance
figures of any technology surveyed by Liang et al. [2020]: twisted-fiber muscles support
loads more than one hundred times those of a biological muscle of the same length and
weight, with output stresses of 22 MPa. Their activation route, however, is shared with
shape-memory alloys, whose heating is fast but whose cooling is limited by heat transfer
away from the element, and that asymmetry sets the cycle time.
Closer to muscle, series-elastic and pneumatic actuators trade peak force and band-
width for compliance, a trade that wearable and rehabilitation robotics exploit because
force control and backdrivability matter most when a machine interacts with a human
[Aks¨oz et al., 2019, Leibach, 2020, Li et al., 2020]. A recent review of pneumatic artificial

PDF PAGE 38
15
muscle technology documents continued progress in actuator design and in assistive
applications [Zhagiparova et al., 2025]. None of these technologies yet combines muscle-
like force, strain, and compliance in one actuator, which is the combination a biomimetic
humanoid leg demands and that braided pneumatic actuators approximate most closely.
Figure 2.2 compares the operating mechanisms of these actuator technologies.
(A) Electric torque motor (B) Braided pneumatic actuator
(C) Hydraulic cylinder (D) Dielectric elastomer

2.2.2 Braided Pneumatic Actuators
In contrast to the technologies above, McKibben-style braided pneumatic actuators (BPAs)
combine low mass, high force-to-weight ratio, and intrinsic compliance with a force–length
curve that qualitatively resembles that of skeletal muscle [Liang et al., 2020, Hunt et al.,
2017a]. A BPA consists of an inflatable bladder inside a braided sleeve; pressurizing the
bladder expands the bladder radially and contracts the sleeve axially, producing tensile
force. As shown by Chou and Hannaford, the force–length relationship of McKibben
actuators is governed by geometric constraints imposed by the braided shell, leading to
a nonlinear dependence of force on actuator length through the braid angle [Chou and
Hannaford, 1996]: actuator force is proportional to pressure and to a nonlinear geometric
term (3 cos2 θ´ 1), which governs both the maximum contraction limit and the curvature
of the force–length relationship. Predicting their force as a function of length and pressure
is therefore the first improvement of the torque tools developed in this dissertation, and
the state of that prediction is the subject of the next subsection.
Figure 2.3 shows the commercial actuator assembly used in the laboratory.
However, BPAs exhibit several important differences from biological muscle. First,
BPA maximum tensile force occurs at resting (i.e., uninflated) length ( l0) rather than at
an optimal fiber length [Liang et al., 2020]. Second, their maximum contraction ratio
is substantially lower than that of human muscle, presenting challenges for achieving
biomimetic joint ranges of motion [Hunt et al., 2017a, S´arosi et al., 2017, Liang et al., 2020].
Third, BPAs have a highly nonlinear dependence on internal pressure ( P), contraction
(ϵ), and loading state [Hunt et al., 2017a, Martens and Boblan, 2017], which complicates

PDF PAGE 40
17
Figure 2.3: CAD representation of a Festo DMSP braided pneumatic actuator with the
mounting and pneumatic connections used in the laboratory assembly [Festo AG & Co. KG,
2026]. The long cylindrical body contains the bladder and braided sleeve; pressurization
through the end fittings converts radial expansion into axial contraction.

PDF PAGE 41
18
control and motivates high-fidelity force–length–pressure modeling. Despite these differ-
ences, their muscle-like properties have made them attractive for biomimetic quadrupeds
[Aschenbeck et al., 2006, Scharzenberger, 2019], humanoids [Asano et al., 2017, Hitzmann
et al., 2018], and musculoskeletal arms [Kurumaya et al., 2016, Chen et al., 2019].
2.2.3 BPA Force–Length–Pressure Models
Previous studies have generated empirical models of the force–length–pressure relation-
ship of BPAs. S ´arosi et al. [2017] discuss several high-fidelity BPA force models. They
present a static force model with a 21-coefficient polynomial function for a Festo MAS-
20-200N (i.e., I20 mm, l0 = 200 mm) with an impressive R2 = 0.9994, as well as S´arosi’s
static force model for a Festo DMSP-20-400N-RM-RM (i.e., I20 mm, l0 = 400 mm), with
six coefficients and a R2 = 0.9995 [S´arosi, 2012, S´arosi et al., 2013]. Martens and Boblan
present an even more accurate model, in terms of absolute error, using five coefficients for a
DMSP-10-250 (i.e., I10 mm, l0 = 250 mm) and a DMSP-20-300 (i.e., I20 mm, l0 = 300 mm)
[Martens and Boblan, 2017]. However, as demonstrated in this work, different resting
lengths produce different force–length curves, preventing the use of these single-length
models for designing systems with different resting lengths.
Hunt et al. [2017a] looked at six resting lengths of I10 mm Festo BPAs, accounting for
differences in maximum contractile percentages, and elucidated the force-length-pressure
relationship. In particular, for a given contraction percent and force F (in Newtons), the
scalar pressure P (in kPa) required to produce this force for a I10 mm Festo artificial
muscle can be determined by solving the equation:

2.3 Human Lower-Limb Biomechanics
Designing a biomimetic leg requires a quantitative benchmark of human muscle–joint
behavior. This dissertation uses OpenSim [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2011, 2018] and
the Gait2392 model, which descends from the interactive lower-extremity model of Delp

PDF PAGE 44
21
et al. [1990] and includes musculoskeletal geometry from Hoy et al. [1990], planar knee
kinematics from Yamaguchi and Zajac [1989], and a lower-limb model with 23 degrees
of freedom and 54 musculotendon actuators [Arnold et al., 2010]. Muscle moment arms,
force-generating properties, and functional roles in such models have been characterized
extensively [Zajac and Gordon, 1989, Brand et al., 1982, Menegaldo et al., 2004, Millard
et al., 2013, Sherman et al., 2013, Suderman and Vasavada, 2012], and standard reporting
conventions for kinematics and joint mechanics are established by the International Society
of Biomechanics [Wu and Cavanagh, 1995, Wu et al., 2002, 2005, Derrick et al., 2020].
The moment arm is the quantity that turns muscle force into joint torque, and two
methods of obtaining it anchor the literature. The geometric method computes the arm
directly from attachment-point data: the arm about a joint axis is the perpendicular distance
from that axis to the muscle’s line of action, evaluated at the configuration of interest.
Brand et al. [1982] assembled the canonical lower-extremity attachment anatomy from
cadaver measurements so that such computations could be made at all, and reported
that anatomical variation and marking errors rarely shift calculated moment arms by
more than twenty percent, usually by less than ten, while attachment data taken from
a single unscaled specimen can bias the resulting force predictions substantially. The
tendon-excursion method instead defines the arm as the sensitivity of musculotendon
length to joint angle, r=Bl/Bθ , obtained by perturbing a joint and differencing path
lengths. Menegaldo et al. [2004] fitted polynomial regressions of musculotendon lengths
and three-dimensional moment arms across the joint space of a lower-limb model so that
either quantity could be evaluated cheaply inside large simulations. Both methods deliver
configuration-dependent quantities rather than constants, and the dependence compounds

PDF PAGE 45
22
for multi-articular muscles, whose arm about one joint varies with the configuration of
every joint they cross [Zajac and Gordon, 1989, Young et al., 2019]; the full-muscle rat
hindlimb model of Young et al. [2019] makes that adjacent-joint dependence a first-class
result of its attachment-point technique. OpenSim evaluates moment arms as muscle-
length sensitivities of this kind, so the arms used throughout this dissertation are properties
of the Gait2392 geometry rather than measured inputs [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018].
The human knee is the joint where these definitions are stressed hardest. Its instanta-
neous center of rotation migrates posteriorly with flexion, a behavior captured by four-bar
linkage descriptions of the joint [Romero et al., 2020, Steele, 2018], and Romero et al. [2020]
treat locating that moving center as a kinematic problem in its own right, solved with
natural coordinates so that the answer stays cheap to evaluate when an optimization must
recompute it many times. That difficulty is visible at the repository level: the SimTK
repository that distributes OpenSim also hosts multiple projects dedicated specifically to
the human knee, and they differ in how they formalize joint torques, centers of rotation,
and reaction forces for the same anatomy. A hinge knee, a four-bar knee, and a contact-
defined tibiofemoral joint give different moment arms for identical muscle paths, so the
modeling choice must be matched to the question asked of it. The ISB joint coordinate
system recommendations were written against exactly this definitional freedom, to keep
reports of joint motion comparable across laboratories [Wu et al., 2002]. Throughout this
dissertation, Gait2392 serves as the reference against which the robot’s muscle routing,
moment arms, and torque capabilities are designed and judged.

PDF PAGE 46
23
2.4 Biomimetic Knee and Bipedal Robot Model
Steele’s biomimetic knee joint supplies the physical knee used in this dissertation [Steele
et al., 2017, Steele, 2018, Steele et al., 2018]. It is a one-degree-of-freedom four-bar linkage
whose crossed links reproduce the posterior migration of the human knee’s instantaneous
center of rotation (ICR) during flexion, so the rolling-and-sliding behavior of the human
joint emerges from the link geometry itself rather than from cams or gear profiles; the
design was calculated to reproduce an average sliding-to-rolling ratio of 0.079 over a 151˝
range of motion [Steele et al., 2018]. Because the ICR migrates, muscle moment arms about
the knee change with flexion angle in a human-like way, which is exactly the behavior
a torque prediction tool must capture. The biomimetic knee tests of Chapter 4 therefore
exercise the corrected torque model against this geometry, and Figure 2.5 summarizes the
mechanism.
Figure 2.5: Biomimetic knee mechanism.(a)Sagittal cutaway at full extension showing
the crossed four-bar linkage.(b)Exploded view of the mechanical stop. Reproduced from
Steele et al. [2018, Fig. 6] under CC BY 4.0.

PDF PAGE 47
24
In the author’s master’s work, muscle lengths, moment arms, and isometric joint
torques were computed over the range of motion for both the Gait2392 model and a PAM-
actuated robot leg model, demonstrating that several robot muscle torque profiles could
closely match the human model while many fell far short for part of the RoM [Bolen, 2019,
Bolen and Hunt, 2019]. Hand placement of muscle origins and insertions was a painstaking
and iterative task, and the placement optimization that replaced it is dissertation work,
discussed in Chapter 4. Figure 2.6 places the laboratory’s bipedal skeleton beside the
OpenSim muscle-routing reference developed for it.
The mechanics of that master’s study are worth recording because the dissertation’s
torque work reuses them. Moment arms were computed geometrically from attachment
points in the body frame, as the cross product of a joint-axis unit vector with the vector
from the joint center to the muscle’s line of action, the same construction described in
Section 2.3. PAM routings were then placed by hand in OpenSim beside the Gait2392
paths they imitate, moved to respect Festo BPA contraction limits and to keep actuators
from interfering with one another, and the result was saved as the gait2392 robotbody
model that the later chapters of this dissertation continue to refine [Bolen, 2019]. Isometric
torque followed as muscle force multiplied by moment arm, evaluated across each joint’s
range of motion. The biceps femoris long head shows what the comparison exposed: its
path differs visibly between the two models as the knee flexes, and the robot model’s
knee- and hip-axis torque surfaces reach only a fraction of the human model’s peaks over
the hip–knee range. Profiles of this kind, read across the full muscle set, defined the
matching problem that the placement optimization later formalized. Figure 2.7 shows
the routing models and the moment-arm construction, and Figure 2.8 shows the biceps

2.5 Neural Control of Locomotion: Central Pattern Generators
Locomotion in mammals is driven by spinal central pattern generators (CPGs), neural
circuits that can produce alternating rhythmic activity of flexor and extensor motoneurons
in the absence of rhythmic input and proprioceptive feedback [Brown, 1911, Rybak et al.,
2006]. The modern architectural understanding of mammalian locomotor CPGs comes
largely from the modeling work of the Rybak group and collaborators. Rybak et al. [2006]
proposed that the locomotor CPG consists of two levels: a half-center rhythm generator
(RG) and a pattern formation (PF) network, with reciprocal inhibitory interactions between
antagonist neural populations at each level. This two-level organization explains, among
other phenomena, spontaneous deletions of motoneuron activity during fictive locomotion
in which the locomotor period and phase are maintained, and it permits independent
control of the level of motoneuron activity and of step-cycle timing. McCrea and Rybak
[2008] reviewed the electrophysiological support for this organization, and Rybak et al.
[2015] related the model architecture to genetically identified spinal interneuron classes.
Left–right coordination is provided by commissural pathways between the two half-centers
[Molkov et al., 2015, Danner et al., 2016], and the rhythmogenic core of the CPG continues to
be refined experimentally [Rancic and Gosgnach, 2021]. How this architecture is expressed
as simulable circuit models is the subject of Section 2.7; the locomotor controller developed
in Chapter 6 is built directly on this two-level organization.

2.6 Sensory Feedback in Locomotion
Sensory feedback is not a passive correction layer but an integral part of locomotor pattern
generation. Reviews of afferent control of the CPG [Van de Crommert et al., 1998] and of
muscle proprioceptive feedback and spinal networks [Windhorst, 2007, 2022] summarize
the classical picture: group Ia afferents from muscle spindles signal muscle length and
velocity, group Ib afferents from Golgi tendon organs signal force, and group II afferents
signal length, with load-related feedback playing a role distinct from length-related feed-
back during locomotion [Dietz and Duysens, 2000]. Removing proprioceptive feedback
degrades locomotor patterns in mice [Akay et al., 2014]; proprioceptive input regulates and
adapts locomotor activity [Lam and Pearson, 2002]; and the relative contributions of pro-
prioceptive and vestibular systems to locomotion are the subject of ongoing study [Akay
and Murray, 2021, Akay, 2020]. Of particular relevance to the tests planned in Chapter 7,
tonic central and sensory stimuli have been shown to facilitate involuntary air-stepping
in humans [Selionov et al., 2009], demonstrating that simple graded stimulation of mo-
toneuronal and rhythmic circuits can elicit stepping-like output, an experiment readily
reproduced in simulation by tonic stimulation of pattern-formation and rhythm-generator
populations. The proprioceptive and contact feedback channels designed in Chapter 6
follow this afferent organization.
Three published circuits, reproduced in Figures 2.10, 2.11, and 2.12, place these afferent
channels in their circuit context. The rat hindlimb controller of Deng et al. [2019] is
organized exactly as Section 2.5 describes: a rhythm-generator half-center and a pattern-
formation layer sit above a sensory–motor network in which extensor and flexor muscle

PDF PAGE 54
31
receptors feed back through interneurons and Renshaw cells onto the same motoneurons
the CPG drives. The human-scale model of Di Russo et al. [2023] carries the idea to a full
lower limb: each agonist–antagonist muscle pair carries Ia, II, and Ib reflex populations
with Renshaw cells, and pattern-formation nodes from the locomotor controller terminate
on the same motoneuron pools. The cat interlimb model of Shinohara et al. [2025] adds
the left–right half of the problem, coupling the two rhythm generators through V3 and
commissural interneuron populations while force, length, and velocity feedback returns
from every muscle. The schematics differ in animal, scale, and emphasis, but they wire the
same afferent classes of this section into the circuit at the level each hypothesis concerns,
whether that is the rhythm generator, the pattern-formation layer, or the motor pool.

2.7 Computational Neuroscience of Locomotor Circuits
Computational neuroscience expresses hypotheses about locomotor circuitry as executable
models. A locomotor model is a system of coupled differential equations in which every
element of the schematic corresponds to an identified neural population or pathway;
the wiring diagram is not an illustration of the model, it is the model. The founding
hypothesis is Brown’s half-center: alternating flexor and extensor activity arises from two
reciprocally inhibitory central populations with opposing bias [Brown, 1911]. Modern
instantiations of the half-center endow each neuron with persistent sodium channels,
whose slow inactivation terminates each burst so that reciprocal inhibition switches the
populations without any phasic input [Deng et al., 2019]. Rhythmogenesis, in this tradition,
is a property of the intrinsic membrane dynamics, not of a clock elsewhere in the network.
The two-level organization described in Section 2.5 is the field’s current working
architecture, and its models account for specific electrophysiological observations: the
fictive-locomotion deletions reproduced by Rybak et al. [2006], the genetically identified
interneuron classes mapped onto the model by Rybak et al. [2015], and the organization
of circuits controlling two-joint muscles reviewed by Shevtsova et al. [2016]. Coupling
such circuits to musculoskeletal models shows how afferent feedback shapes CPG output
[Markin et al., 2010] and how biomechanics and sensory feedback regularize the behavior of
different CPG architectures [Deng et al., 2022]. Physiologically plausible models of human
locomotion use the same machinery to dissect the relative contributions of reflexes and
CPGs, with locomotor primitives, Ia, II, and Ib pathways, and Renshaw cells represented
explicitly [Di Russo et al., 2023], and recent models address interlimb coordination under

PDF PAGE 60
37
abrupt perturbations such as the sudden loss of ground support [Shinohara et al., 2025].
For a mechanical engineer, the productive way to read this literature is as a controls
tradition in which the block diagram is the controller itself: each block is an identifiable
neural population with conductance-based dynamics, and each connection is a synaptic
conductance with a physiologically interpretable weight. The connection schematics
published with these models are therefore worth reading as carefully as their equations.
The controller developed in Chapter 6 comes from this tradition: its two-layer organization
follows the rat hindlimb model of Deng et al. [2019], and its verification tests are drawn
from the deletion and stimulation phenomena that the models of Rybak et al. [2006] and
the air-stepping experiments of Selionov et al. [2009] explain.
2.8 Synthetic Nervous Systems
For robots, these biological architectures can be implemented as synthetic nervous systems
(SNSs): networks of conductance-based spiking and non-spiking neurons that are small
enough to run in real time yet biologically grounded enough to be compared against
animal data. The approach has been pursued most persistently by the insect-biorobotics
lineage of the Quinn laboratory at Case Western Reserve University and by laboratories
closely connected to it, the author’s own included. Works in this lineage span several
platforms: CPG-based neural control of a pneumatically actuated dog robot [Hunt et al.,
2017b], analytically designed dynamic neural networks for human balance control [Hilts
et al., 2019], the full-muscle rat hindlimb model and its moment-arm analysis [Young et al.,
2019], the two-layer CPG neuromechanical walker built on it [Deng et al., 2019], which

PDF PAGE 61
38
supplies this dissertation’s controller architecture, and the Muscle Mutt quadruped, a
canine-inspired BPA robot designed at the author’s laboratory, the Agile and Adaptive
Robotics Laboratory (AARL) at Portland State University, for synthetic nervous system
control [Scharzenberger, 2019]. Related controllers from the wider neuromechanical
community include sensory-entrained CPG control of bipedal walking [Li et al., 2017],
inter-leg coordination analyses in stick-insect-inspired controllers [Nourse et al., 2019],
reinforcement-reflex hybrids in insects [Goldsmith et al., 2021], and adaptive hindlimb
walking controllers [Schilling et al., 2022].
The SNS-Toolbox [Nourse et al., 2023] makes such networks practical: it is an open-
source Python package that simulates hundreds to thousands of spiking and non-spiking
neurons in real time or faster, supports multiple software and hardware backends including
GPUs and embedded platforms, and couples directly to physics simulators such as MuJoCo
for musculoskeletal control. The field it serves is active and growing: the perspective
review of Szczecinski et al. [2023] traces neuromorphic legged-locomotion control from its
insect-robot beginnings through current platforms to open problems, and physical testbeds
in this lineage now extend from insect-scale robots to a robotic cat hindlimb [Armstead
et al., 2025].
The boundary between background and contribution runs through this section as
well. The prior AnimatLab walker with which the author’s program first applied these
architectures to a biped [Bolen and Morrow, 2020] is presented as prior work of this
dissertation in Section 6.1, the Sensory Afferent Database that organizes the afferent data
such networks require [Bolen et al., 2023] is the subject of Chapter 5, and the simulation
pipeline that couples the controller to the robot model is built in Chapter 6. None of it is

PDF PAGE 62
39
repeated as background here.
2.9 Neuromechanical Simulation
Neuromechanical simulation, the coupling of a musculoskeletal model to a model of
the neural controller that drives it, has advanced along three partially overlapping lines.
The first line embeds reflex physiology directly into the controller. The muscle-reflex
walking model of Geyer and Herr [2010] demonstrated that positive force, length, and
velocity feedback around each muscle, organized with simple interlimb coordination, is
sufficient to produce self-stabilizing human-like walking dynamics and realistic muscle
activations without any central pattern generator. That model became the reference point
for a generation of reflex-based neuromechanical controllers, including the cat hindlimb
models of Section 2.7 [Markin et al., 2010, Shevtsova et al., 2016] and their extensions.
Hybrid work makes the complementarity of the two strategies explicit: Haeufle et al. [2018]
combined feed-forward muscle activations with neuronal reflex feedback in simulated
human walking and found that the robustness benefit of each depends on which muscle
carries it.
The second line replaces hand-designed circuitry with machine learning. Song et al.
[2021] trained deep reinforcement learning policies on a physiologically realistic mus-
culoskeletal model in OpenSim, reproducing human kinematics and muscle activations
across locomotion tasks, with the learned policy re-usable for experiments that would be
difficult in the laboratory. This approach inherits the fidelity of the musculoskeletal model
but encodes control in opaque function approximations: the trained policy can reproduce

PDF PAGE 63
40
behavior, but the individual “neurons” of the controller do not correspond to identifiable
physiological circuits, so the class of scientific questions it can answer, such as lesions,
deletions, and pathway stimulation, is narrower than its behavioral repertoire. Recent
perspectives on predictive musculoskeletal simulation [De Groote and Falisse, 2021] place
both learned and optimal-control approaches in the same frame: the models are mature,
and the open problems are the cost of solving them and the biological interpretability of
the control they find.
The third line, in which this dissertation sits, is biologically grounded synthetic ner-
vous systems: controllers whose neurons and synapses are explicit, conductance-based,
and mapped to identified physiological circuits, simulated at speeds sufficient for real-
time robotics [Nourse et al., 2023]. Hierarchical CPG control is meanwhile spreading
to bipedal-capable musculoskeletal models, including motor-module bipedal walkers
[Ichimura and Yamazaki, 2019], two-layer rhythm-generator and pattern-formation control
of a musculoskeletal macaque model [Nishizaki et al., 2026], and two-level CPG control
of muscle-driven robotic hind legs [Fukuoka et al., 2021], though always in simulation
with generic musculature or on non-humanoid platforms; a two-layer SNS driving a
custom-anatomy, artificial-muscle humanoid leg remains unclaimed. What distinguishes
the present program from all three lines is the combination it attempts: an anatomically
grounded controller (two-layer CPG with Ia, Ib, II, and contact feedback), a biomechani-
cally validated pneumatic model (Chapter 4), and an eventual physical robot.

PDF PAGE 64
41
2.9.1 Simulation Tooling
All three lines depend on a software substrate that is itself part of the method: muscu-
loskeletal geometry too elaborate to integrate by hand, joined to neural models too large
to wire by hand. The tools used in this dissertation, and the ones adjacent to it in the same
ecosystem, are the following.
OpenSim [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018], distributed through the SimTK (Simulation
Toolkit) repository, provides the biomechanical modeling side: musculoskeletal models
with detailed bone geometry, muscle routing, and force-generating properties, along with
tools for analyzing moment arms and isometric torque. These models are approximations
with known limits rather than exact anatomies: muscle paths are interpolations over
discrete attachment and wrapping data, and near the ends of a joint’s range the routed
paths of sparsely wrapped muscles can bunch up or pass through bone, an interference
documented within Gait2392-derived routings themselves [Bolen, 2019]. Their geometry
is accurate for a purpose, namely moment arms and torque predictions over the functional
range of motion, and that is the standard against which the robot model of this program
is compared. The same SimTK infrastructure hosts SCONE, an open-source package for
predictive simulation and optimization of neuromuscular control of biological motion
[Geijtenbeek, 2019]. AnimatLab [Cofer et al., 2010] provides a graphical environment in
which physical bodies and synthetic neural systems are constructed and co-simulated; the
AARL’s prior walker (Section 6.1) was built in it. MuJoCo [Todorov et al., 2012] provides
the physics-engine side: fast, accurate simulation of rigid-body dynamics with contacts,
along with muscle-like actuators that include activation dynamics. MyoConverter bridges

PDF PAGE 65
42
the biomechanical and physics-engine worlds by converting OpenSim musculoskeletal
models to MuJoCo format with optimized muscle kinematics and kinetics [Caggiano et al.,
2022, Wang et al., 2022]. Combined with the SNS-Toolbox’s MuJoCo backend [Nourse
et al., 2023], I used these tools to convert the laboratory’s modified OpenSim leg model to
MuJoCo and couple it to a synthetic nervous system, a pipeline assembled and evaluated
in Chapter 6. Simulation speed is the practical enabler: a single overnight run can sweep
thousands of parameter combinations or execute hundreds of simulated gait cycles per
condition, throughput that no human-subject or animal protocol could match, and that
transforms lesion and stimulation studies from bespoke demonstrations into statistically
powered experiments.
2.10 Testing Neuromechanical Hypotheses on Robots: The Ethical Case
The experiments this research program is designed to enable, including deleting a muscle
from a limb, blocking a sensory pathway, lesioning part of a rhythm generator, and apply-
ing tonic stimulation to spinal circuits, cannot be performed on healthy humans. Invasive
intervention on the human neuromuscular system is limited to clinical populations, is
tightly constrained by ethics review, and permits neither the within-subject control nor
the reversibility that neuromechanical hypotheses demand. The same experiments in
animals also carry real moral cost, and their justification is governed by the Three Rs
framework: replacement of animal subjects with non-animal methods, reduction of the
numbers required, and refinement of the remaining procedures [Russell and Burch, 1959,
Tannenbaum and Bennett, 2015]. A robot that implements a validated neuromechanical

PDF PAGE 66
43
hypothesis is precisely such a replacement method: every deletion, lesion, and stimulation
experiment that runs on the synthetic nervous system is an experiment that does not
require a living subject, and the robot population can be duplicated exactly, so that “sub-
jects” are statistically identical across conditions, a form of reduction no breeding colony
can offer. Refinement follows as well: the failure modes of these experiments damage
hardware, not tissue.
Figure 2.15 shows what that accounting leaves out. The image is a frame from footage of
a real decerebrate-cat experiment, the class of preparation on which the field’s understand-
ing of spinal locomotion has been built since its beginnings [Brown, 1911, WhiteCoatWaste,
n.d.]: a living cat, its forebrain disconnected by a brainstem transection, walks on the
treadmill because the neurons distal to the laceration are still living, still connected, and
still firing action potentials. The published record of this work is drawn almost exclusively
in schematic, labeled boxes for half-centers and color-coded reflex arcs that are honest
about the circuitry and silent about the animal. The figure is included as the counterweight,
documenting what the experiments looked like, and therefore what a replacement method
stands in for.
The procedures behind that record are documented in films that remain publicly
available. A 50 min classroom recording posted by the UTHealth Houston School of
Dentistry first shows an intact cat playing with its handler, righting itself when lowered and
placing its paws accurately on the edge of a table, and then documents the decerebration
performed for the class [UTHealth Houston School of Dentistry, 2025]: the animal is
anesthetized with open-drop ether, more than the horse in the same demonstration series
received, the carotid arteries are ligated and a tracheal cannula installed, and the skull

PDF PAGE 67
44
Figure 2.15: Frame from archival footage of a decerebrate-cat treadmill experiment [White-
CoatWaste, n.d.], restored and machine colorized; color and fine detail are inferred by the
restoration rather than recovered from the source. The cat walks on the treadmill belt while
held in a stereotactic head holder whose fixation pin passes through or beside the eye
orbit, with skull clamps and slung support completing the frame. Shown as documentary
context for the replacement argument of this section, not as gait evidence.

PDF PAGE 68
45
is opened with a trephine before the brainstem is transected between the superior and
inferior colliculi. The treadmill preparations that established locomotor neurophysiology
are likewise preserved in the historical literature: Stuart [2007] reproduce the arrangement
of the high-decerebrate cat treadmill experiments, with electromyographic recording from
the hindlimb muscles, from the original Russian-language reports [Severin et al., 1967].
The honest accounting is murkier than the Three Rs framing alone suggests, because
the models a robot implements are themselves products of animal experimentation. The
half-center organization, the two-level rhythm and pattern architecture, and the length,
force, and load signaling of the afferents were each established in preparations like the
ones described above [Brown, 1911, Severin et al., 1967, Rybak et al., 2006], and the
databases of afferent properties that parameterize sensory feedback are compiled from
animal recordings [Bolen et al., 2023]. A synthetic nervous system therefore replaces
future experiments; it does not replace, and cannot repay, the animal work on which its
architecture is built. Recognizing this inheritance sharpens rather than undermines the
case for robotic subjects: the knowledge purchased at that cost is precisely what makes a
robotic replacement scientifically meaningful, and the robot is the instrument that keeps
that knowledge productive without adding to the ledger.
For a robot to serve as a replacement subject, however, it must first earn standing as
a scientific model. Webb’s influential analyses of bio-robotic methodology [Webb, 2001,
2002] argue that a robot is a good model of biological behavior when the mechanism it
implements is tested at the same level of organization as the target phenomenon, matching
dynamics, control structure, and sensory ecology rather than surface appearance. The vali-
dation chain of this dissertation is constructed to that standard: the actuator force model

PDF PAGE 69
46
is validated against bench data (Section 3.2.1), the joint torque model against isometric
measurements and human torque envelopes (Section 4.2), and the neuromechanical con-
troller is verified against published tonic stimulation and deletion phenomena [Selionov
et al., 2009, Rybak et al., 2006], the verification step that precedes locomotion tuning in this
program. Only then can a robot experiment be read as evidence about the hypothesized
biological circuit rather than about the robot.
The argument is ultimately bidirectional, completing the loop described in Section 1.2.
Experiments that are unethical, impractical, or statistically unreachable in humans and
animals become routine on the robot; the resulting insights into neuromechanical control
feed back into biology, into the design of the next robot iteration, and directly into prosthetic
and orthotic devices that face the same problem of controlling muscle-like actuators for
human users, whose central pattern generator controllers are an active topic in lower-limb
exoskeleton research [Duan et al., 2024, Pery et al., 2026]. The ethical case and the scientific
case are the same case: the robot is the instrument that makes these experiments possible.

PDF PAGE 70
47
3 Isometric Force Characterization of Braided Pneumatic Actuators
Publication note: Sections 3.1.1–3.1.2, Section 3.2.1, and Sections 3.3.1–3.3.5, with their
associated figures and tables, appeared in Bolen et al. [2026] (Bolen, Elzein, Pang, and
Hunt,Actuators, 2026). For that work, B.B. performed the conceptualization, methodology,
software, validation, formal analysis, investigation, data curation, visualization, and
original draft preparation; M.E. and L.P . contributed methodology, software, investigation,
and data curation; and A.H. supervised the work, administered the project, and acquired
funding. All co-authors contributed to writing, review, and editing.
This chapter develops and validates the actuator force models on which the rest of
the dissertation builds, and it reads as one self-contained study, from methods through
results to discussion. Sections 3.1.1 and 3.1.2 present the methods: the force equations used
throughout this work, and the test jig and measurement procedure used to characterize
Festo BPAs with 10 mm and 20 mm uninflated diameters over a wide range of resting
lengths, contractions, and pressures. Section 3.2.1 reports the resulting maximum-force
and normalized force–strain–pressure models and compares them with the manufacturer’s
tool. The remaining sections discuss the effects of resting length, the nondimensionalized
force prediction, the comparison with other published BPA models, the implications
for real-time control and simulation, and the analogy between these artificial muscles
and biological muscle. The validated force model is the one used for the knee-torque
predictions of Chapter 4 and for the BPA actuation of the neuromechanical simulations in

PDF PAGE 71
48
Chapter 6.

3.1.2 BPA Isometric Force Characterization Experiment
To measure artificial muscle force as a function of length and pressure, we built a test
jig from extruded aluminum and 3D printed parts. Festo BPAs with 10 mm and 20 mm
diameter were tested with various resting lengths and different amounts of contraction.

PDF PAGE 73
50
These data are used to develop an improved model that more accurately predicts maximum
muscle force based on l0, current muscle length lm, and pressure P. The results are
compared with existing models and the new data are used to create an updated model.
A test jig was made of 80/20 brand 1515 series extruded aluminum (Figure 3.1). Artifi-
cial muscles were placed vertically in the jig one at a time. The upper end was attached to
a load cell. Force data was collected using a CALT DYLY-103 100 kg S-shaped load cell for
theI10 mm BPAs and a Loadstar RAS1-2HKS-S for theI20 mm BPAs. The load cell was
used in conjunction with a HX711 Load Cell Amplifier. Pressure data was obtained from a
Freescale MPX5700 GP 5 V pressure sensor. Building air supply pressure was controlled
with two pressure regulators in series. The first was a Parker model 20R113GC 0 ´ 120 psi
pressure regulator. The second was a Husky 3 /8 in. High Performance Air Regulator
HDA72200. A Festo VTUG-10-MRCR-S1T-26V20-T516LA-UL-532S-8K valve manifold
VTUG-G was used to deliver air from the pressure regulator to the actuator. This manifold
is comprised of eight two-in-one bidirectional normally closed Festo VUVG-S10-T32C-
AZT-M5-1T1L valves.
Pressure and load cell amplifier data were sent to MATLAB 2021b via an Arduino
Uno style Sparkfun BlackBoard C microcontroller. The computers that used MATLAB
were running either Windows 10 or Windows 11. During phases when the Arduino was
collecting force and pressure data to send to MATLAB, the Arduino would also trigger
(via MATLAB) an Onsemi 2N4401 NPN transistor to make the valve manifold open or
close the valve. For other data collection, the valve was manually opened and closed.
The inner distance between the hose clamps on each BPA was measured with a FANUC
tape measure to determine the muscle’s length l and resting length ( l0). This is how

PDF PAGE 74
51
Festo AG & Co. KG [2026] defines l0, although in Hunt et al. [2017a] it was measured
to also include end cap length. We then inflated each BPA to P620 = 620 kPa, with one
end allowed to move freely in the axial Degree of Freedom (DoF), and measured the
length l620 to calculate maximum contraction at 620 kPa using Equation (3.2). The distance
between the crossmembers was then controlled to obtain different amounts of contraction,
the muscles were inflated to various pressures, and the contractile force was recorded
at the pressure–contraction pairs. This was done for I10 mm BPA resting lengths (l0) of
112 mm–518 mm. ForI20 mm BPAs, resting lengths (l0) of 300 mm–509 mm were used.
3.2 Results
3.2.1 BPA Force Characterization
This section reports the results of the isometric force characterization experiments described
in Section 3.1.2. First, maximum isometric force at 620 kPa is modeled as a function of
resting length. The remaining data are then normalized to build a predictive force surface,
and the resulting model is compared with the manufacturer’s tool and with other published
BPA models.
Maximum Force at 620 kPa

diameters at 620 kPa show a dependency on resting length (Figure 3.2). This is a previ-

Force as a Function of Pressure and Resting Length
Data from BPA characterization tests of the 10 mm muscles resulted in force–pressure
pairings for different muscle resting lengths (l0) (Figure 3.3a). Similar to the maximum
force data, these data show a force response resembling an arctan curve along the l0
dimension with a more linear response to changes in pressure. Using the Nonlinear Least
Squares method and a Least Absolute Residual robustness, we fit an arctan curve to the
data to get the maximum force given a resting length and pressure as described by the
model form given in Section 3.1.1, wherea1 = 0.4895 NkPa´1 and a2 = 0.030 68kPa´1 m´1

actuator. Goodness-of-fit measures are given in Table 3.1; throughout this dissertation,
goodness of fit is reported as the root-mean-square error (RMSE), the adjusted fraction
of variance unexplained (FVU, for which R2 = 1 ´FVU ), and the maximum absolute
residual, as defined alongside the reference implementation in Appendix A.

should be recorded in each muscle used on a robot to best predict the force it may produce
at different pressures and contraction.

Force as a Function of Pressure and Contraction
In addition to being a function of the pressure and resting length, the force produced
by the actuator is also a function of the amount of actuator contraction, with less force
being applied as the actuator contracts. To build this model, the collected data of force,
pressure, and contraction are normalized by dividing by F620, P620, and ϵ620, respectively.
This had the effect of compressing the data into a 3D surface ranging from 0 to 1 on all
axes. Normalized data are compared with pressure isoclines of force predicted by the
Festo tool divided by the maximum force equation described in the previous section in
Figure 3.4. With this comparison, it is clear that the Festo tool over-predicts the expected
force, especially at lower pressures, contraction, and resting lengths.

We therefore derived our own equation for isometric force in the BPA as a function of

absolute residual of 10.6%. Solving (3.4) for the I20 mm BPA, yields different coefficients,
and results are seen in Figure 3.4b. Coefficient values and goodness-of-fit statistics are
found in Table 3.2.
3.3 Discussion
3.3.1 Effects of Resting Length
We have developed new F620 equations for 10 mm and 20 mm diameter Festo BPAs that
capture the change in maximum force produced by the actuators at 620kPa as a function
of their resting length. When examining Equation (3.7) and measured data in Figure 3.2b,
it can be seen that as l0 goes to infinity, F62010 goes to 470 N. When the Festo tool [Lang,
Robert, 2005] was queried at l0 = 1 m as an approximation of infinity, it predicts the
I10 mm BPA can produce F= 490 N at P620. This is within the 10% variability that Festo
states may occur in manufacturing tolerances. Similarly, the Festo data predict a I20 mm
BPA will produce F= 1570 N. Our results for Equation (3.8) show that F62020 goes to
1460 N as l0 approaches infinity. Although the difference is much larger, it is also within
the 10% manufacturing tolerance.

PDF PAGE 84
61
Our measured data differ significantly from the Festo model as l0 goes to zero. For the
measured data, as the length gets smaller, the force does as well, whereas the Festo tool
predicts exponential force increase (see Figure 3.2). As a specific example, when specifying
l0 = 112 mm for the 10 mm diameter BPAs, the Festo predicted F620 is 498.6 N. However,
using Equation (3.7),F 620 is calculated to be 335.3 N. Actual force in thel 0 =112 mm was
measured at 350.9 N. Therefore, the error in predicted F620 for the I10 mm BPA is 4.5% for
our model and 42.1% using the Festo tool. Researchers using BPA resting lengths under
300 mm should take note of these results.
Festo AG & Co. KG [2026] (p. 12) states that force will be reduced by approximately
10% for a minimum nominal length. In Festo AG & Co. KG [2018] (p. 14) they say the force
can deviate by 10% based on factors such as production variation, material variation, and
deviation from nominal length (l0). Festo AG & Co. KG [2013] (p. 13) notes that testing
was done with BPAs that are ten times the uninflated diameter, and that theoretical force
can increase by up to 10%. In each datasheet, the characteristic curves appear to be the
same. As demonstrated above, the 100 mmI 10 mm BPA should have much less force
than what is shown in the curve. This is our experience and the reason for the testing. It is
unclear why the MuscleSIM tool [Lang, Robert, 2005] then predicts higher force as resting
length decreases. It could be a reversed sign coefficient in their internal curve-fit model.
We have shown here that as the nominal length goes to zero, so does force.
BPA models commonly predict higher forces than are measured experimentally because
they do not account for end effects in regions of the pressure vessel where those effects
dominate. Nominally, the clamped ends fix the inner and outer diameter while allowing
axial and radial displacement. In the isometric case, there is only radial displacement.

PDF PAGE 85
62
The boundary conditions force steep gradients in stress and strain near the ends. Near
the boundary conditions, axial, radial, and circumferential strains deviate from analytic
solutions for a cylindrical model. For a constant pressure, the end effect region appears to
have the same length. Therefore, in shorter BPAs, the end effects dominate. The axial length
scale of the boundary section is comparable to l0, and the active force differs substantially
from that of an assumed perfect cylinder.
Manufacturing variations seem to play an important role in the behavior of these
actuators. It remains to be seen how ϵ620 can be known a priori. We still suspect that it is a
function of l0, but it might also be a function of other factors such as product batch number
or UV light exposure [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2026] (p. 15); [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2018] (p. 6);
[Festo AG & Co. KG, 2013] (p. 13). We found there to be larger variations in the 10 mm
BPAs than in the 20mm BPAs. Modeling work on ideal McKibben actuators describes how
the relationship between contraction and initial length is a function of the total number
of twists and the angle of twist in the usable muscle area [Chou and Hannaford, 1996]. It
is unclear if there are variations in the angle and number of twists in the Festo BPAs due
to manufacturing. Uncovering the relationship between ϵ620 and l0 for the Festo DMSP
artificial muscles, if it exists in a meaningful way, will require additional controlled tests.
Similarly, it is not clear how the maximum force might change with manufacturing
variations. This was reasonably corrected for by normalizing the equations to each in-
dividual’s maximum contraction, but more nuanced details might emerge with more
data collection. Not knowing these relationships a priori means there is still a need for
robots and other systems to go through an iterative design stage if it is discovered that
the system does not produce the torque or have the RoM that the design team expects.

PDF PAGE 86
63
However, the data and models provided here should enable faster redesigns with fewer
additional measurements.
3.3.2 Nondimensionalized Force Prediction
We have also introduced the concept of nondimensionalized isometric force that is a
function of relative strain and relative pressure (Equation (3.4)). This elegant equation
has only three coefficients with low error (see Table 3.2). In the work presented here, we
introduce the relative pressure term, P˚ =P/P 620. Our data show that by normalizing
force, contraction, and pressure, we are able to create a simplified force equation as
a function of contraction and pressure that scales well with initial actuator length, as
expressed by Equation (3.6) in Section 3.1.1.
For example, givenl0 = 54 mm and P= 300 kPa, the measured force wasFactual =103 N.
Force prediction using only the Festo tool predicts the force to be Fpredict =270 N. This is
161% greater than the measured force. Using Equations (3.7) and (3.4) with Equation (3.6)
gives Fpredict =F ˚(0, 0.48)¨F 620(0.054) = 0.49 ¨ 230 N = 112 N. This is an error of 8%,
which is much more accurate than using the Festo tool only.
The presented work is capable of predicting force output for 10 mm and 20 mm
muscles with different initial muscle lengths, internal pressure, and contraction amount
with minimal initial data collection. However, it remains to be seen if these relationships
are maintained over the full lifecycle of the muscles. There are not yet any peer reviewed
studies investigating the long-term behavior of these artificial muscles, though the Festo
datasheet provides some indication of what can be expected [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2026]

PDF PAGE 87
64
(p.19). They report that the 40 mm BPA can complete 1 million cycles at 6000 N (maximum
rated load), or 10 million cycles at 4000 N. The service life of the other size BPAs is not
reported; however, it is mentioned that thermal stressing may reduce the lifespan and they
should be inspected every 500,000 cycles for signs of aging [Festo AG & Co. KG, 2018]
(pp. 10–11). Future research should look at the behavior of these muscles over time.
In our work, P620 = 620 kPa is the maximum supply pressure for our system, and other
users of this actuator may use a different supply pressure. However, this supply pressure
is not required, and normalizing by this value was chosen as a method for improving the
optimization techniques by reducing the sensitivity of the term associated with pressure.
This equation will work for any pressure supplies below 620kPa. We also anticipate the
equation will work for pressures up to 800 kPa (the maximum rated pressure for Festo
I10 mm BPAs); however, we do not have the data to confirm this.
3.3.3 Comparison with Other Published BPA Models
The proposed model was compared with the formulations by S ´arosi [2012], S ´arosi and
Fabulya [2012], and Martens and Boblan [2017], to evaluate their predictive performance
across actuator sizes and operating conditions (see Table 3.3 and Figure 3.5). For the
I20 mm BPA, parameters from [S´arosi, 2012] were used, corresponding to actuators with
resting lengths of 200 mm and 400 mm. These were compared against the experimental
data at l0 = 300 mm and l0 = 450 mm, respectively. For the I10 mm BPA with resting
lengths of 257 mm and 233 mm, parameters from [S´arosi and Fabulya, 2012] were applied.
In each case, the maximum contraction parameter ( kmax) was determined numerically

PDF PAGE 88
65
by solving F(6.2 bar, kmax ) = 0, ensuring consistency with the experimental maximum
pressure condition.

While the S´arosi [2012] and S´arosi and Fabulya [2012] models capture general trends
in force–contraction behavior, discrepancies were observed when applying parameters
derived from different actuator lengths. In particular, the model tends to over-predict force
at higher pressures and low amounts of contraction, suggesting sensitivity to geometric
scaling effects not explicitly accounted for in the formulation.
The Martens and Boblan [2017] model is derived from a geometric and material-based
framework and provides a more physically grounded representation of actuator behavior.
However, its accuracy depends strongly on precise geometric parameters, including braid
angle, wall thickness, and fiber length. Small deviations in these parameters can lead to
significant differences in predicted force, particularly at higher pressures.

The Martens and Boblan model was first evaluated at the same pressure–contraction
