PDF PAGE 90
67
indicating that it reproduces the general published force map shape but is sensitive to the
assumed initial geometry. Examining their I20 mm BPA, for example, using the explicitly
stated l0 = 300 mm and initial diameter D0 = 20 mm significantly under-predicted the
results compared to using the implied l0 = 296 mm ([Martens and Boblan, 2017] (Table
A1)) and D0 +H 0 = 21.8 mm, where H0 = 1.8 mm is the material thickness. The diameter
adjustment can be inferred from [Chou and Hannaford, 1996]. This adjusted diameter was
also included for the I10 mm BPA to give a better fit. As can be seen in Figure 3.5 and
Table 3.3, the highest accuracy is observed when I20 mm and l0 = 300 mm, which is at or
very near their experimental measurements. For the I10 mm BPAs the accuracy decreases
even though their experimentally measured l0 = 250 mm is close to our l0 = 257 mm and
l0 = 233 mm. The largest error occurs at I20 mm and l0 = 450 mm and the model starts
to predict negative force. We have calculated that the FPE ¨ dD
DL term becomes very large
compared to the others [Martens and Boblan, 2017] (p. 5). This suggests that the model is
sensitive to geometric parameter selection and interpretation, particularly with respect to
initial length and diameter definitions.
In comparison, the present normalized model demonstrates improved consistency
across actuator lengths by incorporating experimentally derived scaling through F620 and
ϵ620. This normalization reduces sensitivity to geometric variation and enables a unified
representation of actuator behavior, while maintaining predictive accuracy over the tested
range of pressures and contractions.

PDF PAGE 91
68
3.3.4 Implications for Real-Time Control and Simulation
Although pneumatic muscle actuators exhibit hysteresis, thermodynamic effects, and
other dynamic behavior, accurate and high-performance controller designs can still be
achieved using a static force map approach when the underlying static force model is
accurate. Martens and Boblan explicitly note that BPA dynamics are often dominated
by the air dynamics inside the actuator and the mass flow behavior of the valve, such
that precise modeling of the static force characteristic remains crucial for torque control
accuracy [Martens and Boblan, 2017]. This view is consistent with more recent BPA control
work, including a sensor-less torque control interface for BPA-driven joints [Martens
et al., 2018], stable force/position/stiffness control of antagonistic pneumatic-muscle
joints [Ugurlu et al., 2019, Hunt et al., 2017b], and sensor-less angle stiffness control of
antagonistic BPA systems [Shin and Kogiso, 2022].
In this context, the present model is well suited for real-time use because it reduces the
static isometric force prediction to a low-order normalized surface, F˚(ϵ˚, P˚), scaled by
the resting-length-dependent term F620(l0). This structure is computationally inexpensive
to evaluate online and is also suitable for lookup-table implementation. While the present
work does not model the full actuator–valve dynamics, it provides the static force rela-
tionship needed as a feedforward or inner-model component in real-time torque control,
simulation, and preliminary design workflows. It is anticipated that improved real-time
control for high-speed and high-precision systems will benefit more from improved mod-
eling of airflow dynamics than from modeling actuator hysteresis, damping, and other
dynamic behavior. Characterizing the actuators under dynamic operating conditions,

PDF PAGE 92
69
such as isokinetic (constant-velocity), isobaric, and isotonic (constant-load) contractions,
is a natural next step beyond the isometric characterization reported here, for which the
pressure–force dynamics model of Elzein et al. [2025] provides the modeling foundation.
3.3.5 Biomimetic Artificial Muscle Analogy
BPA artificial muscles are often said to be analogous to biological muscles because they
have force–length curves and can produce force only in tension. This analogy is worth
closer inspection. For example, the passive force–length curve has been described as an
exponential function arising from connective tissue elements, while the active force–length
relationship is typically modeled as a bell-shaped curve reflecting actin–myosin over-
lap [Winters, 1990]. These formulations emphasize that muscle force generation is gov-
erned by nonlinear geometric and material interactions rather than simple linear elasticity.
McKibben-type pneumatic artificial muscles (of which Festo is an example) exhibit anal-
ogous nonlinear behavior, though arising from fundamentally different physical mech-
anisms. As shown by Chou and Hannaford, the force–length relationship of McKibben
actuators is derived from geometric constraints imposed by the braided shell, leading
to a nonlinear dependence of force on actuator length through the braid angle [Chou
and Hannaford, 1996]. In their formulation, actuator force is proportional to pressure
and a nonlinear geometric term (3 cos2 θ´ 1), which governs both the maximum contrac-
tion limit and the curvature of the force–length relationship. The work presented here
demonstrates that this nonlinear curve can be well approximated by an exponential as
well.

PDF PAGE 93
70
However, the behavior of these artificial muscles differs distinctly from that of biological
muscles. For example, the optimal fiber length of a biological muscle,lOFL , is not equivalent
to l0 in an artificial muscle. Artificial muscles produce their maximum active force at their
resting length, which is also their maximum length. In contrast, the maximum active force
that a biological muscle can produce is somewhere in the middle of the total contractile
length of the muscle.
Furthermore, biological muscles can adapt the optimal operating length through struc-
tural remodeling (e.g., sarcomere addition or tendon compliance) while BPAs have a fixed
geometric configuration. This allows biological systems to self-optimize for the particular
tasks the animal is subjected to. In order to re-optimize a robot for new tasks, new actuators
would have to be swapped into the system. This distinction highlights a key limitation
of BPAs in biomimetic applications, while also reinforcing the importance of accurate
nonlinear modeling for control and design.
The analogy also suggests two design observations. First, the force–length penalty is
shared by both families: force falls as the actuator operates away from its optimum, so a
limb design must route each actuator such that its highest-force operating range coincides
with the joint angles where torque is needed most, because the force lost at the extremes
of the range of motion cannot be recovered by pressure alone. Second, the two families
gain moment arm in different ways. Skeletal muscle attaches to the skeleton itself, through
tendons or directly over broad areas of bone, so the moment arms of large animals follow
from skeletal geometry. The robot’s BPAs instead act through brackets that protrude from
the limb segments; that hardware is mechanically exposed, and its series compliance is
one of the loss mechanisms quantified in Chapter 4.

PDF PAGE 94
71
The analogy finally raises a question that this dissertation can pose but not settle. Every
muscle-like actuator loses force as it operates away from its optimal length, and a soft
actuator working against soft structure has no rigid skeleton from which to gain a moment
arm. Given those two facts, it may be no accident that nearly all large land animals are
built on rigid skeletons and that large soft-bodied land animals are essentially absent.
Whether the force–length penalty contributes to that absence, or whether it reflects other
constraints such as support and protection, is a question for comparative biomechanics
rather than for a single actuator study. For the designer, the lesson holds regardless of the
answer: some moment arm must come from somewhere, and on this robot it comes from
protruding brackets whose behavior must be modeled rather than ignored.

PDF PAGE 95
72
4 Isometric Knee Torque
Publication note: This chapter is the subject of an unpublished manuscript in preparation
by Bolen, Burgess, Morrow, Elzein, Pang, Scharzenberger, and Hunt [Bolen et al., 2025].
For that work, B.B. performed the conceptualization, methodology, software, validation,
formal analysis, investigation, data curation, visualization, and original draft preparation;
M.E., L.P ., L.B., and C.M. contributed methodology, software, investigation, and data
curation; and A.H. supervised the work, administered the project, and acquired funding.
All co-authors contributed to writing, review, and editing.
This chapter connects the actuator force characterization of Chapter 3 to torque about
the robot knee. The rigid-body torque model of the earlier robot work assumed inex-
tensible muscle paths on rigid links, and the isometric tests reported here show where
that assumption fails and by how much. The chapter proceeds from the simple pinned
knee to the biomimetic joint. It first presents the mesh-optimization method that places
the actuator attachment routes, a method that is compliance-unaware in its origin, and
then the identification of the correction terms χ0 through χ3 that removes the rigid-body
idealization from the torque prediction. The identified terms are validated on held-out
tests, the route optimizers are updated to carry them, and the chapter closes with the
redesigned routes and their physical torque tests. The methods, results, and discussion
follow the manuscript noted above and include the physical-validation sweeps of the
redesigned routes.

PDF PAGE 96
73
4.1 Methods
4.1.1 Robot Leg Model and Knee Test Stands
The primary components of the robot leg are the knee joint, femur, tibia, BPA assemblies,
and artificial tendons (Fig. 4.1A–C). The artificial bone components are 3D printed using
a combination of Onyx, carbon fiber, and high-strength high-temperature fiberglass on
Markforged Onyx One and Mark Two printers, as well as machined links for the knee
mechanism. Onyx is a proprietary Markforged material that consists of chopped carbon
fiber in nylon. Artificial tendons are made with Shimano bicycle brake cable.
The leg assembly geometry and dimensions are based on bones scans of a 6 ft (1.83 m)
tall person [Bolen and Hunt, 2019, Seth et al., 2018, Delp et al., 1990, 2007]. Two knee
joints were tested, one pure rotational joint, and one biomimetic joint with rotation and
translation. Each joint is driven by two antagonistic Festo actuators. Both robot joints have
the same center of rotation in the femur frame atθ k =0 deg.
Details on the biomimetic robot knee used in this study can be found in [Steele, 2018,
Steele et al., 2017, 2018]. In brief, this 1-DoF joint (Fig. 4.2) uses a four-bar linkage to
change the effective moment arm during rotation. The intersection point of the internal
crossed links define the instantaneous center of rotation (ICR). This causes the joint ICR
location in the femur frame to translate posteriorly in the X direction and distally in the
Y direction except for a brief proximal move near full flexion. Reference International
Society of Biomechanics (ISB) coordinate frames are given by Wu and Cavanagh [1995],
Wu et al. [2002]. This is similar to how the human knee behaves [Bolen and Hunt, 2019,
Morrow et al., 2020, Seth et al., 2018, Delp et al., 2007, 1990]. The correct location of the

ICR is important for both theoretical and experimental torque calculations.
In the pinned knee joint, uniarticular knee extensor and knee flexor are used to actuate
the joint. Each knee joint type was tested with a single I10 mm Festo BPA per muscle.
The biomimetic knee was additionally tested with I20 mm Festo BPAs in a paired con-
figuration: the extensor with two BPAs in parallel, and the flexor with two BPAs whose
pathways are mirrored about the Z axis. Each artificial muscle is pinned at the muscle
origin location on the femur while the other end is either pinned or attached to the muscle
insertion location on the tibia via an artificial tendon.
Torque about the knee is generated by the moment of the BPA force about the joint’s
instantaneous center of rotation (ICR). Two vectors enter that moment: the position vector
d, drawn from the ICR to the point where the actuator force is applied, and the force vector
F=∥F∥ ˆu, whose direction ˆu is the line of action of the actuator. The moment of the force
about the joint is m=dˆF , and its magnitude is the force magnitude multiplied by the
moment armr, the perpendicular distance from the joint axis to the line of action,
r=∥dˆ ˆu∥(4.1)
The moment arm is therefore a scalar, and it is the same quantity whether it is computed
from the position vector with Eq. (4.1) or measured directly with a tape measure. In this
study it is calculated using the method specified by Young et al. [2019]. The scalar moment
armr k about the joint axis ˆzk (i.e., theZaxis in the knee frame) is calculated as

A muscle path may be modeled as a single segment running from origin to insertion,
or as a chain of segments joined at via points. The segment that crosses from one reference
frame into the other, here from the femur frame to the tibia frame, is the free muscle
segment: because its endpoints ride on different bodies, its length and its direction respond
directly to joint rotation, whereas a segment contained within one body’s frame rotates
rigidly with that body and changes neither. Here p f is the force-path segment vector, and
pproj,xy locates a point on that line relative to the joint, projected onto the plane normal to
the joint axis. The normalized cross product in Equation (4.2) is the in-plane normal to
the force path. Its dot product with the projected position gives the signed moment arm
(Figure 2.7). Moment arm is calculated in the tibial body frame.
Details of the existing model and the torque calculation methodology in the modeled
robot are given by Bolen and Hunt [2019], Morrow et al. [2020].
4.1.2 Knee Torque Data Collection and Equipment
A test jig was built to collect knee torque measurements at different knee angles over its
range of motion. The test stand frame is made predominantly out of 80/20 1515 series
extruded aluminum components. The knee joint is free to rotate the tibia while the femur
is fixed to the frame. A force sensor has one end connected to the tibia and the other is
connected to a swing arm. The swing arm is tied to a winch with 3/16 inch Kevlar rope
from Quality Nylon Rope routed over intermediate idler pulleys (see Fig. 4.1Band 4.1C).
At each measured position of θk the winch would be locked and the BPA would be inflated
and then deflated. This test procedure kept the measured results as isometric as possible.

PDF PAGE 101
78
The knee joint is co-axial with gravity to eliminate gravitational torque (see Fig. 4.1Aand
Fig. 4.1C).
Force data was collected using one of two different sensors. The first is a MODERN
STEP 300 kg digital crane scale. The second force sensor is a CALT DYLY-103 100 kg S
shaped load cell. The load cell is used in conjunction with a HX711 Load Cell Amplifier.
Pressure is measured with a Freescale MPX5700 GP 5 V pressure sensor. Building air
supply pressure is controlled with two pressure regulators in series. The first is a Parker
model 20R113GC 0 ´ 120 psi pressure regulator. The second is a Husky 3 /8 inch High
Performance Air Regulator HDA72200. A Festo VTUG-10-MRCR-S1T-26V20-T516LA-UL-
T532S-8K valve manifold was used to supply air to the actuator. Pressure and load cell
amplifier data are sent to MATLAB via an Arduino Uno style Sparkfun BlackBoard C
microcontroller.
The length measurements were made using a tape measure. When there was sufficient
curvature in the BPA during knee extensor torque tests, a flexible piece of string was used
to mark the axial length and then transferred to the tape measure. Angle measurements
were taken with a Medigauge digital electronic goniometer or with MALENOO analog
goniometers of 6, 8, or 12 inch lengths.
4.1.3 Hybrid Torque Calculation Method
A Hybrid Torque calculation method was developed to help uncover inconsistencies
between the model and recorded torque values. This method measures the actual muscle
length and angles of the robot to calculate force and joint torque. With this method, it is

PDF PAGE 102
79
easier to determine which parts of the torque equation did not achieve their predicted
value and provide a correction to that part of the equation. The classical mechanics way to
calculate the torque about a joint is to take the cross product of the position vector d and
the force vectorF, both defined in Section 4.1.1,
m=dˆF(4.3)
Knee torque is the scalar component of this moment about the joint axis, written Mk to
make its axis explicit. Because the moment arm is the perpendicular distance from that
axis to the line of action, the vector cross product of Eq. (4.3) reduces to an ordinary scalar
product,
Mk =r F(4.4)
where r is the scalar moment arm of Eq. (4.1), here measured directly with a tape
measure as the perpendicular distance from the knee axis to the actuator’s line of action,
and F is the scalar force magnitude, calculated by measuring the BPA’s P, l0, l620 and
lm values and using the model fit equations determined in the following section. The
hybrid method therefore replaces the model-predicted moment arm rk of Eq. (4.2) and the
model-predicted muscle length with their measured counterparts, leaving the force model
as the only predicted quantity.

PDF PAGE 103
80
4.1.4 Improved Torque Model
The classical kinematic torque model assumed rigid links and inextensible muscle paths.
Data collected during the first flexor experiments (Section 4.2) showed that this assumption
does not hold for a pneumatic system: measured muscle lengths were consistently shorter
than predicted, and measured torque deviated from prediction in a load-dependent manner.
This section describes the corrections that were developed to account for a constant length
offset, series compliance in the attachment hardware and artificial tendons, and the loss of
usable muscle length when a BPA wraps around the joint.
These corrections are deliberately formulated to be identified from experiment rather
than derived analytically, because the physical effects they represent are distributed
and not separately measurable. The lumped compliance absorbs the cantilevered three-
dimensional-printed attachment brackets, whose geometry is a printed CAD part rather
than a prismatic beam with a tractable second moment of area, together with the bone fix-
tures, the cable winch of the test stand, and the artificial tendon. Predicting that aggregate
stiffness from first principles would require finite-element models of each component with
material properties (anisotropic printed polymer, braided sleeve, construction-dependent
tendon stiffness) that are themselves unmeasured. The correction terms therefore param-
eterize only what the torque model needs: χ0 absorbs the constant length discrepancy
observed at zero load, χ1 and χ2 are effective stiffnesses in the bracket frame’s principal
directions, and χ3 scales the wrapping-length loss. Each is identified by global optimiza-
tion against the measured torque rather than solved in closed form, for two reasons. First,
the terms enter the prediction through an implicit force balance, because the compliance

PDF PAGE 104
81
displacement depends on the force that it itself modifies, so the inverse problem has
no analytic solution. Second, the parameters are only jointly identifiable: preliminary
fits showed that widely different stiffness pairs can reproduce the training tests nearly
equally well, so the adopted solution must be selected by cross-validated prediction on
held-out tests rather than by residual minimization alone, which is what the multiobjective
Pareto-front procedure of Section 4.1.4 provides.
Length Offset and System Stiffness
Analysis of measured muscle length data shows that it was consistently shorter than the
predicted muscle length at all joint angles. This shorter length results in less muscle force,
a shifted RoM, and less torque in the experimental setup. That this shorter length occurs
when ε= 1 and F= 0 indicates that a constant length offset should be used. Therefore, to
produce an improved torque prediction model,l m was recalculated as
lm =l LMT ´2l f itting ´χ 0 (4.5)
where lLMT is the path length of the musculotendon from origin to insertion point,
l f itting is the length of the end caps, andχ 0 is a constant length offset.
Additionally, as the muscles produce more force, compliant components in the limb
segments and attachment hardware deform. This deformation changes the effective
braided pneumatic actuator (BPA) length, which alters contraction and therefore changes
the force predicted by the BPA model. This effect is most apparent at high forces and
low contractions. The purpose of the compliance model developed next is predictive, not

PDF PAGE 105
82
structural: it exists so that the torque calculation can correct the actuator length for load-
dependent deformation, and it makes no claim about the stiffness of any one component
in isolation. Accordingly, the stiffness values that populate the model, χ1 and χ2, are not
measured on the hardware directly; they are identified by the optimization of Section 4.1.4
from the measured torque, and every stiffness value quoted later in this chapter is an
optimizer output. The combined hardware compliance is approximated using Hooke’s
law in the bracket’s principal directions.
A local bracket frame was established at the attachment bracket. The ˆxbr axis was
aligned with the vector from the bracket reference point to the BPA attachment point and
represents the stiffest direction of the bracket. The ˆybr axis was chosen to point in the
posterior direction with respect to the tibia frame, corresponding to the primary cantilever
bending direction (see Fig. 4.4), while the ˆzbr axis completes the right-handed frame. In
this frame the bracket is modeled as three independent linear springs along the principal
axes, with stiffnessesk x,k y, andk z in thex br,y br, andz br directions.
For the two-rotation evaluator, the bracket frame was constructed from the vector
joining the bracket origin to attachment point pA or pB. The first rotation, through θz about
the fixed space-frame axis ˆzs, points the intermediate axis ˆx1 toward the sagittal-plane
projection, psag

This projected-compliance form is used because the force is applied along ˆu, while the

Here keq is the equivalent stiffness of every compliant element in the actuator path, and
the bracket-only case is the special case with no tendon. When the BPA attaches directly
to the bracket, keq excludes the tendon and equals k ˆu,br of Eq. (4.18). When an artificial
tendon was used, it was modeled as an axial spring with stiffness kten: bracket deflection
and tendon stretch both shorten the effective BPA length along the actuator line of action,
the two elements carry the same force, and their scalar compliances add in series,
keq =
(︃ 1
k ˆu,br
+ 1
kten
)︃´1
(4.20)
This series reduction is exact only under a specific set of conditions, each of which
corresponds to a way the physical hardware could violate it. The bracket and the tendon
must carry the same force, which holds for two massless elements coupled in series under
quasi-static loading; an additional compliant coupling would require its own term in the
series compliance. Their displacements must be kinematically additive along the line of
action, with no displacement lost to off-axis geometry. Within a single iteration of the
force balance the line of action ˆu is treated as fixed, which is a local approximation: once
the balance returns the compliance displacement, the calculation updates the attachment
locations with the deflected bracket positions and re-evaluates ˆu and the moment arm
at the deflected geometry, as described below. The elements are assumed to carry no
moment, so rotational compliance and force–moment coupling at the attachments are
neglected. Both elements are linear, with stiffness independent of deflection, and the

PDF PAGE 112
89
tendon is modeled without preload, so its tangent stiffness does not stiffen with load state.
Finally, compliance deflection shortens the actuator, as Eq.(4.21) assumes, because a BPA
produces force only in tension: under its own pulling force the compliant attachments
give way in the direction that relieves that tension. A deflection that would lengthen
the actuator can be produced by an antagonistic pull, but that loading lies outside this
treatment.
Since δ is the scalar compliance displacement along the BPA line of action, then the
compliance-corrected active BPA length used in the force balance is
lm(δ) =l m(0)´δ(4.21)
wherel m(0)is the initial muscle length calculated from (4.5).
For a specified set of correction parameters, the following calculation solves the
attachment-compliance displacement at each knee angle; it is distinct from the optimiza-
tion that identifies those parameters from experimental torque data (Section 4.1.4). The
corresponding relative contraction is ε˚(δ). Since the BPA force depends on contraction,
and contraction depends on the compliance displacement, δ is the root of the nonlinear
force-balance equation
Fmax ¨F BPA(ε˚(δ),P ˚) ´k eq δ=0 (4.22)
The root is found with a bracketed scalar solver (fzero in MATLAB) on the physically
admissible interval between zero deflection and the deflection that would bring the actua-
tor to its maximum usable contraction. The evaluator supplies no custom solver options,

PDF PAGE 113
90
so termination uses the default displacement tolerance. This numerical tolerance does
not establish the accuracy of the physical model. Two safeguards act as secondary stop
conditions. If the actuator already sits at or beyond its maximum usable contraction at
zero deflection, no root is sought and δ is set to zero; if the residual shows no sign change
across the interval, the solver likewise returnsδ=0 rather than a spurious root.
After solving Eq. 4.22, the corresponding force magnitude was used in Eq. 4.12 to
compute the vector bracket deflection. This deflection was transformed back into the
limb reference frame and used to update the BPA attachment location. The routed BPA
length, force direction, moment arm, and torque were then recalculated using the updated
attachment location. In the present implementation, the optimized stiffness terms χ1 and
χ2 were treated as effective compliance parameters for the attachment system rather than
direct measurements of an isolated bracket. This allowed unmodeled compliance in the
surrounding hardware to be represented by the reduced-order stiffness model.
The actuator path can carry compliant hardware at both ends: the insertion-side bracket
of the preceding development together with a second, origin-side bracket. Figure 4.5 distin-
guishes the three-dimensional displacement of each bracket from the scalar displacement
along the BPA force path. For bracket j, the displacement resolves into components η1,j,

BPA Fictive Contraction
As the knee flexes, the extensor BPA contacts the anterior joint surface and bends around it
(Figure 4.6). The rigid routed-length calculation does not account for the resulting change
in usable contraction. I represent this effect with fictive strain: an empirical increase in
the contraction seen by the force model, beyond the actuator’s geometric contraction. The
correction is phenomenological; it does not resolve wall stresses or the deformation of the
braided actuator. A wrapping-loss term χ3 scales the associated reduction in usable length:

Identification of the Torque-Model Correction Terms
I identified the correction terms in two stages using gamultiobj in MATLAB. The flexor
stage fits (χ0, χ1, χ2) with compliance at both attachments. The adopted fold trains on the
l0 = 48.5 cm and 41.0 cm tests and validates on the 45.7 cm and 40.6 cm tendon tests. The
extensor stage locks (χ1, χ2) to that same flexor-front member and fits only (χ0, χ3). Its
adopted fold trains on the 43.6 cm, 43.2 cm-tendon, and 45.7 cm tests and validates on the
40.0 cm plain, 40.0 cm-tendon, and 46.5 cm tests. The extensor length offset is allowed to
be negative. Appendix C records the test constants, bounds, stiffness-axis assignments,
and adopted values in Tables C1, C4, C7, C6, and C8.
An initial single-bracket identification preceded the two-bracket model. Its results
are retained as historical comparisons, but the two-stage procedure defines the adopted
correction terms. Each candidate is evaluated using RMSE, FVU, and maximum absolute
residual. These metrics jointly measure torque error; none isolates a unique source such as
level offset, waveform shape, or angular misalignment.
The objective vector of the identification is defined explicitly. For a candidate correction
vector χ, (χ0, χ1, χ2) in the flexor stage and (χ0, χ3) on the locked stiffness pair in the
extensor refit, the predicted torque curve of test i is interpolated to its ni measured knee
angles θij, giving residuals eij =M ij ´ ˆM(θij; χ) against the measured torque Mij, and the
three goodness-of-fit components of that test are

RMSE summarizes the overall error magnitude. FVU expresses the residual sum of squares
relative to the sum of squared deviations of the measured torque from its mean¯Mi, with the
factor (ni ´ 1)/ni carrying the small-sample adjustment of the evaluators. The maximum
absolute residual records the largest pointwise error. RMSE and the maximum residual
have torque units, whereas FVU is dimensionless. For a given test, FVU is proportional
to squared RMSE, so the two metrics are not independent measures of shape and level.
Across tests, their different scaling changes the relative weighting of the errors. The solver
minimizes the objective vector

objectives dimensionless and the training tests commensurate. The search carried no con-
straints beyond the parameter bounds of each stage, and the returned Pareto fronts were
filtered to members scoring at or below the zero-correction baseline on every component
of every test in that stage’s identification pool.
The solver is the genetic multiobjective solver gamultiobj, an implementation of the
elitist nondominated-sorting genetic algorithm NSGA-II [Deb et al., 2002, The MathWorks,
Inc., 2025], which returns the Pareto front from which the adopted solution is selected by

PDF PAGE 119
96
the holdout-validation procedure using held-out tests. The production runs use adaptive-
feasible mutation and scattered crossover at a fraction of 0.8, with MATLAB’s default
Pareto fraction of 0.35; the population settings are stage-specific. The flexor two-bracket
fit evolves a population of 150 for up to 600 generations and terminates early once the
geometric average relative change in the spread of the Pareto front falls below 4 ˆ 10´3;
the extensor refit uses a population of 50 for up to 150 generations with a spread tolerance
of 1 ˆ 10´3. In both stages the parameters are searched in a normalized space, with length
offsets in centimeters and stiffnesses on a base-ten logarithmic scale, so that the genetic
search samples the stiffness bounds, which span an order of magnitude, uniformly by
decade, and the initial population is seeded around the expected solution region. The
search bounds and the adopted values are recorded in Appendix C.
4.1.5 Compliance-Unaware Mesh-Optimization Route Design
The attachment locations of the BPAs are themselves design variables. Morrow and I
developed the mesh-placement method as part of this work [Morrow et al., 2020]. The
placement problem was first solved by mesh optimization: candidate origins are enumer-
ated on the femur bone-mesh surface and candidate insertions on the tibia bone-mesh
surface, the torque profile of every candidate pair is evaluated over the range of motion
with the straight-route muscle calculation, and the best-scoring pair is kept. In its original
form this method is compliance-unaware. Routes are chains of via points on rigid links,
the attachments are inextensible, and the predicted torque is the rigid-body prediction;
nothing in the search knows that the hardware between actuator and joint bends, stretches,

PDF PAGE 120
97
and settles under load. The implementation minimizes a cost that rewards torque surplus
in the required direction and penalizes misalignment of the human and robot torque

PDF PAGE 124
101
Human torque targets are computed from the Gait2392 lower-extremity model [Delp
et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018]: the flexor is graded against the biceps femoris short head
and the extensor against vastus medialis. Each target is a maximum-isometric torque
curve: OpenSim moment arms, obtained as the partial derivative of muscle-tendon length
with respect to the knee coordinate, r(θk) =Bl MT/Bθk, are evaluated across the range
of motion and multiplied by each muscle’s maximum isometric force (provenance and
exported tables in Appendix A). Hard constraints (bone clearance, wrap feasibility, actuator
strain and contraction limits, tendon geometry) are enforced alongside the cost, and any
candidate whose prediction fails is rejected with a prohibitive penalty.
The solution pipeline is common to both configurations. Each run begins with a full
sanity evaluation of the seed design and a small smoke test of the solver setup, then
executes surrogateopt, MATLAB’s surrogate-assisted global solver for expensive black-
box objectives, which builds a spatial interpolation surrogate over sampled candidate
points and searches it adaptively [The MathWorks, Inc., 2025], in parallel under per-
configuration evaluation budgets of 3,000 for the extensor route and 1,000 for the flexor
route (the later cam variant of Section 4.3.2 uses 1,000 surrogate and 1,500 refinement
evaluations), and finally refines the best feasible point with patternsearch, a derivative-
free mesh-adaptive direct search method [Audet and Dennis, 2006, The MathWorks, Inc.,
2025], for 15,000 further evaluations. The surrogate budgets sit well past the observed
plateau of a few hundred evaluations, so they cap run time rather than define convergence.
Each stage stops on its evaluation budget; the pattern search also stops early when its
mesh collapses below the mesh tolerance with the constraints satisfied, which is the usual
exit once the design has converged. The entry scripts and their cost functions are archived

PDF PAGE 125
102
with the code.
Although both configurations share this pipeline, their cost functions differ in ways
that reflect the mechanics. The flexor BPA routes around a single moving wrap point, so
clearance from the tibia and femoral condyles is enforced by continuous signed-distance
constraints, and its cost adds an off-axis torque penalty (torques about the joint axes other
than the knee axis, relative to the original single-BPA route) and a penalty if the wrap
fails to release cleanly as the knee extends. The extensor BPA wraps over the front of the
joint through as many as seven sequential contacts, so clearance is enforced instead by the
contact-elimination rule that governs the route, and its cost penalizes torque overshoot
above the human curve and excessive actuator contraction, which risks bottoming out
at high flexion. The flexor and extensor targets alike carry a required torque margin of
5 % above the human curve, so that a route that merely touches the target at any angle is
rejected. With two BPAs per joint, the flexor route is solved as two separately evaluated
paths, while the extensor model scales the force of a single shared route by the actuator
count.
In the redesign runs of this dissertation, every candidate route of Equations (4.32)
and (4.33) is evaluated through the stiffness-aware force balance of Section 4.1.4, with
the identified correction terms applied. The objective family itself descends from the
compliance-unaware formulation of Equation (4.29): the search moves attachment geome-
try against a human torque envelope in both cases, and what changed between the two is
the prediction that scores the geometry, not the design variables. That evolution, and the
final flexor and extensor objectives built on it, are taken up in Section 4.3.2.

PDF PAGE 126
103
4.2 Results
With the actuator force model validated at the actuator level, the same model was used to
predict isometric torque about the knee joint for the test stands described in Sections 4.1.1
and 4.1.3. Measured torque is compared against three predictions: the original rigid-body
torque model of Section 2.4, a prediction using the improved BPA force characterization
alone (Chapter 3), and the improved torque model of Section 4.1.4 after optimization. The
hybrid torque method (Section 4.1.3) is used to isolate the sources of error.
4.2.1 Flexor on Pin Joint
Figure 4.7 shows the measured flexor torque on the pinned knee next to the pre-optimization
prediction series for the four cross-validation tests. Both pre-optimization force models
overpredicted the magnitude of the measured flexor torque, and on the angles where the
two predictions are both defined the improved BPA characterization fit the measurements
better than the original rigid-body prediction on every test, although it degraded near
full extension, where the rigid-body prediction is undefined altogether (Table 4.3). The
back-calculated (hybrid) torque reproduced the measurements closely, which localizes the
misprediction to the modeled kinematics, the muscle length lm and the moment arm r,
rather than to the force model.

4.2.2 Extensor on Pin Joint
When the new stiffness and length offset model was used to predict torque for the knee
extensor, measured torque was slightly higher than the original rigid-body model predicts
for the 48.0 cm resting length BPA, slightly lower than expected for the 45.7cm BPA, and
much lower for the 41.5 cm BPA. Only the χ1 and χ2 terms from the previous optimization
results were used with the extensor configuration. By inspection, the zero point of the
measured torque was shifted to the right of the original model, indicating an offset opposite
in sign to the χ0 term in the previous section. The BPA with l0 = 45.7 cm illustrates the
pattern: the hybrid-method calculated torque correlates well with the originally predicted
torque, while the actual measured torque diverges.
An initial single-bracket extensor fit yielded χ0 =´ 10.9 mm and χ3 = 0.123; its
historical goodness-of-fit values are retained in Table C11. The adopted two-stage refit
instead uses the shared flexor stiffness pair and the six-test fold defined in Section 4.1.4.
Figure 4.9 and Table 4.5 report that adopted fit.

4.2.4 Flexor on Biomimetic Knee
Fig. 4.12 shows the biomimetic-knee flexor measurements with the flexor terms carried
over unchanged from the pinned-knee identification; the bracket frame for the I10 mm
BPA is shown in Fig. 4.4C, and for the I20 mm BPA in Fig. 4.4D. PanelApresents the
I10 mm actuator at l0 = 41.5 cm with a 21 mm artificial tendon (I1.5 mm Shimano bicycle
brake cable wrapped four times), pressurized to 604 kPa; the corrected model reached an
RMSE of 0.3007 N m, an FVU of 0.0102, and a maximum absolute residual of 0.4647 N m.
PanelBpresents the I20 mm actuator at l0 = 41.5 cm with a 14 mm artificial tendon made
out of the same brake cable, wrapped three times so that six cable segments share the
load, each wrap contributing two tendon lengths and cross-sectional areas. PanelBwas
tested at 620 kPa, for which the corrected model reached an RMSE of 1.8024 N m, an FVU
of 0.0499, and a maximum absolute residual of 3.5678 N m.
The torque of the single I20 mm flexor actuator of panelBdid not exceed the magni-
tude of the maximum human isometric flexion torque between 60˝ and 100˝ of knee flexion,
and within a ˘20% band about the human mean it still fell short of human-level torque
between 78˝ and 103˝ of flexion. Because operating at 620 kPa across the full desired range
of motion would load the printed femur beyond its ultimate strength, single points were
also recorded at the reduced pressures 560kPa, 421 kPa, 325 kPa and 281 kPa, which show
that the desired range of motion is achievable at reduced pressure. This shortfall in flexion
torque defines the target that the attachment-route redesign of Section 4.3.2 addresses.
PanelCshows the outcome for the redesigned two-BPA flexor route of Section 4.3.6: across
its fifteen tested measurements from ´119˝ to 5˝, the measured torque magnitude met or

PDF PAGE 137
114
exceeded the human biceps-femoris short-head maximum at every point, with margins
from 0.42 N m at´105˝ to 17.0 N m at´14 ˝.
4.3 Discussion
4.3.1 Reasons for the Correction Terms
The correction terms of Section 4.1.4 exist because the physical transmission between
the actuator and the joint is neither rigid nor lossless, and each term names one family
of physical reasons. A constant length offset χ0 exists because of manufacturing and
positioning tolerances: the printed bone segments, the machined knee links, and the end-
cap fittings each carry dimensional tolerance, the actuator is pinned at a measured location
on that stack of tolerances, and the accumulated discrepancy appears as a length that is
consistently shorter than the predicted musculotendon path at every joint angle, including
at zero load where no compliance can act. The stiffness terms χ1 and χ2 exist because
loaded components displace: the isometric system is not rigid, and there is compliance in
the winch cable of the test stand, the artificial tendons, and the cantilevered attachment
brackets that bend and stretch under actuator load. The wrapping-loss term χ3 exists
because a BPA that bends over the joint loses usable contraction length that no straight-line
model accounts for, and because the same loading could displace the actuator off its
intended seat, as the observed slip off the anterior head of the femur bolt shows.
Our improved BPA characterization predicts torque more accurately than the original
BPA model (see Fig. 4.7). This only accounts for part of the error. Many factors affect
the torque results. Compliance in the testing system, positioning tolerances, flexibility of

Figure 4.12: Biomimetic-knee torque versus knee angle for the flexor BPAs. Knee angle
is negative in flexion, and flexion torque is negative.(A) I10 mm, l0 = 41.5 cm actuator

pinned knee; the dashed light orange line is the unoptimized prediction of the improved

PDF PAGE 139
116
brackets, imperfect path modeling, and variation in individual BPAs are other factors.
Simplifying and testing the model revealed deficiencies in our previous analysis. The
isometric system is not rigid. There is compliance in the winch cable, artificial tendons,
and brackets that bend and stretch. We introduced a constant length offset term χ0, axial
stiffness χ1, and bending stiffnessχ2. The bracket frame of reference was placed at the onset
of the cantilevered section and rotated about Z. The optimization identified parameter
values for a I10 mm BPA withl0 = 48.5 cm in the simple pinned knee configuration. These
results were validated with a secondI10 mm BPA withl 0 =45.7 cm.
The modeling of extensor BPA paths that wrap around the knee joint is an approxima-
tion with inherent error (Fig. 4.11). When the moment arm is short, small changes to it
can have a large effect on the resultant torque [Young et al., 2019]. It was assumed that
wrapping the BPA around the joint would be easier to account for than adding a patella,
deterministically designing its location as a function of knee angle, and accounting for fric-
tion. At greater knee flexion angles, shorter l0 BPAs were observed to stretch and compress
(see Fig. 4.1C). The same loading sometimes caused the BPA to slip off the anterior head
of the bolt that holds the femur to the knee. This produced a ˘20 mm displacement in
the Z direction, changing both the force vector and the torque. Fig. 4.9 shows a deviation
between measured and originally predicted results that widens as expected torque in-
creases. This indicates system compliance. However, the hybrid-calculated torque matches
or exceeds the predicted torque, except at greater flexion angles for the l0 = 41.5 cm BPA,
indicating a loss of functional lm; this is why the χ3 term was created in (4.24). Fig. 4.9
shows the improved fit from running the optimization that includes this term.
The biomimetic knee joint with an extensor BPA was used to validate the extensor

PDF PAGE 140
117
optimization results. The corrected prediction more closely matches the measured torque
values, although it remains higher than the measurements.
The nonlinear biomimetic robotic system presented here replaces rigid-body simplifica-
tions commonly used in other robotic and biomechanical systems. Instead, progressing
from simple to complex systems elucidated compliance mechanisms that can lead to more
accurate first-iteration designs. Technologies have been developed to predict system bend-
ing and axial compliance, a constant length offset, and a loss of usable length that occurs
as artificial muscle bends around a joint.
The quasi-static force characterization also fixes the coefficients that a dynamic actuator
model needs. Writing the actuator’s contraction as a second-order system,m¨ε˚ +c( ˙ε˚) ˙ε˚ +
k(ε˚)ε˚ =F , the stiffness term is the tangent slope of the normalized force surface of
Equation (3.4), k(ε˚, P˚) = BF

. Setting the mass and
the external force to zero then yields the equivalent viscous damping coefficient, c( ˙ε˚) =
(k(ε ˚)ε˚)/ ˙ε˚, the term that dynamic state-space actuator models, such as the pressure–force
formulation of Elzein et al. [2025], pair with this stiffness.
4.3.2 Optimizer Updates
The results figures use the adopted two-stage identification. The procedure of Section 4.1.4
yields χ0 = 3.94 mm, χ1 = 3.998 ˆ 104 N m´1, and χ2 = 1.473 ˆ 104 N m´1 for the flexor,
and χ0 =´ 6.38 mm with χ3 = 0.158 for the extensor on that same locked stiffness
pair (Table 4.6); the search bounds and the adopted values are recorded in Appendix C
(Tables C7 and C8).

PDF PAGE 141

What the correction terms do to the prediction is quantified in Tables 4.4 and 4.5. The
corrected flexor model holds FVU at or below 0.18 on the four tests of the identification,
the worst case being the 48.5 cm training test at 0.172. The excluded-test bookkeeping is
retained in Appendix C. On the extensor identification the corrected model improves the
RMSE on every used test, cutting the training-triple mean from 2.50 N¨m to 1.00 N¨m
and the validation-triple mean from 2.55 N¨m to 1.10 N¨m , and holds FVU below unity
on every used test except the 43.2 cm tendon test. On the biomimetic extensor test (l0 =
51.8 cm) the corrected prediction improves RMSE from 3.03N¨m to 1.17 N¨m and FVU
from 4.49 to 0.67, so the shared-pair model explains the bulk of that configuration’s
measured torque variance as well as its level. The genetic multiobjective solver is retained
for the identification, where the Pareto front is required for holdout validation, and the
surrogate-plus-pattern-search pipeline serves the route redesign (Section 4.1.5).
The mesh-optimization solution that preceded this identification met the torque goal
it was given, and its inability to deliver that torque in hardware is precisely what the
correction terms repair. The compliance-unaware mesh optimizer of Section 4.1.5 places
attachment routes whose rigid-body prediction meets or exceeds the magnitude of the
human torque curves, and on the model it does: the exceed-human-torque-magnitude
goal was achieved by the placement solutions it returned. The isometric tests of this

PDF PAGE 142
119
chapter show what the hardware did with those solutions. Measured muscle lengths
ran shorter than predicted, measured torque fell below prediction in a load-dependent
manner, and the shortfall grew exactly where actuator force was highest. The goal had
been met by a model that did not know the system bends; meeting it in hardware required
a stiffness-aware prediction, and every optimizer update below is built on one.
The identified parameters themselves hold well beyond the configuration that produced
them, which is what allows the optimizers to trust the corrected prediction on new designs.
The terms were identified on the pinned knee, a single-revolute joint, with I10 mm BPAs,
and they carry unchanged to a different knee type, to larger actuators, and into the
reinforced hardware: on the biomimetic four-bar knee the shared-pair model cuts the
extensor FVU from 4.49 to 0.67; with the I20 mm actuators and the high-strength high-
temperature-fiberglass-reinforced parts of the redesigned system, the carried-over flexor
terms hold FVU at 0.0102 on the 10mm biomimetic flexor test and 0.0499 on the 20mm test;
and the redesigned routes, dimensioned end to end on the corrected prediction, deliver
measured torque that meets or exceeds the human-reference magnitudes over the tested
range (Section 4.3.6). One stiffness pair, identified once, spans the joint types, actuator
sizes, and part materials of the whole program.
The final mesh optimizers close the loop between the two families of Section 4.1.5, and
their cost functions record the evolution explicitly. The original mesh optimization scored
rigid-body torque surplus with the reward of Equation (4.29). The route redesign replaced
the reward with the penalized requirement of Equations (4.32) and (4.33), shortfall against
a required envelope, evaluated through the stiffness-aware force balance. The extensor’s

PDF PAGE 143
120
final form, the cam-profile redesign, adds three terms to the family objective,

of Equation (4.31), and the moment-arm step and cam regularizer carry the weights of

the solver keeps buying moment arm after the envelope is met, and the step term holds
the release schedule smooth while it does.
The initial route searches bounded the BPA origins and insertions near the correspond-
ing human muscle attachments to preserve biomimetic routing. Those anatomical bounds
had to be relaxed where the available BPA contraction could not cover the knee range of
motion. The resulting routes then required a second tradeoff: reducing the actuator forces
carried by the attachment hardware after parts failed during testing. The cam redesign
changes the moment arm and contact geometry to retain the torque requirement at lower
actuator force.
The final flexor optimizer applies the wrapping-loss term χ3, identified on the extensor,
because the flexor route can also bring the BPA into contact with the tibia and the femoral
condyles at high flexion, and an actuator that a possible contact could bend must be scored
with the length that bending removes. Its search box was deliberately expanded as an
acknowledged tradeoff with biomimicry: the muscle origin was freed to move more than
100 mm proximal of the femoral shaft, far from the human biceps femoris origin, purely for
reserve contraction, the extra actuator length, and therefore force and torque, available at

PDF PAGE 144
121
high flexion where the single-actuator tests fell short. Two 20mm BPAs are carried, both to
reach the required torque and to limit the off-axis torque that the single-mirrored-actuator
arrangement was found to load parts past their breaking point. A mirrored two-actuator
solution, the two routes exact reflections of one another about the mid-sagittal plane, was
found and rejected: it is not bio-realistic if a pelvis is ever introduced, since the medial
origin would sit where pelvic structure belongs. The adopted solution is a staggered-offset
pair, with a separately solved route for each actuator; the second insertion mirrors the first
across the sagittal plane, while its origin preserves the first route’s lateral endpoint offset,
so both origins sit off the same side of the knee and the two routes do not cross. Each route
is given its own wrapping calculation and its own collision checks.
The final extensor optimizer faces a requirement of a different order. The extensor target
is set by vastus medialis, the quadriceps muscle with the smallest maximum isometric force
in the reference set, and even that weakest-quad envelope demands roughly twice the peak
torque of the flexor requirement, 50.32 N m against 23.16 N m. The two 20mm extensor
BPAs nominally share one path, a single route whose force is scaled by the actuator count,
because the extensor route wraps over the front of the joint through as many as seven
sequential contacts and duplicating that chain would double the interference problem. That
shared-path economy has a programming cost: the large solution space of the wrapped
route makes the contact and release angles, the knee angles at which each wrap contact
activates and releases, difficult to schedule by inspection, and the calculation that produces
them is formalized in Appendix C.7. The routing was additionally modified to reduce the
force the actuators exert, because the direct route’s higher actuator forces broke the printed
and machined parts during testing; reducing moment-arm demand and contact severity at

PDF PAGE 145
122
lower force is what led into the cam algorithm of Equation (4.34).
The route redesign built on these values addresses the single-muscle shortfall at high
flexion angles documented in Section 4.2: each configuration now carries two 20 mm BPAs
per route. The flexor solution meets the flexor torque target with a minimum margin of
+5.01 % at ´107˝, with a separately solved route for each actuator. The second insertion
mirrors the first across the sagittal plane, while its origin preserves the first route’s lateral
endpoint offset; its wrapping and clearance are evaluated independently. The direct
extensor route meets or exceeds its human-scaled target with the required 5 % overshoot
as the thinnest margin at every angle, +5.02 % at worst, while respecting the wrap-release
schedule and contraction limits of the route-elimination rule (Appendix C); it was later
superseded by the cam redesign of Section 4.3.2. These are mechanical design predictions.
Their complete incorporation into the converted neuromechanical models remains future
integration work.
Redesigned-Route Results on the Gait2392 Robot-Body Model
This subsection records the quantitative outcomes of the redesigned routes on the Gait2392-
derived robot-body model, whose muscle force and torque targets define the redesign
objectives. All three result families were produced by the surrogate-plus-pattern-search
pipeline of Section 4.1.5 under the adopted correction terms (Appendix C, Table C8) with
one shared actuator plan: two 20 mm BPAs per route, a 620 kPa pressure ceiling, and
a maximum usable contraction of 0.255 in the force model. Each family minimizes its
own objective function, a unitless weighted sum of normalized penalty terms in which

PDF PAGE 146
123
zero corresponds to no violated penalty, led by the worst-angle and mean-square torque
shortfall against the required 5 %-overshoot human envelope, followed by overshoot, rest-
length, strain, contraction, and mild design-change penalties. The three functions differ in
penalty families and normalizations (the flexor and pulley objectives add off-axis-excess,
wrap-release, and second-BPA anchor terms, and the pulley objective evaluates its torque
requirement on the insertion-side torque after the tackle’s force loss), so their numerical
values are not commensurable with one another. The human-scaled requirements are
the OpenSim isometric torque profiles of the reference human muscles over the same
design-grid angles, the vastus medialis for the extensor and the biceps femoris short head
for the flexor.
The direct extensor route exceeds the human envelope in magnitude at every angle,
with zero shortfall points: across the design range the predicted two-BPA extensor torque
spans 35.45 N¨m to 109.4 N¨m against the human-scaled requirement of 20.25 N¨m to
50.32 N¨m , and the required 5 % overshoot is consumed as the thinnest margin exactly as
designed, at a minimum margin of+5.02 % at the worst-case angle of´20.2 ˝.
Allowing the tibial-side route points to trace a cam profile produced the current extensor
design (Figure 4.13). The final cam geometry includes the optimized route and the verified
adjustment of the distal route point and tendon length. Its minimum torque margin is
+30.83 % with respect to the required envelope, the human target plus the 5 % overshoot,
and +39.77 % with respect to the plain human target. The pressure required to meet the
envelope spans 269kPa–507 kPa across the range of motion, comfortably above the 150kPa
regulated floor of the VTUG valve manifold that supplies the hardware. The geometry
confirms that the headroom is not bought with routing risk: the tibial wrap follows a

The flexor route, built from two separately evaluated BPA paths, meets the flexor torque
target with a minimum margin fraction of 0.0501 against the required 0.05. Its predicted
knee-axis torque spans ´81.23 N¨m to ´3.864 N¨m against the human-scaled requirement
of ´23.16 N¨m to ´2.461 N¨m (peak magnitude 23.16 N¨m ), again exceeding the human
envelope at every angle. The flexor redesign applies the adopted correction terms of
Table C8, including the wrapping factor identified on the extensor configuration.
The Mono/Bi-pulley algorithms extend the route search space by allowing the artificial
tendon to be reeved over pulleys before insertion. On the knee-flexor route, the pulley
variant converged to a collision-feasible layout with zero bound violations and a peak
predicted flexion torque of 85.56 N¨m on the insertion-side measure, the torque applied at
the insertion after the tackle’s force loss; Section 4.3.3 quantifies what the redirect adds on
the knee-axis measure and the off-axis torque it bounds.
4.3.3 Mono- and Bi-Pulley Tendon Routing
Every optimizer of the preceding sections scores its routes with the MonoPamDataExplicit-
type calculation: a fixed chain of via points carrying one segment across from the parent
body into the child, its contacts wrapped and released by the elimination rule, its length
and moment arm read from that chain. The Mono- and Bi-pulley algorithms are the
generalization of exactly that calculation. The artificial tendon is reeved over pulleys

PDF PAGE 149
126
before insertion, forming a reverse pulley, a block-and-tackle transmission: withGtackle
line parts supporting the moving block, the tendon travel at the insertion is multiplied by
G while the force delivered there is divided by G, and at G= 1 the reeved route reduces to
the straight-tendon route of the direct redesign. The tendon exit is either body-fixed on the
proximal body so that its direction rotates with the joint, or anchored through a housing
to the distal body, and the tackle’s increased internal force and the pulley-block bracket
deflections enter the same stiffness-aware force balance as the direct route’s brackets. Read
this way the pulley variants are not a new actuator concept but the general routing case of
the mesh-optimization machinery, of which every direct route is theG=1 special case.
At unity transmission, the knee-flexor pulley route increased peak predicted knee-axis
torque from 81.23 N m to 85.74 N m, while limiting off-axis torque to 0.803 N m. This
comparison measures the effect of the two optimized routing configurations; their different
objective functions do not isolate the pulley alone. The pulley serves as a redirect in
this configuration rather than a force multiplier. Appendix C.8 records the optimizer
diagnostics and the broader route searches.
A subsequent transmission study re-solved actuator sizes for each tackle configuration
at five anatomical muscle sites. A two-BPA pair through a two-part tackle preserves net
actuator force while doubling insertion travel; increasing travel can extend the angle range
over which a route meets its torque requirement. The corresponding torque and margin
surfaces are shown in Figures C3 and C4. These calculations use the moving-force-point
routing, with the adjacent joint fixed for the biarticular evaluation planes; they do not
establish simultaneous performance across both joints. Physical verification of the pulley
configurations remains future work.

PDF PAGE 150
127
4.3.4 Actuator-Count and Transmission Study for Vastus Medialis
The vastus medialis study evaluates one to three parallel 20 mm BPAs and one to three
tackle line parts at 620 kPa, with actuator and tendon lengths re-solved for each combina-
tion. The attachment geometry is fixed by the study’s routing family. Both OpenSim and
cable-path moment arms are tested, with and without the compliance correction. In the
corrected mode the calculation uses the pulley model directly; this model does not include
the wrapping-loss termχ 3, so it is not the same prediction as the cam redesign.
No tested configuration meets the target at all five graded angles. The best coverage
is four of five, obtained with three BPAs through a two-part tackle using the compliance
correction and the cable-path moment arm. Its four passing-angle margins are +56.3,
+40.1, +35.4, and +1.0 N m. It fails at 10 ˝, where this routing family approaches zero
torque. The cable path uses a ring point 0.75mm off the bone surface. These results identify
a limitation of the tested routing family; they do not establish a minimum actuator count
capable of meeting the complete envelope. The separate cam redesign meets its own target
over its design grid with two BPAs (Section 4.3.2).
4.3.5 Whole-Leg Transmission Capability
The configuration survey grades each actuator against the same-named single muscle
of the Gait2392 model [Delp et al., 2007, Seth et al., 2018] on a 5 ˆ 5 grid of two joint
coordinates. The hip adduction domain is clipped by its flexion-dependent envelope. A
cell meets the requirement when the BPA is taut and its torque has the human torque’s
sign and at least its magnitude. The coverage metric is the fraction of included grid cells

PDF PAGE 151
128
meeting that condition; the aggregate is the unweighted mean of the per-actuator fractions,
not a continuous-area integral or a whole-leg torque sum.
The BPA force model is evaluated at 620 kPa [Bolen et al., 2026]. The comparison
includes all 27 actuator sites (Table C12). The baseline uses the master’s-era attachment
routing at unity transmission; its eight nominal 40mm sites are evaluated with four parallel
20 mm BPAs, while the other sites use the survey’s base configuration. The updated side
selects the saved transmission-ladder winner at each site, with actuator length and tendon
length re-solved per configuration. It retains the same underlying attachment routing and
the original diameter specification, including 40 mm actuators at those eight sites. The
comparison therefore combines transmission, actuator sizing, and the baseline diameter
substitution; it does not isolate an effect of attachment placement or compare two all-20mm
assemblies.
The mean grid coverage increases from 37.28 % to 65.19 %, improving at 23 sites and
tying at four . A separate sign-aware torque-ratio score averages min(M R/M H, 1) over the
graded cells, assigning zero to inactive cells. Its mean falls from ´1.93 to ´3.63. Negative
ratios penalize torque in the wrong direction, so greater coverage can coexist with a worse
mean ratio where severe wrong-sign predictions remain. These metrics support different
conclusions and should be considered together.
For comparison, the average of the two axis-wise mean coverages is 38.39 % for the
baseline and 66.17 % for the updated configurations. On a complete rectangular grid,
averaging row and column coverages equals the overall cell fraction exactly; it cannot
reduce the influence of edge poses there. The two summaries differ only on the four
hip-envelope domains with excluded cells. The two metrics and the torque-ratio score are

PDF PAGE 152
129
reported together, so their different weightings remain visible.
4.3.6 Physical Tests of the Redesigned Routes
The redesigned cam-extensor route was tested at nine isometric holds, and the two-BPA
flexor route at fifteen holds. Both test sets span knee angles from ´119˝ to 5˝. The test-
specific analysis scripts transform the measured load-cell wrench to the moving knee
center and evaluate model predictions at each measured angle and pressure. Figures 4.14
and 4.15 compare these reductions with the human-reference torques and the optimized
620 kPa curves.
The measured torque magnitudes reach or exceed the human-reference magnitudes
over most of the tested range. Agreement with the pressure-specific prediction is uneven,
particularly for the flexor at intermediate angles. The tests therefore support the redesigned
routes’ torque capacity while identifying prediction errors that remain to be explained.
Since pressure varied between holds, the measured points do not constitute a full-pressure
envelope at every angle.

PDF PAGE 153
130

model predictions at the measured angles and pressures. The pink curve is the optimized
620 kPa prediction, and the black dotted curve is the human target. Point area scales with

5 The Sensory Afferent Database
5.1 A Structured Afferent Resource
The synthetic nervous system of Chapter 6 is assembled from physiological parts: Ia, Ib,
and II afferents, the reflex pathways they drive, and the spinal circuits those pathways
modulate. Building that controller is a design problem, and design needs parameters, not
only principles. For each sensory channel, the designer has to know what the receptor
encodes, over what ranges of length, velocity, and force it operates and with what receptor
dynamics, and what the afferent does at the spinal end: which interneuron populations it
contacts, whether the action is excitatory or inhibitory, whether the target is ipsilateral or
contralateral, and in which phase of the step cycle the pathway is open. Every element of
that specification was established experimentally, one pathway and one preparation at a
time, across a century of work in cat, mouse, rat, and human preparations, and the results
are distributed over an enormous primary literature rather than collected anywhere. A
controller designer is not the only reader stranded by that scatter. An animal researcher
planning the next experiment wants to know which preparations established a pathway
property, and how the property compares across species, before committing a preparation
to it. A roboticist wants to know which receptor encoding a sensor should imitate. Those
readers ask different questions of the same literature, and the literature answers none of
them without being gathered, structured, and searchable first.
Reviews assemble part of this knowledge, but none can serve as the working resource

PDF PAGE 156
133
itself. The three this dissertation draws on illustrate both the value and the limit: Van de
Crommert et al. [1998] organize the sensory control of the central pattern generator around
the treadmill-training literature, Windhorst [2007] assigns functional roles to muscle pro-
prioceptive feedback within a broader argument about spinal networks, and Windhorst
[2022] weighs accumulated spinal-circuit model assumptions against the experimental
record. Each synthesizes a slice of the literature; none can be filtered by afferent class,
animal preparation, or pathway, none carries its demonstrating papers as attached and
followable evidence, and each is fixed at its publication date. Extracting gain ranges,
receptor dynamics, and phase-dependent feedback roles from reviews organized around a
hypothesis means redoing the literature search the review was meant to spare the reader.
When this work began, no structured compilation of afferent properties existed from which
a controller designer, an animal researcher, or a roboticist could draw them; every reader
redid the same search.
The Sensory Afferent Database (SADb) was built within the AARL’s NeuroNex Com-
munication, Coordination, and Control in Nervous Systems (C3NS) effort to close that
gap, and we presented it at Neuroscience 2023 [Bolen et al., 2023]. It is a database rather
than a bibliography: each paper carries distilled mechanism notes, each sensory feedback
pathway is a first-class record linked to the papers that established it, and each computa-
tional model of afferent control is linked to its source paper and to the pathway records it
implements. The database lives in a five-table Airtable base (Papers, Feedback, Models,
Review Papers, and Recorders) backed by the laboratory’s Zotero libraries, with the DOI
as the canonical identifier for every record.
This chapter and the controller chapter are therefore built as one program. The database

PDF PAGE 157
134
informs the sensory channels of Chapter 6: the afferent classes the controller monitors,
the signal encoding each channel carries, and the connection rules its circuit implements
all trace to records held here, and Section 5.4 documents that mapping. The sections that
follow describe how the corpus was assembled, audited, pruned, and packaged into its
present form.
The audit and curation effort reported here began from a three-way reconciliation,
because the database’s three homes did not agree. The author’s personal Zotero collec-
tion held 538 items, the AARL group library’s Biology subtree held 911 items across 27
collections, and the Airtable base held 99 papers. Matching record by record on DOI and
normalized title showed that the personal collection was essentially a subset of the group
library (all 483 personal DOIs present) and that the Airtable base was a small curated
subset rather than a mirror: 95 of its 99 papers resolved to records in the group library’s
Biology subtree, while 443 personal items had never been entered at all. The gap was
imported in verified batches, with every written record re-read and checked after writing,
bringing the Papers table to 532 records. The corpus then kept growing while curation
caught up: citation mining in the style of Research Rabbit contributed a Crossref-verified
digest of recent papers, newly saved references flowed in from the laboratory’s Zotero
libraries, and a bulk pass imported 372 papers auto-classified from the laboratory’s staged
collection of PDFs. By the close of the expansion the audited corpus stood at 943 papers,
the full set against which every later decision in this chapter was made.
Curation then proceeded under rules designed to make every note citable. A note is
written from the paper’s own text, from the abstract at minimum and from the full text
when available, never from the curator’s recollection of the paper; papers for which no

PDF PAGE 158
135
text could be found were logged and left bare rather than guessed. Notes are one to three
sentences of distilled mechanism, following the template of the laboratory’s earliest records,
and free-text fields are constrained by controlled vocabularies for animal preparations
and afferent classes. Each feedback pathway is named by a fixed grammar of afferent,
action, side, joint, and phase (for example, “Ib stance to swing”), so that pathway records
compose into circuits the way the controller will use them. Ambiguous cases were flagged
for a human ruling rather than decided silently.
The final curation wave added the fields that make the corpus answerable as a database:
afferent class, animal-study potential, robot-and-simulation translation notes, and prune
status fields, together with a corpus citation graph exposed as record-to-record links.
Drafting was machine-assisted at scale, with 374 papers curated in fifteen batches; every
batch passed automated vocabulary, abstract-paste, and duplicate gates before anything
was written, and every record was verified against its grounding text after the write.
Grounding remained the binding constraint throughout. A PDF-first rule recovered
grounding for 47 of 55 flagged papers from the laboratory’s own library, and a university-
authenticated browser session recovered abstracts for 24 of the 70 papers that had no
findable text; the rest stayed bare by rule.
Pruning followed, in three stages, each approved before execution, and together the
stages removed 436 records: 382 papers, close to four hundred, plus 54 orphaned review,
model, and pathway records that lost their anchors to the paper cuts. First, 95 records
left as off-topic or orphaned: 88 papers removed as satellite topics (machine-learning
and aerodynamics methods, flow sensing without neurophysiology, and fish swimming
biomechanics), as duplicates, or as isolated records stranded by an earlier strict flagging

PDF PAGE 159
136
pass, plus seven orphaned pathway, model, and review records. Second, a cut list of
290 papers assembled from explicit rules for duplicates, monographs, and conference
abstracts, and from a weighted rubric ranking the remainder by citation count, age, rating,
network isolation, and record thinness, while 351 protected records (foundational authors,
feedback-linked papers, highly cited work, and pre-1940 classics) were excluded from
consideration. Third, a final round removed four papers and 47 review records whose
links had been emptied by the earlier cuts. The prune was deliberate on two grounds. The
working base had swollen past the laboratory’s free-plan 1,000-record cap, and a curated
resource that answers a design question in ten records beats a haystack of a thousand;
the audit had shown the corpus contained both, and the satellite material was answering
nothing. No record was destroyed: every deletion was snapshotted, and the packaged
explorer described below carries the 382 cut papers under an archived flag, so the audited
corpus remains reconstructible in full.
The audit and the prune are also why the laboratory built its own explorer app rather
than adopting an existing tool. Airtable remains the working home of the data, but it is a
curation tool, not a reader: it cannot travel past the laboratory’s account, and a flat grid
answers none of the questions this corpus exists to answer. An off-the-shelf bibliometric
viewer was tried first and retired within weeks; it drew one crowded picture of the whole
corpus, offered no drill-through from a cluster to the papers inside it, and let a handful of
mega-cited classics dominate every view. The query patterns this resource must serve are
specific: filter by afferent class, animal preparation, or pathway; follow a pathway record to
the papers that established it in each species; walk the citation neighborhood of a candidate
anchor paper; count the corpus by any two dimensions at once. A circuit designer wiring

PDF PAGE 160
137
a reflex, an animal researcher choosing a preparation, and a roboticist choosing a sensor
analog ask different questions of the same records, and no existing tool answered any
of them. The corpus itself was therefore packaged as a laboratory tool: a single-file,
offline explorer embedding the audited corpus, the citation graph, and the named clusters,
with searchable and pivotable tables, focus-mode citation neighborhoods drawn in the
laboratory’s own convention of papers as neurons with excitatory and inhibitory synapses,
and a generated knowledge base with an index page plus cluster, pathway, afferent, and
animal pages, all distributed as a zip that runs in a browser on any machine with no
network connection.
5.2 Audited Corpus and Curation Coverage
At the close of the curation effort the working base held 957 records: 561 papers, 39
feedback-pathway records, 244 model records, 109 review records, and 4 recorder entries,
43 records below the laboratory’s 1,000-record operating cap, with room to grow. Coverage
of the retained papers is concentrated where the laboratory’s design and experimental
work needs it. Of the 561 retained papers, 523 carry curation notes, 426 carry animal-
preparation tags, 240 carry notes assessing each paper’s animal-study potential, 118 are
linked to named feedback pathways, 86 carry an afferent classification, and 258 carry notes
translating the paper’s findings toward robot or simulation use; 550 carry DOIs, and 38
remain bare pending a findable text.
The citation graph was built over the full audited corpus of 943 papers, which includes
the 382 records the prune later removed and which the packaged database retains under

PDF PAGE 161
138
an archived flag. Within that corpus, 730 papers are connected by 11,130 directed in-
corpus citation pairs, and a Louvain community partition of the graph resolves 18 named
topic clusters, from the classics of afferent locomotor control and spinal interneuron
circuit modeling through human balance and posture to bioinspired flow sensing and
deep-learning methods. The partition earned its keep in the prune: the satellite clusters
it exposed, the flow-and-flight sensing, fish swimming, and neural-network method
clusters, match the topic groups the first prune stage removed. Within that audited
corpus, 833 papers carry curation notes and 318 carry attached PDFs, and the prune’s 382
removed records remain in the package under an archived flag so the audited scope stays
reconstructible in full. The corpus, its citation graph, its cluster assignments, and the flat
corpus export are distributed together in the packaged explorer, and Figure 5.1 shows
its table view as packaged. Figure 5.2 shows the same corpus as the packaged citation
graph: the bubble map draws every paper as a neuron, its soma sized by citation count,
and focusing a paper colors its direct citation neighborhood by direction.
5.3 AI-Assisted Literature Triage
Expanding a curated corpus is dominated by triage: deciding, paper by paper, what a
record is about and whether it belongs. We evaluated AI tools for this task in a comparative
study presented at Neuroscience 2024 [Bolen and Hunt, 2024]. The evaluation found the
expected trade: the tools accelerated the population of the database, but their output still
required substantial human oversight for accuracy.
The curation workflow reported here is the operational answer to that finding, a divi-
