141
sion of labor in which the machine drafts and the human rules. The machine contributed
scale: batch drafting from grounded text, automated gates that rejected abstract pastes
and duplicate records before writing, and per-record verification after it. The gates earned
their place on caught failures, including a record that shipped another paper’s abstract
under its title until the grounding pass flagged it. The human held every decision that
required judgment or carried risk: the curation vocabulary and its amendments, the 164
flagged judgment calls surfaced for ruling, and every single deletion in the prune, each
stage of which waited for explicit approval. The gates and flags are not ceremony; they are
the oversight the 2024 evaluation showed was necessary, made concrete.
5.4 Toward Robot and Simulation Use
The database exists to feed the sensorium of Chapter 6. Its pathway records enumerate
the signal taxonomy the controller’s sensory channels implement: Ia afferents carrying
length and velocity, Ib afferents carrying force, II afferents carrying length, and contact
receptors marking the stance–swing boundary (Section 6.2); Table 5.1 summarizes what
the corpus establishes for each class. The pathway naming grammar maps directly onto
circuit edges, since a record’s afferent class fixes the signal encoding, its phase fixes the
gating, and its action fixes the sign, so translating a pathway record into a controller
connection is a lookup rather than a literature search; Table 5.2 lists the principal pathway
records with the papers that demonstrate them. The Models table carries the lineage
the controller instantiates, from the two-level rhythm-generator and pattern-formation
architecture [Rybak et al., 2006] through muscle-reflex walking [Geyer and Herr, 2010] to

PDF PAGE 165
142
the two-layer neuromechanical walker the laboratory’s controller descends from [Deng
et al., 2019], and the 258 robot-and-simulation translation notes exist precisely to shorten
the step from paper to wiring decision. Figure 5.3 shows the two-dimension count the
explorer’s pivotable tables produce over the retained corpus, the afferent classes against
the animal preparations: the classical cat preparation carries the largest share of the Ia, Ib,
II, and cutaneous records, and the arthropod records enter through the mechanosensory
class.

The 39 pathway records extend beyond the mammalian core of Table 5.2: paired
excitation and inhibition records cover the chordotonal organs and trochanteral hair plates
of insect legs, a load-feedback record covers the campaniform sensilla, and further records
hold fictive locomotion without sensory feedback (12 linked papers), group III/IV fatigue,
and one paper each for somatosensory, vestibular, and visual contributions to postural
control. The controller of Chapter 6 implements the mammalian subset.
The corpus also reaches ahead to the sensorium extensions planned in Section 7.2.5. The
human balance and posture cluster and the multisensory recalibration cluster are the entry
points for the vestibular and ocular feedback work, already curated and already connected
to the locomotor core through the citation graph. The knowledge base makes this reach
practical for new students as well: the cluster, pathway, and animal pages compress a
literature that took months to curate into an onboarding document measured in pages,
and the animal pages let an experimentalist start from a preparation rather than a topic.
The honest accounting is that the resource is deeper than it is broad in places. The
afferent classification covers 86 of 561 retained papers, concentrated in the pathway-rich
core rather than spread across the corpus, and the flagged vocabulary gaps (vestibular
and visual preparations among them) mark where the classification must grow before
the extensions of Section 7.2.5 can be wired from the database alone. The growth policy
follows from the prune: additions should be substantive records or clearly flagged stub
twins, never bare rows, so that the corpus that the controller work, the next experiments,
and the next students inherit stays as searchable as the one this chapter documents.

PDF PAGE 169
146
6 Synthetic Nervous System Simulation
The preceding chapters establish how braided pneumatic actuator force and knee
torque can be predicted. To study how neural control interacts with these mechanics, I also
needed a simulation environment that connects anatomically organized spinal circuits to
the robot’s muscle routing and BPA force model. The existing tools reviewed in Chapter 2
(Section 2.9) provide the neural, biomechanical, and physics components separately; using
them for this robot required implemented interfaces and tests of the behavior preserved
through conversion. The gap this chapter fills is that integration: no existing environment
coupled an anatomically organized two-level controller, with Ia, Ib, group II, and contact
feedback, to a BPA-actuated robot model with validated actuator forces.
In this chapter I develop and evaluate those interfaces in MuJoCo with SNS-Toolbox
and in a graphical Simulink workflow, and I verify them rather than assume them: a
controller ported between environments cannot be presumed equivalent, so each port is
checked against its source implementation and, where the lineage’s archived models allow,
against the published behavior of the models it descends from. The complementary roles
of the environments reflect the complementary needs of the program, fast scripted closed-
loop simulation for tuning and evaluation, graphical circuit construction for assembly and
inspection, and CAD-derived mechanics for the physical assemblies; Section 6.2.2 states
which capability each environment was missing before this work. I also examine whether
selected behaviors persist when graded neural components are replaced with spiking

PDF PAGE 170
147
components. The contribution is the integration and its verification, with the distinction
from existing Simulink libraries established in Section 6.2.2 by direct comparison. The
neural vocabulary, the two-level central pattern generator with rhythm-generator and
pattern-formation layers, the graded neuron and synapse models, and the afferent channels,
is defined in Sections 2.5–2.8 and used here without redefinition.
The demonstrations establish specific component and closed-loop capabilities, and they
identify failures of behavior transfer and limits of the assembled models with equal weight.
Supported stepping and progression are distinguished from unsupported walking, which
has not been demonstrated. Section 6.1 records the AnimatLab walker whose hand-tuning
motivated this verification-first procedure: manual tuning left the circuits’ parameter
sensitivity unmeasured, so the present procedure records performance under parameter
and input perturbations instead. Section 6.2 is the methods section, Section 6.3 the results,
Section 6.4 the spiking mirrors, and Section 6.5 the discussion.
6.1 The AnimatLab Bipedal Walker
As preliminary work of this dissertation, built before the simulation toolchain of this
chapter existed, the author and Morrow constructed a neuromechanical model of a bipedal
walker in AnimatLab, a graphical environment for building biomechanical systems actu-
ated by synthetic neural systems [Cofer et al., 2010]. That model is the direct ancestor of
the work reported here: its biomechanics and its two-layer controller architecture carry
through every model in this chapter, and the limitations of its hand-tuned parameters are
the reason the present toolchain emphasizes systematic verification.
The biomechanical model consisted of nine rigid bodies (pelvis, thigh, shank, foot,

PDF PAGE 171
148
and toe segments) connected by sagittal-plane hinge joints with human-like rotational
capabilities and actuated by antagonistic pairs of Hill-type muscles at the hip, knee, and
ankle. The toe joint, the author’s addition to the Li design, carried a passive spring instead
of a muscle pair, simplifying the system. The mechanics followed the suspended bipedal
walking model of Li et al. [2017], whose pattern generators were entrained by heel and
toe mechanosensory receptors and hip-angle position sensors standing in for the group-I
loading, cutaneous, and hip-joint afferents of the biological scheme; muscle afferents were
not modeled. The Li model also required support against falling: a system with only
planar joints cannot actively balance side to side.
The synthetic nervous network was built from non-spiking, leaky-integrator neurons
and followed the two-layer CPG organization of Deng et al. [2019]. A rhythm-generator
layer set the timing of the system; a pattern-formation layer reshaped the timing sig-
nals into phase-appropriate activation of antagonist muscle groups; and a sensorimotor
layer containing Renshaw-cell-like recurrent inhibition (motor-neuron collaterals exciting
inhibitory cells that feed back onto the same pool) drove the motor neurons. The sensori-
motor layer received Ia (length and velocity) and Ib (tension) afferent feedback from the
muscles, but the feedback gains were never systematically tuned, and no ground-contact
signals were modeled. Every applied current, synaptic weight, and muscle-tension param-
eter was set by hand: each was adjusted by trial and error, one at a time, until the three
layers oscillated in phase and the motor neurons produced stepping-like activation of the
muscle pairs. That hand-tuning consumed the bulk of the project’s effort, left no record of
the sensitivity of the behavior to individual parameters, and did not generalize when new
feedback pathways were added; those limitations directly motivated the verification-first

PDF PAGE 172
149
approach of the remainder of this chapter. Alternating, stepping-like activity and motion
were demonstrated with the walker suspended in the air, and the results were presented
at the 2021 NIH BRAIN Initiative Investigators Meeting [Bolen and Hunt, 2021].
6.2 Methods: A Neuromechanical Simulation Toolchain
This section documents the laboratory in build order: the existing tools it stands on, the
new tools built from them, each new tool’s construction and fundamental demonstration,
and the methodology by which the controllers are tuned and checked. Figure 6.1 diagrams
the flow the toolchain implements, from model conversion through closed-loop simulation
to the embedded hardware stack of Chapter 7. Quantitative results for every fundamental
demonstration, the replications of published studies, and the experiments the tools enable
follow in Sections 6.3 and 6.4.
6.2.1 Existing Tools and Data Resources
OpenSim supplies the musculoskeletal modeling side, the robot’s source model, and
the moment-arm and torque standards the conversions are audited against [Delp et al.,
2007, Seth et al., 2018]. MuJoCo supplies the physics engine [Todorov et al., 2012], and
MyoConverter performs the base OpenSim-to-MuJoCo conversion [Caggiano et al., 2022,
Wang et al., 2022]. SNS-Toolbox builds and integrates the spinal networks [Nourse et al.,
2023]. AnimatLab hosts the controller lineage’s archived reference models and remains the
graphical prototyping environment [Cofer et al., 2010]. Simulink and Simscape Multibody
supply the controls-engineering environment into which the SNS component models and

PDF PAGE 174
151
the CAD-derived bodies are ported. SCONE provides the reference point for mature
predictive neuromuscular simulation [Geijtenbeek, 2019]: its published balance and gait
demonstrations were reproduced headless as a practice baseline, and the standing-balance
stage of the training curriculum (Section 6.5.2) is modeled on its balance tutorial.
The SADb of Chapter 5 is the data resource among the existing tools [Bolen et al., 2023].
Its segments are the five-table Airtable base (Papers, Feedback, Models, Review Papers,
and Recorders), the laboratory’s Zotero libraries that feed it, citation mining in the Research
Rabbit style that expanded it, and the laboratory’s own packaged explorer application that
distributes it. The SADb framed this chapter’s benchmarks and validation measurements:
its pathway records fix what each afferent class encodes and what each connection rule
does, so the sensory channels, the reflex topologies, and the acceptance phenomena against
which the controllers are validated (the deafferentation and stimulation results cited in
Section 6.3.6) are looked up rather than re-derived from the primary literature.
6.2.2 New Tools
Four tool families are new, and each exists because a needed combination did not. First,
the full-lower-body pipeline: the robot’s OpenSim musculoskeletal model converted
to MuJoCo, audited against its source, and coupled to an SNS-Toolbox spinal network
through a BPA force interface, with proprioceptive and heel and toe contact feedback
closing the loop. Second, the SNS-Simscape library: the neuron, synapse, afferent-encoder,
and actuator models of the SNS formalism as masked Simulink blocks, together with the
imported SolidWorks and OpenSim bodies that give them real geometry to control. Third,

PDF PAGE 175
152
the controller ports: the Deng-organization two-level network, the contact-driven W2L
core, and the Li walker re-implemented across NumPy, Simulink, MuJoCo, and Simscape
Multibody and onto the converted models, so that the same controller runs under different
realizations in different programs. Fourth, the verification instruments: the cross-engine
harness, the circuit-structure audits, and the spiking-neuron mirrors that test whether
conclusions depend on the graded-coding assumption.
Because a result that holds in only one software implementation is difficult to trust, the
same contact-driven rhythm core was re-implemented in Simulink and checked against
its NumPy reference, the archived AnimatLab models of the same lineage were re-run
headless so that engine-level and network-level agreement could be separated, and spiking-
neuron mirrors of all three simulation families test the coding assumption. The justification
for every component is the experimental capability it adds, and the standard of proof for
each is the fundamental demonstration that shows it works: a basic reflex pathway, the
replication of a published rhythm or gait, or the removal of a single pathway class.
6.2.3 AnimatLab Body and Controller Organization
The walker representation and controller hierarchy are shown in Figures 6.2 and 6.4. The
hierarchy separates rhythm generation, hip control, and knee–ankle pattern formation
within left- and right-side neural subsystems. As a tool, this environment exists because
the program’s controller lineage is archived in it: the reference models whose replication
is reported in Section 6.3 live as AnimatLab projects, and new circuit organizations are
prototyped in the same format. The current AnimatLab working model was instrumented

PDF PAGE 176
153
for a new 10 s phase-1 run recording joint, neural, contact-sensory, and body-motion
channels; that instrumented run is the tool’s fundamental demonstration, and its recordings
are reported in Section 6.3 and examined there without treating them as evidence of stable
walking or effective ground-contact gating. The run is distinct from the earlier walker
study of Section 6.1.
Since that run, the working model has been reorganized into a complete bilateral net-
work. The right-side rhythm-generator, hip, and knee–ankle subsystems were mirrored
from the left, inhibitory commissural pathways were added between the two rhythm
generators, and each leg gained four biarticular muscle pathways (gastrocnemius, biceps
femoris short head, semimembranosus, and rectus femoris) with human maximum iso-
metric forces from the Gait2392 data set. The reorganized project opens in the AnimatLab
environment without errors and exports a standalone simulation that runs headless. Its
rhythm-generator populations hold a static latch under the tonic drive levels tested so far
rather than oscillating, so conductance tuning toward robust oscillation is the active step;
no locomotor claim is made for the reorganized model beyond its verified assembly.
Figure 6.3 illustrates the rhythm-generator, pattern-formation, and sensorimotor or-
ganization at the neuron and synapse level. These reference schematics complement the
subsystem view by making the antagonist pathways and sensory connections visible.
6.2.4 MuJoCo Conversion and Synthetic BPA Knee
The next two tools operate in the MuJoCo engine: a conversion pipeline that moves the
robot’s musculoskeletal model from OpenSim into MuJoCo [Delp et al., 2007, Todorov

PDF PAGE 180
157
et al., 2012], and a force interface that makes the converted model’s actuators behave as
the BPAs of Chapter 3 rather than as generic muscles. The conversion exists because no
physics engine carried the robot’s custom muscle routing natively. The MyoConverter
program performs the base conversion [Caggiano et al., 2022, Wang et al., 2022]; because an
automatic conversion of a model with custom muscle routing introduces specific defects,
among them muscle paths that are not mirrored correctly between the two legs and joint
rotation axes with reversed sign, the conversion is followed by repair scripts, short Python
programs that correct each defect and re-audit the converted model against the OpenSim
source. The conversion runs in a recorded software environment, with the engine and
library versions fixed, so that it can be re-run from the source model and reproduced. The
force interface implements the quasi-static BPA force model of Chapter 3 in Python so that
it runs inside the simulation loop: at each time step it reads every actuator’s length and
extension rate from the MuJoCo tendon paths and returns the actuator force, including
the correction-factor force balance of Chapter 4. The fundamental demonstration of the
pair is a synthetic knee: a minimal SNS with reciprocal inhibition driving a synthetic
knee with antagonistic flexor and extensor BPAs (Figure 6.5, which distinguishes the
converted robot from this separate knee demonstration). The demonstration establishes
the controller–actuator–joint interface; it does not establish SNS control of the converted
robot or validated correction-factor torque predictions in MuJoCo.
The conversion and the force interface are complete and verified. The converted model
carries all 92 muscle-tendon actuators, with attachment geometry audited against the
OpenSim source, and the joint sign conventions of the robot’s modified muscle paths were
verified dynamically: each actuator was activated on its own in simulation, and the sign of

PDF PAGE 181
158
the joint torque it produced identified its action at that joint. The check has to be dynamic
because the attachment points of these paths move with joint configuration, so the routing
cannot be read from a fixed table of moment arms. The Python implementation reproduces
the MATLAB force calculation, including the correction-factor force balance. An SNS-
Toolbox network couples to MuJoCo at a 2ms neural step, matching the physics-engine
timestep, with muscle force feedback entering as Ib afferent input. On this coupling, a
410-neuron two-level spinal network has been assembled for the converted model (per-
leg rhythm-generator populations, four phase-shifted pattern-formation groups per leg,
motoneuron pools for all actuators, and Ia, Ib, and II afferent populations), and its rhythm
generator has been verified in isolation to oscillate at an approximately 0.9 s cycle with the
two legs in antiphase under tonic drive. That isolated oscillation doubles as the rhythm-
generator-level stimulus test: tonic drive with no phasic input evokes the alternating
rhythm, the simulation-side analogue of the tonic central and sensory stimulation that
facilitates involuntary air-stepping in humans [Selionov et al., 2009].
The sensory channels that close the loop trace to the SADb of Chapter 5 [Bolen et al.,
2023]: the afferent classes the controller monitors, Ia carrying length and velocity, Ib
carrying force, and group II carrying length, together with the heel and toe contact receptors
that mark the stance–swing boundary, are the classes the database’s curated records
were assembled to cover, and the signal encoding and the connection rules each channel
implements follow from those records (Tables 5.1 and 5.2) rather than from ad hoc literature
searches at wiring time.
Full-body closed-loop co-simulation runs on this coupling: the four production walking
configurations of Section 6.3.5 complete 16 s ground evaluations with gait cycles detected

PDF PAGE 182
159
and scored. Early full-body runs developed non-finite leg-joint states during longer simu-
lations; those faults were traced to the converted model’s moving-attachment followers,
whose sampled range limits fought the coupling constraints that tie each follower to its
path point, and repairing those constraints resolved them. The ground evaluations run
under a simulated physical support harness, a body-weight-relief rig acting on the model’s
pelvis that mirrors the laboratory’s overhead support rig, and unsupported walking is not
among the claimed results (Section 6.5.5 states exactly what walks and what does not).
6.2.5 Simulink SNS Library and Reflex Topology
The Simulink tool is a block library, built so that synthetic nervous systems can also be
assembled and inspected in a standard controls-engineering environment. Its seven core
component blocks are a non-spiking (graded) neuron, a graded chemical synapse, a spiking
leaky integrate-and-fire neuron, Ia and Ib afferent encoders, muscle activation dynamics,
and a generic BPA force block. The blocks implement the same neuron and synapse
equations used by the SNS-Toolbox networks of the preceding subsection, and their
inventory is drawn in Figure 6.6 (top), which also shows a functional schematic of the non-
spiking neuron implementation (bottom). The full library adds a synapse-sum junction
for neurons receiving more than six synaptic inputs, three diameter-specific BPA force
elements (10 mm, 20 mm and 40 mm) implementing the fitted force surfaces of Chapter 3,
and a Hill-type biological muscle element, for twelve blocks before the spiking additions of
Section 6.2.8. The library and its demonstrations run in base Simulink without additional
toolboxes; the block icons derive their excitatory and inhibitory symbols automatically

PDF PAGE 183
160

 (B) Synthetic BPA knee

and extensor tendon routes; transparency exposes the routes through the view. The models
are not shown to the same scale. These views document model construction, not SNS

from the sign of each synapse, in a color-blind-safe palette, and the publication figures
regenerate from scripts.
The library’s fundamental demonstrations are two basic reflex pathways. In the first,
the core blocks are assembled into a reduced-order knee-reflex model with antagonist
actuation (Figure 6.7): Ia stretch feedback excites the motor neuron of the stretched muscle
and inhibits that of its antagonist (reciprocal inhibition), while Ib force feedback inhibits the
motor neuron of the muscle that generated the force (autogenic inhibition). In the second,
an arm holds a beverage container while a pour loads it, with and without the stretch
reflex enabled. Between them, the two demonstrations show the library reproducing the
canonical spinal reflex pathways at the scale where their mechanisms are visible; their
quantitative results are reported in Section 6.3.2 beside their spiking twins. To couple the
controller to detailed mechanical CAD, the SolidWorks assembly is exported and imported
into Simscape Multibody. The URDF import path is verified on a trial knee assembly, and
the fuller Multibody-Link XML import of the knee test assembly exposes a documented
defect: the exporter drops the SolidWorks hinge mates as unsupported, so the knee degree
of freedom arrives welded, and the defined fix is to re-mate each hinge as a concentric
and coincident pair before re-export. The tendon hardware is modeled in CAD but not
yet inserted into the assembly. The demonstration model retains provisional mechanical
parameters, the generic BPA force block remains a placeholder, and the saved runs are
therefore not used to establish quantitative knee-regulation performance.

6.2.6 Controller and Body Ports into Simulink and Simscape Multibody
Making the controllers and bodies portable is the third new-tool family, and it exists
because the program’s models must run in more than one program. On the controller side,
the Deng two-layer CPG was rebuilt as separate editable Simulink files, rhythm-generator
and pattern-formation layers apart, with the published parameter tables carried over
[Deng et al., 2019, Nourse et al., 2023]. One cell-level choice in that rebuild proved load-
bearing for portability, a burst-termination property of the rhythm-generator cells whose
membrane mechanism is recorded with the neuron equations in Appendix B; Section 6.5.1
returns to why it matters. The tuned production spinal network is exported in the other
direction: a script turns the tuned network definition into an editable Simulink model built
from the library blocks of the preceding subsection, one block per neuron and one per
synapse, with every value living in an editable block mask so that the model regenerates
whenever the tuning changes.
On the body side, two import routes bring real geometry into Simscape Multibody.
The SolidWorks knee assembly travels the URDF and Multibody-Link XML routes of
the preceding subsection. The OpenSim gait2392 simbody model travels OpenSim to
MuJoCo to URDF to Simscape Multibody, with the massless muscle path-point slides
preserved as links so that actuators can attach at the same physical points as in the source
model; a URDF carries no muscles, so actuation re-enters as SNS library blocks driving
the imported joints. Finally, a MuJoCo–Simulink bridge block set, rebuilt against the
same engine version as the conversion, closes the loop from the Simulink side: muscle
activation commands in, joint and actuator sensors out, with the model’s saved reference

PDF PAGE 188
165
pose applied at initialization.
6.2.7 Cross-Platform Verification of the Contact-Driven Rhythm Core
The verification harness, in the software-testing sense of a fixed script suite that re-runs
and checks the models, is a tool in its own right, and its existence is the chapter’s answer
to a trust problem: the toolchain spans several simulation engines, and a result that holds
in only one software implementation is difficult to trust. Its fundamental demonstration is
replication, in two forms; the same rhythm core rebuilt in two engines must reproduce the
same rhythm, and the lineage’s archived models must reproduce their published behavior
in their original engine. The harness is defined around the contact-driven rhythm core of
the Walker-2-Layer (W2L) bipedal architecture: four rhythm-generator populations, one
extensor and one flexor population per leg, plus graded pattern-formation and sensory
cells, driven by heel-contact mechanosensory feedback. The rhythm cells are spiking
neurons that self-terminate each burst, so the mutually inhibiting extensor and flexor
populations of a pair alternate [Deng et al., 2019]; the burst-termination mechanism is
recorded with the neuron equations in Appendix B. The same core was implemented twice
from a common connectivity specification, once as a NumPy reference integration through
the SNS-Toolbox backend and once as native Simulink blocks from the library of Section 6.2,
and both were driven with an identical protocol: a 12 s run at a 2 ms neural step, tonic
drive with a brief kickoff pulse, and alternating 1 Hz heel-contact trains. Agreement was
scored on rhythm-generator burst period, left–right antiphase correlation, burst counts,
and full-trace correlation against the NumPy reference. The archived AnimatLab reference

PDF PAGE 189
166
models (the bilateral contact-driven model, the modern W2L model, and the Li walker)
were additionally re-run headless from their standalone simulators with instrumented
charts, so that engine-level agreement and network-level agreement could be separated.
The protocol covers the rhythm core and the archived models only; full-network and
whole-body behavior are reported as limitations alongside the results in Section 6.3.
6.2.8 Spiking-Neuron Mirrors of the Simulation Models
The final tool tests an assumption rather than adding a simulation capability. The con-
trollers documented above all compute with graded (non-spiking) neurons, in which the
membrane potential itself is the analog activity variable that drives the musculoskeletal
model. To test that the conclusions drawn from those models are not artifacts of graded
coding, spiking-neuron mirrors of all three simulation families were built, following one
design rule: the motor-neuron layer stays non-spiking, because the musculoskeletal model
consumes a continuous activation command, while the afferent encoders (the neurons
that convert sensor signals into synaptic input), rhythm-generator populations, pattern-
formation cells, interneurons, and commissural (cross-side) cells become leaky integrate-
and-fire neurons operating at real membrane-potential ranges, so that the network carries
spikes above the motor-neuron layer and analog voltages below it [Szczecinski et al., 2023,
Nourse et al., 2023]. Event-based coding is expected to matter most in the afferent and
rhythm layers, where mechanosensor onsets and phase transitions are discrete events, and
least at the motor-neuron output, which the muscle activation dynamics low-pass filter
anyway.

PDF PAGE 190
167
The MuJoCo–SNS-Toolbox mirror ( build network spiking.py) reproduces the pro-
duction spinal network of Section 6.2 population for population and connection for con-
nection, and is selected at build time by a single environment-variable switch so that
the default build path is untouched; that the non-spiking pipeline is unchanged was
proven by re-running its defaults gate and baseline evaluation to bit-exact agreement.
Because SNS-Toolbox 1.5.2 ships no spiking bursting-neuron class, the rhythm-generator
populations are adapting integrate-and-fire cells: burst termination is carried by spike-
frequency adaptation of the firing threshold, whose time constant reuses the corresponding
slow-inactivation value of the non-spiking build so that tuned adaptation values transfer
between the two. Spiking cells rest at ´70 mV, spike at ´50 mV, and reset to ´60 mV, with
a tonic bias current parking their equilibrium a few millivolts below threshold, the analog
cells’ near-threshold operating point. The network sub-steps at 0.5 ms (four sub-steps
inside each 2ms physics step) so that spike timing is resolved without changing the physics
integration, and non-spiking readout taps are appended for the runner’s stance gates and
neural logging so that all existing analysis code reads analog levels unchanged.
The mirror’s synaptic weights are set by a calibration script rather than by hand. Spikes
onto the non-spiking motor neurons are calibrated by mean conductance, so that a steady
presynaptic train reproduces the non-spiking steady-state motor-neuron voltage (calibra-
tion residuals at or below 3 ˆ 10´4 mV); synapses between spiking cells are calibrated by
firing-rate transfer (a 40 Hz source drives its target at 40 Hz, and a calibrated inhibitory
synapse silences a 40 Hz-driven cell); the graded-to-spiking command pathway is scaled
from the measured afferent current-to-rate map. The full calibration, with the measured
residual of every factor, is tabulated in Appendix B (Table B3).

PDF PAGE 191
168
In the Simulink family, three blocks were added additively to the SNS library of
Section 6.2 (for fifteen blocks in total): a SpikingNeuron with the same internal synaptic
summation as the non-spiking block, an event-wired SpikingSynapse whose conductance
jumps by a fixed increment per presynaptic spike and saturates at its maximum, and a
HybridSpikingSynapse that carries the same conductance dynamics onto a non-spiking
postsynaptic cell. The blocks were verified against the analytic single-spike conductance
decay (maximum deviation 6.2 ˆ 10´7 µS), against a verbatim re-implementation of the
SNS-Toolbox update equations on a hybrid two-neuron circuit (identical spike count and
maximum spike-time deviation 11.75 µs), and against the SNS-Toolbox numpy backend
itself (mean interspike-interval difference 0.19 µs). Spiking twins of the knee-reflex and
beverage-holding demonstrations were then built by copy-and-patch: only the sensory
interneuron layer and its synapses are replaced by spiking cells, while the motor neurons,
muscles, joints, and measurements are identical to the committed demonstrations, and the
twin is run at an operating point matched to the baseline gains.
In the AnimatLab family, spiking copies of the runnable standalone exports were
produced by a conversion script that rewrites copies of the model files, leaving every
original file untouched: neuron thresholds are moved from the graded regime (unreachably
positive) into the spiking regime, every graded chemical synapse type is re-emitted as
a per-spike synapse with the same identity and amplitude (interpreted as a per-spike
conductance increment with 10ms decay), and the per-connection class tag that AnimatLab
stores independently of the synapse type is flipped to match. A calibrated variant sets each
cell class’s threshold just below its measured graded peak, and an ablation that converts
the thresholds but keeps the graded synapses isolates which change does what. Each copy

PDF PAGE 192
169
passes a structural validator (neuron, connection, synapse-type, chart, and stimulus sets
identical to its original, and every class tag consistent) and runs headless to its configured
end. The 2023 spiking walker model of the same lineage, which was tuned as a rate code
from birth, serves as the spiking reference baseline for this family.
Each mirror is verified against its non-spiking twin by a fixed gate set rather than by
inspection: a topology gate requires the connection multiset to be identical between the two
builds under three gain configurations; a rhythm gate requires self-sustained alternating
rhythm under constant drive with the period within 20 % of the analog build; a basin gate
requires the rhythm to persist under 1 % multiplicative jitter on every scalar parameter;
an air-regime smoke gate compares cadence, duty, and joint ranges of the deafferented
suspended-air preparation; and a regression gate requires every committed non-spiking
artifact to reproduce its recorded values exactly with the spiking blocks present in the
library. The mirrors carry mapped parameters only, with no new tuning, so their results
(Section 6.4) establish what transfers structurally and what requires re-tuning, and any
fresh tuning effort is future work.
6.2.9 T uning, Robustness, and Reference Data
Above the physiologically set values, the free gains of each network are tuned by systematic
search rather than by hand: a sequential model-based optimizer (a tree-structured Parzen
estimator) proposes gain sets, each proposal is scored by a full simulation, and each stage’s
winner seeds the next. The design rule that matters here is that no hand-tuned parameter
enters a production configuration; the failure mode of the prior walker (Section 6.1) was

PDF PAGE 193
170
precisely hand tuning that left no record of sensitivity. The stage structure and the scoring
philosophy are the subject of Section 6.5.2.
Robustness is enforced during tuning rather than inspected afterward. Finalist configu-
rations must retain their rhythm under 1 % multiplicative jitter on every scalar parameter
(the basin gate of Section 6.2.8 applies the same test to the mirrors), survive stepping
of the tonic drive about the tuned value, and, for standing configurations, absorb push
perturbations without falling. The requirement exists because the tuned networks sit near
bifurcations of their dynamics, a property this chapter makes explicit in its cross-engine
and spiking results: a winner whose basin is narrow does not survive a solver change, let
alone a hardware transfer.
The reference data for tuning and evaluation are inverse-kinematics cycles from many
human subjects of different sizes and speeds rather than a single subject: a 43-cycle
locomotion reference library, detailed in Section 6.3.5, comprising running trials from
eight subjects at four speeds, walking references at prescribed speeds from 0.5 m s´1 to
2.0 m s´1 plus self-selected trials, and one predicted-walking cycle. Working against many
references penalizes tuning that has memorized one subject’s kinematics, in the same spirit
as the perturbation gates. Circuit structure is treated as data under the same methodology:
every network’s connection multiset is compared programmatically against its intended
topology (the topology gates), and the publication schematics are generated from the
compiled network with each drawn edge asserted against the compiled connection list, so
the drawings cannot drift from the circuits they document.

PDF PAGE 194
171
6.3 Results: Tool Demonstrations and Experiments
This section reports what the tools do, ordered by the questions that justify them. The
AnimatLab instrumented run demonstrates the prototyping environment’s recording
capability. The Simulink library demonstrations and the cross-program ports establish
the new Simulink-side tools at their fundamental scale. The cross-engine verification
and the headless reruns replicate known behavior: the rhythm core must reproduce itself
across engines, and the archived models must reproduce their published rhythms and
gaits. The gait-validation sweep then measures how far the tuned networks remain from
human reference locomotion, and the final subsection presents the pathway-removal and
drive-perturbation experiments as the laboratory’s deletion and stimulus experiments.
The BPA-actuated walker that demonstrates the full MuJoCo chain end to end is discussed
in Section 6.5.4, where the state of the walking program is consolidated.
6.3.1 AnimatLab Phase-1 Recordings
The phase-1 run described in Section 6.2 is the AnimatLab tool’s fundamental demonstra-
tion: it exercises the instrumented model and the recording chain end to end. The run
produced synchronized joint, neural, contact-sensory, and body-motion recordings over
10 s; basic checks confirmed finite values and a uniform 0.2ms sampling interval in both
exported charts, each containing 50,001 samples from 0 through 10 s with nine trailing
zero-filled rows beyond the configured chart end excluded. Figure 6.8 shows bilateral hip,
knee, and ankle angles. The contact-sensory channels record membrane voltage rather
than contact force, and the model included an externally applied horizontal force during

PDF PAGE 195
172
the first second. The run demonstrates signal acquisition and alternating activity, not
validated locomotor performance: neither stable walking nor effective ground-contact
gating was established. The neural and contact-sensory recordings remain archived with
the run.
6.3.2 Simulink Library Demonstrations and Cross-Program Ports
The SNS-Simscape library demonstrates the canonical reflex pathways at the scale where
their mechanisms are visible. In the knee-reflex demonstration, the joint reads 15.79 ˝
at 0.05 s and settles at a mean of 43.5˝ over the last 2 s, with a mild irregular antagonist
alternation about the mean. In the beverage-holding demonstration, the stretch reflex
limits the maximum sag of the loaded arm to 2.00˝ against 7.85˝ with the reflex disabled;
the committed values are tabulated beside their spiking twins in Table 6.5, Section 6.4.
A third demonstration closes the loop onto the actuator model itself: a two-population
CPG with burst-terminating adaptation drives the antagonist BPA pair of the one-degree-
of-freedom knee through the library’s diameter-specific force blocks, sweeping the joint
through 9.7˝ to 48.0˝.
The controller ports verify at the same standard. The Deng rhythm-generator and
pattern-formation files, driven by a single 10 nA pulse of 20 ms, oscillate continuously at a
1.938 s period against the 1.94 s NumPy reference, with the extensor and flexor populations
anticorrelated at r=´ 0.86. The exported full-network Simulink model reproduces its
NumPy reference to a maximum deviation of 4.2 ˆ 10´6 mV across all 410 neurons at
0.3 s; beyond roughly 0.4 s the network’s chaos makes any two integrators diverge in

phase (Section 6.5.1), so the verification window is the early transient and the faithful
reproduction is the fixed-step Euler run at the production step size.
The MuJoCo–Simulink bridge was proven against an independent Python integration
of the same engine version: single-muscle activation, two-rate control, and sensor-readback
tests all pass, the readback bit-exact over the scored window. On the bridge, a single-muscle
Ia/II/Ib reflex loop on the converted model’s vastus medialis bounds the consequences of
a postural collapse: without the reflex, the falling model drives transient muscle forces
past 40 kN before the solver fails, and with it, the same loop holds muscle force at 469 N
while the stretch response recruits the muscle. The full exported network then ran as a
Simulink model block driving all 92 muscle actuators of the converted model, completing
a 5 s closed-loop run with all drives finite and 96 % of the muscles recruited. That run also
isolated a real property: under tonic drive alone, the Simulink realization settles to the
tonic fixed point while the NumPy reference oscillates, the near-bifurcation behavior that
Section 6.5.1 treats at length, and the production rhythm requires the full input schedule
rather than drive alone.
The OpenSim-to-Simscape import completes the Simulink-side tool set. Thegait2392 simbody
model arrives in Simscape Multibody as a mechanism of 1,446 blocks and 149 joints, of
which 20 are the anatomical revolute joints, 85 are the massless path-point slides that carry
the muscle attachment points, and 44 are the fixed-link offset chains the conversion route
requires; the imported mechanism compiles and simulates. What the route does not carry
are the muscles themselves, which re-enter as SNS library blocks driving the imported
joints, the division of labor the chapter’s architecture assumes throughout.

PDF PAGE 198
175
6.3.3 Cross-Engine Verification of the Contact-Driven Rhythm Core
Between the NumPy reference and the native Simulink reimplementation of the W2L
rhythm core, both legs’ rhythm-generator burst periods matched at 1.000 s versus 1.000 s
(0.0% error), the left–right antiphase correlation reproduced the reference exactly ( r=
´0.525), and all ten expected bursts occurred in the scored window. The full voltage traces
cross-correlate at r= 0.99996–0.99998 at a ´2 ms lag with an RMSE of 0.099–0.111 mV
(Figure 6.9); the residual lag is consistent with the one-step delay of the discrete Simulink
solver.
The same protocol also runs the chapter’s first deletion experiment, and it surfaced
an architectural fact rather than a defect: with the heel-contact trains removed, the full
network holds a static latch under tonic drive with kickoff alone, in every implementation,
so phasic contact feedback is part of the drive architecture of the W2L design, not an
optional perturbation. The result is a load dependence that echoes the decerebrate-cat
locomotion reviewed in Section 2.5, and it is the reason the pathway-removal experiments
of Section 6.3.6 treat the contact channels as a pathway class rather than as a boundary
condition.
6.3.4 Headless Validation of the AnimatLab Reference Models
The second half of the verification suite’s fundamental demonstration is replication of
published results in the original engine. The archived AnimatLab reference models were
re-run headless from their standalone simulators with instrumented charts, and eleven
targetable rhythm and gait metrics were extracted automatically; all eleven pass their

PDF PAGE 199
176
Figure 6.9: Left and right rhythm-generator extensor population voltages of the W2L
contact-driven rhythm core under the shared verification protocol, from the NumPy
reference (SNS-Toolbox backend) and the native Simulink reimplementation. Scored
metrics (burst period, left–right antiphase correlation, and burst counts) agree exactly, and
the full traces cross-correlate atrě0.99996 at a´2 ms lag.

PDF PAGE 200
177
acceptance bands. In the bilateral contact-driven model, the rhythm generators oscillate
at periods of 0.450 s (left) and 0.467 s (right) with a left–right lag of 0.529 cycle (cross-
correlation r= 0.976), confirming alternating gait timing. In the modern W2L model,
the joints oscillate at 2.25 Hz; its flexor populations peak at´56.4 mV and never cross the
´55 mV threshold, so the rhythm is carried by clean extensor/flexor antiphase (r=´ 0.996)
through the graded pathway rather than by flexor spiking. The Li-model rerun [Li et al.,
2017] walks at 0.637 m s´1 with right and left step periods of 1.299(24) s and 1.333(80) s
and duty factors of 0.443 and 0.555, reproducing the published behavior of the original
model in its original engine.
The replication series has a defined extension, and its current boundary is stated plainly.
The recent connectome-level circuit organizations of Shevtsova et al. [2016], Di Russo et al.
[2023], and Shinohara et al. [2025] are specified in this program as circuit-rule translations,
connection lists carrying per-edge citations, from which running models in SNS-Toolbox
and SNS-Simscape would be built. On the current record they have not been built or
verified, and no replication claim is made for them here; they are the next entries of the
series this section’s protocol defines.
6.3.5 Gait-Validation Sweep against the Locomotion Reference Library
As a scope check on the tuned networks rather than a demonstration of a tool, the four
production walking configurations of the converted robot-body model were evaluated
against a library of 43 human locomotion reference cycles: 32 running trials from eight
subjects at four speeds, ten walking references (seven at prescribed speeds from 0.5 m s´1 to

PDF PAGE 201
178
2.0 m s´1, plus three self-selected), and one further reference cycle, the predicted-walking
cycle of the Falisse predictive-simulation dataset ( Case 40 motion in the tables; period
1.11 s, duty factor 0.57). The four configurations span the two controller lineages of this
program: the human-derived production configuration (s3k) and its pruned derivative
(s3kpruned), the six-synergy variant ( syn6), whose pattern-formation layers drive the
motor pools through a six-synergy basis extracted from human gait data, and the W2L-
layout variant (w2lvar), which restructures the rhythm and pattern-formation layers after
the contact-driven W2L circuit of Section 6.2.7. Each was scored with the production
pipeline’s cycle-shape kinematic score, in which 0 denotes perfect agreement and ´25
is the fixed no-cycle fallback, both on its tuning-reference evaluation and against every
library cycle (Table 6.1).

But every configuration’s library scores are one to two orders of magnitude more negative

ten walking references alone ( ´8816) is slightly more negative than its running-only
mean (´8761). For three of the four configurations the most negative single library score
falls on the same reference, the predicted-walking cycle, while the W2L-layout variant’s
most negative reference is a running trial, subject08 Run 20002. All four configurations
produce right-leg-only or asymmetric gait in these evaluations; the family-level reading of
the ranking is taken up in Section 6.5.5.
6.3.6 Stimulus and Deletion Experiments on the Walker Networks
The locomotor physiology that this program’s circuits imitate was established with two
interventions, deletion and stimulation, and the laboratory’s first experiments apply the
same logic at the levels the two-level architecture defines. In the reference works, removing
proprioceptive feedback degrades locomotor patterns in mice [Akay et al., 2014], proprio-
ceptive input regulates and adapts locomotor activity [Lam and Pearson, 2002], and tonic
central and sensory stimulation facilitates involuntary air-stepping in humans [Selionov
et al., 2009]; the two-level CPG models themselves reproduce specific deletion phenomena,
such as spontaneous deletions of motoneuron activity during which the locomotor period
and phase are maintained [Rybak et al., 2006]. The experiments implemented here are
the simulator-side analogues that the assembled tools make inexpensive: removal of an

PDF PAGE 203
180
entire pathway class from a fully tuned walker, re-evaluated on the tuning reference, and
stepping of the tonic drive about its tuned value. They act at the rhythm-generator level
(tonic drive, interleg commissural pathways, and the weak rhythm-generator mutual
excitation), at the sensorimotor level (Renshaw cells), and at the afferent level (Ia, Ib,
group II, and contact channels). Systematic stimulation series at the pattern-formation
and motoneuron levels, and the motoneuron skipping-deletion phenomenon itself, are
defined as verification tests in Chapter 7 and have not yet been run; the experiments below
are therefore reported as pathway deletions and drive stimuli, labeled by the level at
which each acts. The published models do not carry these pathway-class interventions as
performed experiments, so fidelity for them rests on the receiving programs, which apply
the same class-level deletions and drive-stepping protocols to the tuned networks.
The pathway-removal sweep (Table 6.2) removes individual pathway classes from each
configuration and re-evaluates the tuning-reference score. In the production configuration,
removing the Ia afferent pathway freezes the model outright: the evaluation detects no
gait cycles and returns the fixed ´25 fallback. Removing the Renshaw cells, the group-
II afferents, or all afferent classes at once degrades the score to ´267.3, ´255.2, and
´267.8, respectively, against the full model’s´186.4, while removing the Ib, contact, or
interleg commissural channels individually shifts the score by smaller amounts toward
zero (´180.3, ´183.9, and ´174.9). The six-synergy variant behaves differently: with all
afferents removed it scores ´190.5 against its full model’s´200.4 and still walks bilaterally,
with ten cycles on each leg, a knee-flexion minimum of ´71.2˝, and a 0.684 s period, so
its afferent complement is not what sustains its gait. The W2L-layout variant depends on
both the Ia pathway (removed: ´273.5) and the weak rhythm-generator mutual excitation

PDF PAGE 204
181
(removed: ´279.9) against its full model’s´144.5 with four right-leg and six left-leg cycles,
while removing its Renshaw cells leaves a bilateral gait at´142.4.
Table 6.2: Pathway-removal sweep on the tuning-reference evaluation. The score is the
production pipeline’s cycle-shape kinematic score (0 denotes perfect agreement; ´25 is
the fixed no-cycle fallback, a failed evaluation that scores above every genuine gait in this
table). The full-model rows repeat each configuration’s unmodified production evaluation.
Configuration Removed pathway Score

Read against the reference physiology, the sweep’s structure is familiar even where
its details are specific to these networks. Deleting all afferent classes from the production
configuration degrades its gait without abolishing it, which is the qualitative outcome
of the deafferentation experiments [Akay et al., 2014]; the network that matches that
picture most closely is the six-synergy variant, which keeps a bilateral gait with every
afferent class removed. The Ia-only removal freezing the production configuration is a
stronger dependence than the animal experiments show, and it marks that network’s

PDF PAGE 205
182
tuning as built around its length feedback. The degradation under group-II removal is
consistent with proprioceptive regulation of the locomotor pattern [Lam and Pearson,
2002], and the equally large degradation under Renshaw-cell removal indicates that the
recurrent-inhibition loop carries weight in the tuned production network. No experiment
here reproduces the motoneuron-level deletion phenomena of the two-level CPG models
[Rybak et al., 2006]; the deletions act at the afferent, interneuron, and rhythm-generator
levels, and extending them to the pattern-formation and motoneuron levels is scheduled
with the verification tests of Chapter 7.
The drive-perturbation and push tests are the stimulus side. The production configura-
tion never fell in 15 stand and push evaluations (maximum push sway 0.123 m), and its
walk survives drive perturbations of+0.01, ´0.01, and ´0.02 (scores of ´201.9, ´177.0, and
´171.2), freezing only at +0.02; its pruned derivative survives the unperturbed evaluation
(´176.1) and the ´0.02 perturbation (´168.2). The six-synergy and W2L-layout variants fall
under every ˘0.01 and ˘0.02 drive perturbation tested, with pelvis tilt reaching 39˝ to 40˝.
In stimulus terms, a step change of the tonic drive within this range leaves the production
lineage walking while the two variant lineages fall. The sweep therefore identifies the
production configuration and its pruned derivative as the robust pair of the family, and
locates the fragility of the six-synergy and W2L-layout variants under drive perturbation.
6.4 Spiking-Neuron Mirror Results
The spiking-neuron mirrors of Section 6.2.8 were evaluated against their non-spiking twins
in the same order in which the families were introduced: the MuJoCo spinal network, the

PDF PAGE 206
183
Simulink demonstrations, and the AnimatLab walker family. All numbers in this section
come from the gated verification effort whose artifacts and reproduction commands are
catalogued in Appendix B.
6.4.1 MuJoCo Spinal-Network Mirror
The non-spiking production baselines were first re-established on the recorded software
environment: all three current production configurations reproduce their documented
evaluation scores exactly, including the default build’s kinematic score of ´161.57 (the
full-precision value is tabulated with the gate records in Appendix B), and after the
mirror’s selector was added the default build gate ((410, 376, 1186) populations, inputs,
and connections) and that same baseline score reproduce digit for digit, proving the
non-spiking pipeline untouched.
The topology gate passes exactly: under the default, fully tuned, and joint-layer pattern-
formation gain configurations, the spiking mirror’s directed connection multiset is identical
to the non-spiking build’s (1,186, 7,908, and 7,762 connections respectively), with identical
input lists, identical motor-neuron maps, and exactly the expected number of readout taps
(12, 12, and 16). The mirror is structurally the same network.
Under constant drive (network alone, 20 s), the rhythm gate splits. The spiking mirror
self-sustains clean bilateral antiphase, with rhythm-generator extensor and flexor cells
firing at 4.9 Hz and 3.2 Hz per leg and the swing readout sustained in every window, but
its envelope period is 0.621 s against the analog build’s 0.399 s, a 55.6 % lengthening that
misses the gate’s 20 % tolerance (Figure 6.10). The basin gate passes in full: the rhythm

PDF PAGE 207
184
persists under 1 % multiplicative jitter on every scalar parameter in all 12 seeded trials,
with a final-window swing minimum of 4.58 mV against the 5.13 mV baseline (margin
0.89ˆ) and no divergent trials. In the deafferented suspended-air preparation, the mirror
air-steps rhythmically with full-amplitude joint swings (knee range 106.0 ˝ against the
twin’s 117.9˝), but its cadence is roughly ten times faster than the non-spiking twin’s
0.47 Hz air-stepping regime in this preparation, so the air-regime gate fails on the cadence
clause alone.
One untuned ground-walking evaluation was run as a stretch attempt: the spiking
mirror at the non-spiking winner’s mapped parameters, with no re-tuning. It scores worse
overall (kinematic score ´236.35 against ´161.57), as expected for an untuned mapped
starting point, but it produces a genuine bilateral gait (eight right-leg and nine left-leg
cycles, a cycle-window knee flexion minimum of ´79.8˝, no non-finite states, and the
model stays up), whereas the non-spiking winner’s left leg is frozen (zero cycles) in every
recorded run of its study, including this session’s baseline (Figure 6.11, Table 6.3). This is a
single run with no statistics, reported as a lead for the fresh tuning effort that the mirror’s
plan calls for, not as an established gait result.

A representative rhythm-generator-to-motor-pool subnetwork demonstrates the hybrid
motif end to end: the spiking rhythm-generator populations drive, through a chain of
inhibitory interneurons, a non-spiking three-motor-neuron pool whose voltage stays
graded over a 5 mV depth (´52.0 to ´46.9 mV) and tracks its rhythm generator’s rate
envelope. Burst alternation in that subnetwork is partial: the extensor population bursts
four times in 12 s while the flexor population keeps a low-rate floor through the extensor
bursts. This incompleteness is reported as is, and the full 410-population Simulink spiking
conversion is scoped as future work for the same reason.
6.4.3 AnimatLab Spiking Conversions
The AnimatLab family produces the clearest negative result of the spiking comparison,
and it is reported as such. The graded baselines were re-established first: the two-layer
walker’s rhythm generator oscillates at 0.443 s (2.26Hz), the bilateral variant at 0.462 s with
the two legs folded near antiphase (lag ´0.42 cycle, r= 0.869), and the already-spiking
2023-lineage model, the family’s spiking reference, bursts at 1.305 s with folded antiphase
of+0.494 cycle (r=0.703), reproducing its documented period. The current reorganized
project’s standalone export remains latched under the tonic drives examined, consistent
with Section 6.2.
No spiking copy of the three graded models preserves the rhythm (Table 6.6). The two-
layer conversions die at every threshold and decay tried (two or zero rhythm-generator
spikes in 5 s); the bilateral variant latches with one side’s populations saturated at 442Hz–
450 Hz; and the reorganized project’s copy converts its latched baseline into strictly syn-

PDF PAGE 214
191
chronous 11.8 Hz co-bursting with no alternation: the left extensor and flexor populations
emit identical 58-spike trains whose spike-count envelopes correlate atr= 1.000 at zero lag.
The thresholds-only ablation, which keeps the graded synapses, preserves near-baseline
joint oscillations, isolating the synapse conversion as the change that breaks the rhythm
(Figure B1): per-spike conductances at the graded amplitude either starve the reciprocal
loop of inhibitory charge or saturate it, whereas the graded synapses read the membrane
potential continuously and keep oscillating through subthreshold voltages. A spiking
version of this family therefore needs its synaptic amplitudes and decays re-tuned as a
rate code, which is how the 2023 spiking reference was built in the first place, rather than
copied parameter by parameter.

converts the neurons but keeps the graded synapses.

6.5 Discussion
This section consolidates what the results mean for the program: the spiking comparison
first, because its conclusions constrain the rest; then the training curriculum and its scoring
behind the tuned configurations; then the integration of the BPA force model, the standing
of the robot models under their different controllers, and the BPA-actuated robot; and
finally the new tools themselves as the chapter’s contribution.
6.5.1 What Spiking Changes and Does Not
The spiking-mirror comparison (Sections 6.2.8 and 6.4) allows the dissertation’s neural-
coding assumption to be examined directly, and its outcome divides cleanly into what the
coding regime changes and what it leaves alone.
What spiking does not change is the behavior of the controlled model, provided the
design rule and the calibration hold. Because the motor-neuron layer stays non-spiking
and the spikes onto it are calibrated by mean conductance, the interface that the actuator
and torque models of this dissertation attach to, a continuous activation command per
muscle, is unchanged by construction. The Simulink twins make the point quantitatively:
with only the sensory interneuron layer converted, the knee-reflex transient is identical,
the settle band moves by 0.9˝, and the beverage-holding reflex modulation moves by 0.02˝.
To the musculoskeletal model, a calibrated 40Hz train and the graded voltage it replaces
are the same command; the activation dynamics of the muscle model filter the spike
train the same way the biological membrane would. The actuator-level and joint-level
conclusions of Chapters 3–4 therefore do not depend on the neural coding chosen above

PDF PAGE 216
193
the motor-neuron layer.
What spiking changes first is timing. The mirror’s self-sustained rhythm runs 55.6 %
slower than the analog build at the same constant drive, and its air-stepping cadence is
roughly ten times faster than the non-spiking twin’s in that preparation, deviations in
opposite directions in the two tests, which is itself informative, since a single scaling error
would move both the same way. The comparison traced the constant-drive period to two
identifiable knobs, the threshold-increment magnitude and the adaptation time constant,
and demonstrated alternation/period trade-offs along them; the untuned starting point,
not a structural failure, is the honest reading. Any period-matched spiking controller is
therefore a short tuning effort away, and the mirror’s mapped parameters are the correct
starting point for it.
What spiking changes second is robustness, in the mirror’s favor. The analog network’s
constant-drive rhythm sits close to a bifurcation, a parameter region where an exact re-
implementation in a different integration stack can settle to the tonic fixed point instead of
oscillating, so marginal winners risk not surviving a solver change. The spiking rhythm-
generator populations passed the perturbation basin gate in full, with the rhythm persisting
under 1 % jitter on every scalar parameter including the spiking-specific ones, at 0.89ˆ of
the baseline swing. For the hardware-transfer program of Chapter 7, where the network
will be re-implemented on embedded hardware with finite-precision arithmetic, a rhythm
whose basin is wide is the safer substrate, and the sub-stepping cost of resolving spikes
(0.5 ms network steps inside the unchanged 2 ms physics step) is modest.
What spiking does not tolerate is a naive parameter copy at the synapse level. The
AnimatLab conversions show that converting graded synaptic amplitudes to per-spike

PDF PAGE 217
194
increments of the same magnitude breaks the reciprocal rhythm-generator loop outright
(the loop is either starved of inhibitory charge or saturated), while converting only the
thresholds changes nothing, and the 2023 spiking walker of the same lineage walks because
it was tuned as a rate code from birth. Synaptic strength in a spiking network is a rate-
code quantity that must be derived from the presynaptic firing rate and the conductance
decay, exactly as the MuJoCo mirror’s calibration script derives it, not copied from the
graded value. This is the one place where the negative results should temper expectations:
spiking conversion is an exercise in re-derivation, and the calibration-first, gate-everything
procedure of Section 6.2.8 is the transferable method.
Finally, the one place where the untuned spiking mirror outperforms its tuned non-
spiking twin, producing a bilateral ground gait where the winner’s left leg is frozen,
is a single run with no statistics, and it is claimed only as a lead. But it points at the
mechanism the design rule was after: the mirror’s afferents are event-coded, its adaptation
is spike-history-based, and its inhibitory charge arrives in discrete quanta, so the phase
relationships that the analog build locks in around a frozen leg are not the only stable
operating regime of the same topology. Establishing whether that regime survives tuning,
and whether event-coded contact afferents are what unlock it, is precisely the closed-loop
CPG comparison program defined in Chapter 7, and the mirror is now the instrument for
running it.

PDF PAGE 218
195
6.5.2 The Five-Stage Training Curriculum and Its Scoring
The tuned configurations of this chapter were not tuned all at once. The training proceeds
through five stages (Figure 6.13), each adding one capability and each seeded with the
previous stage’s winner so that only the newly activated pathway gains are searched:
deafferented air stepping first, in which the rhythm populations’ oscillation is verified
with the legs suspended and every sensory channel silent; afferented air stepping second,
with heel, toe, and load feedback active in the same suspended preparation; standing
balance third, the stage modeled on the SCONE balance tutorial (Section 6.2.1) and scored
on maintaining posture under push perturbations; ground walking fourth; and ground
walking with the contact-driven pathway variant fifth. Standing before walking is the
design rule: gait tuning begins only after a configuration holds posture, and the failures
the curriculum surfaces early are the cheap ones.
The scoring is stage-appropriate and gated. The air stages are scored on rhythm and
swing depth with the objective rhythm-gated, so a configuration that freezes cannot win
by holding a static pose. The balance stage is scored on posture maintenance under push.
The walking stages use the production pipeline’s cycle-shape kinematic score against a
human reference cycle, in which 0 denotes perfect agreement and a fixed fallback marks
the absence of detectable gait cycles, a value that sits below every genuine gait so that a
frozen model cannot out-score a walker. Scores are never read alone: finalists must pass
the perturbation and basin requirements of Section 6.2.9, because a score achieved on a
narrow basin is a property of the solver rather than of the circuit, a lesson the cross-engine
results of this chapter turn into a gate.

PDF PAGE 219
196
No. Preparation Capability Score
1 Deafferented

the score is read.
