6.5.3 Integration of the BPA Force Model
The actuator model travels with the controller. Inside the MuJoCo loop, actuator force
comes from the quasi-static BPA model of Chapter 3, including the correction-factor force
balance of Chapter 4, rather than from a generic muscle approximation; in Simulink, the
library’s three diameter-specific force blocks implement the same fitted force surfaces,
so a circuit assembled there commands the same actuators as its MuJoCo counterpart.
The demonstrations that close the loop onto the actuator model, the synthetic knee of
Section 6.2, the BPA-CPG leg demonstration of Section 6.3.2, and the BPA-actuated robot
below, all draw their forces from this one validated source, which is what keeps actuator-
level conclusions consistent across engines. The BPA-actuated robot’s peak forces sit at
60% of the pressure ceiling, so the demonstrated gaits operate inside the characterized
pressure range rather than extrapolating past it. What the integration does not yet carry
is actuator dynamics: the force model is quasi-static, and the pressure–force dynamics
under pulse-modulated valve control [Elzein et al., 2025] enter the program as future work
(Chapter 7).
6.5.4 The BPA-Actuated Robot in MuJoCo
The end-to-end demonstration of the MuJoCo chain, conversion pipeline plus force in-
terface plus network builder, is the BPA-actuated robot, and it belongs in this discussion
because it is the tooling’s standing answer to the question the chapter opened with: what
the laboratory is for. The walker geometry of Section 6.1 was exported to MuJoCo and
driven by antagonistic BPA actuator pairs under the split contact-driven network, with

PDF PAGE 221
198
actuator forces computed by the BPA model of Chapter 3. In the air-stepping prepara-
tion, with the harness carrying 70% of the 411 N body weight, the walker produced clean
knee-flexion excursions at a 1.542 s period with a knee flexion range of 63.5˝, peak BPA
forces of 880 N (left) and 1372 N (right) at a peak pressure of 372kPa (60% of the 620 kPa
ceiling), and 7 and 5 heel-contact episodes on the left and right. This run demonstrates the
complete chain from the characterized BPA force model to a multi-joint walker stepping
under synthetic neural control, with actuator sizing operating inside the characterized
pressure range.
The same evaluation series reported its failures with equal weight. On the ground, the
W2L gate model under its untuned default gains stands (full-support stance) but does not
walk: heel duty is zero and forward displacement is +0.001 m over the run. With the left
and right drives separated and the feedback gains re-tuned, the same robot alternates in
antiphase (inter-leg phase 0.487) and progresses 1.72 m over a 30 s window (0.066 m s´1)
under the harness protocol, with the neural drive weaned to 60 % of its bootstrap value
and the result robust under perturbation (12 of 12 basin re-runs). Below 60 % drive the
gait dies, the harness itself is not weaned, and foot duty remains below the strict walking
gate, so the result is supported progression rather than validated walking. The MuJoCo
re-implementation of the Li model produced no stance episodes in any of seven drop-
protocol variants. Together with the latch result of Section 6.3.3, these negatives localize
the open problem precisely: the contact-driven rhythm architecture itself transfers across
engines intact, but translating it to unsupported ground locomotion requires the balance
and weight-bearing layers defined in Chapter 7, and those layers are not present in these
models.

PDF PAGE 222
199
6.5.5 Controller Comparison on the Robot Models and the Walking Status
The robot models carry more than one controller, and the comparison is a result of the
tooling rather than an afterthought. On the converted robot-body model, the four produc-
tion walking configurations span the controller lineages (Section 6.3.5), and scored with
the same cycle-shape kinematic score against the 43-reference library, the family-level fact
is that the robot-walker-lineage configuration, not the human-derived ones, agrees most
closely with the library, while the six-synergy configuration, the most strongly human-
derived in its pattern formation, agrees least closely; closing that gap is a tuning-program
question rather than a tool question, and it is not pursued further here. On the physical-
model side, the SolidWorks knee assembly is imported into Simscape Multibody with its
degree-of-freedom defect documented and its fix defined (Section 6.2), and the imported
gait2392 simbody mechanism awaits its muscle blocks; neither physical model yet carries
a tuned controller, so their controller comparisons remain the MuJoCo-side results above.
The walking status of the tuned configurations deserves a precise statement, because
the chapter’s records support exactly this and no more. Each tuned configuration was
brought through the curriculum’s air and balance stages: deafferented and afferented air
stepping with the legs suspended, and standing balance that absorbs push perturbations
without falling (the production configuration survived 15 stand-and-push evaluations,
Section 6.3.6). On the ground, all walking evaluations run under the support harness of
the tuning pipeline, where the configurations produce gross walking-like progression,
with cycles detected and scored but with right-leg-only or asymmetric gait patterns (Sec-
tion 6.3.5). A subsequent drive-weaning study re-tuned the feedback gains while forcing

PDF PAGE 223
200
the hand-drive down a ladder of scales. Its finalist improves the default build’s evaluation
score from ´161.57 to ´145.07 with a bilateral gait of ten contact cycles per leg (contact
duty 0.71 and 0.65, no non-finite states), survives a closed-loop basin check in eight of eight
perturbed evaluations, and degrades gracefully against independent walking references;
with its feedback held fixed, bilateral stepping persists at every drive scale down to zero
hand-drive, at which the drive pathway is absent from the network entirely, whereas the
un-retuned configuration collapses below 85 % of full drive. The gait remains in-place al-
ternation under the support rig, without forward progression. Unsupported, harness-free
walking has not been demonstrated in this program’s records: the BPA-actuated robot’s
ground model stands without walking under its untuned default gains and reaches only
supported progression at weaned drive (Section 6.5.4), and the archived reference walkers
do not walk unsupported either. Whether any configuration walks without the harness is
therefore an open question, and the balance and weight-bearing layers that would answer
it are the first item of the simulation program defined in Chapter 7.
6.5.6 The New Tools and the Program They Enable
The chapter’s contribution is the tool set. The OpenSim–MyoConverter–SNS-Toolbox
pipeline couples the full-lower-body converted model to a two-level spinal network with
Ia, Ib, group II, and contact feedback, and it runs the full neuromechanical loop faster
than real time. The SNS-Simscape library assembles the same component models in
a standard controls environment, with the reflex demonstrations, the diameter-specific
BPA force blocks, the exported production network, and the imported OpenSim and

PDF PAGE 224
201
SolidWorks bodies behind it. The controller ports put the Deng-lineage and Li-lineage
circuits into NumPy, Simulink, MuJoCo, and Simscape Multibody realizations on the same
bodies, and the verification instruments (the cross-engine harness, the structure audits, the
perturbation and basin gates, and the spiking mirrors) are what make the ports trustworthy.
Every one of these tools is reusable by a researcher who did not build them, and the loop’s
speed is what makes deletion and stimulus experiments cheap enough to repeat.
The laboratory’s limits are on record with equal weight. The archived walkers do not
walk unsupported on the ground; the tuned networks transfer poorly to diverse human
reference cycles and walk right-leg-only or asymmetrically under the harness, or alternate
in place under the support rig at weaned drive; the systematic stimulation series at the
pattern-formation and motoneuron levels, and the motoneuron-level deletion phenomena
of the reference models, are not yet reproduced; the SolidWorks knee arrives in Simscape
Multibody with its joint welded pending re-mating; and the Shinohara, Di Russo, and
Shevtsova replications remain circuit-rule translations rather than running models. What
the tools are for is the program they define next, in Chapter 7: the balance and weight-
bearing layers that unsupported walking requires, the systematic stimulation series at the
motoneuron, pattern-formation, and rhythm-generator levels against which the circuits’
signs, gains, and phase structure will be validated, and the transfer of the whole stack
toward real-time hardware.

PDF PAGE 225
202
7 Discussion
This chapter reads the preceding studies as one program, states what that program
established, and delimits the work the results open. The overview leads with the design
factors that make improved first-pass robot design achievable, then attributes them to
the developments behind them, ordered from the actuator outward to the whole body.
The future-work subsections that follow preserve that order, and each is written for the
researchers who will take it up.
7.1 The Work as a Whole
The outcome that organizes this discussion is that improved first-pass design of a BPA-
actuated biomimetic leg is now achievable: a designer can predict actuator force, joint
torque, and range of motion accurately enough to commit to an actuator set, a resting
length, and a route before the hardware exists (Figure 7.1). At the center of that outcome
are the identified design factors that remove the rigid-body assumptions from the torque
model (Chapter 4): a constant length offset χ0, effective stiffnesses χ1 and χ2 that lump
the series compliance of the attachment brackets, fixtures, and artificial tendons into the
bracket frame’s principal directions, and a wrapping-loss factorχ3 that removes usable
actuator length as an extensor bends over the joint. The terms were identified on the
pinned knee, and they hold up beyond it: carried unchanged to the biomimetic knee
with its reinforced frame, the corrected model explains the bulk of the measured torque
variance there as well, cutting the biomimetic extensor’s fraction of variance unexplained

PDF PAGE 226
203
from 4.49 to 0.67 with the shared terms (Section 4.3.2). Whether the set is complete is itself
now an experimental question: a fourth term χ4, a stiffness acting along the force path
independently of force orientation and presently absorbed by the stiffness pair ( χ1 and
χ2), is arguably the next candidate, and the identification machinery delivered here is the
instrument for settling it.
Improved BPA characterization is the first enabler (Chapter 3). The maximum isometric
force of Festo BPAs was shown to depend strongly on resting length, a previously unre-
ported characteristic that corrects the manufacturer’s tool at exactly the short lengths a leg
design requires, and a nondimensional force surface predicts isometric force from relative
strain and relative pressure with only three diameter-dependent coefficients. Because rest-
ing length is a primary degree of freedom in actuator selection, this model is what makes
that selection rational. The quasi-static surface is complemented at the dynamic end by the
state-space, optimization-based model of pressure and force dynamics in pulse-modulated
BPAs [Elzein et al., 2025], which supplies the actuator behavior that the isometric maps
cannot.
Attachment and routing optimization is the second enabler, and it is tiered. The
placement optimization of Morrow, Bolen, and Hunt found artificial muscle attachment
locations that reproduce human torque profiles and ranges of motion about the trunk and
leg joints [Morrow et al., 2020], converting the earlier finding that transplanted human
attachment locations do not transfer directly to a BPA-actuated robot [Bolen, 2019, Bolen
and Hunt, 2019] into a usable design tool; the route redesigns of Section 4.3.2 rerun that
machinery under the corrected torque model. At the route-selection tier, with two 20mm
BPAs per route and routes chosen with the actuators’ bending in mind, the redesigned

PDF PAGE 227
204
flexor and extensor each meet or exceed the magnitude of their human-scaled torque
targets across the full knee range of motion, and the cam-profile extensor converts the
direct route’s thin margin into genuine design headroom (Section 4.3.2). Stated as a design
program, the generalization is one or more BPAs replacing one or more human muscles
across one or more joints, serving the two degrees of freedom that matter most at each
joint, with routes grown outward from positions close to the bone and held to the spatial
constraints of the candidate actuators and of the BPAs already mounted; the first whole-
body pass over the robot’s actuator set, with its successes and its failures, is reported in
Section 4.3.2. Where the attachments are fixed and only the route and the tendon length
can change, the cam-shape solver applies: the tibial-side route points trace a designed cam
profile, trading biomimicry for torque-profile optimization and reduced component forces.
The Sensory Afferent Database (SADb) is the third enabler (Chapter 5). It organizes
the sensory-afferent literature into a structured afferent resource [Bolen et al., 2023]: of the
561 papers retained after the audit and the prune, 523 carry structured curation notes, 86
carry an afferent classification, and 258 carry notes translating each paper toward robot
or simulation use, and the full audited corpus of 943 papers, including the 382 pruned
records preserved under an archived flag, remains reconstructible in the packaged tool.
The toolset matters as much as the corpus. Its detailed table drills from any filter down to
a paper’s full notes; its pivot view counts the corpus along any two dimensions at once; its
bubble map draws every paper as a neuron, soma sized by citations, with citation links
rendered as excitatory and inhibitory synapses in the laboratory’s convention and a focus
mode that walks the citation neighborhood; and the monthly skimmer–curator workflow
sweeps the recent literature for candidate records and routes every one of them to a human

PDF PAGE 228
205
ruling. The evaluation of AI-assisted literature triage established the division of labor that
makes expansion affordable [Bolen and Hunt, 2024]: the machine drafts, and the human
rules.
The fourth enabler is the set of novel neuromechanical simulation tools (Chapter 6): the
audited OpenSim-to-MuJoCo conversion of the robot’s musculoskeletal model, the force
interface that computes actuator forces inside the loop from the validated BPA model, the
two-level spinal networks built in SNS-Toolbox, the Simulink SNS library for assembly
in a controls-engineering environment, the cross-engine verification harness, and the
spiking-neuron mirrors that test the coding assumption. On these tools the controller
program has matured from assembly into use. Production walking configurations have
been tuned and evaluated in full-body ground simulations, the first pathway-deletion
and drive-stimulus experiments have been run, the gait-validation sweep has measured
how far the tuned networks remain from the diversity of human reference locomotion,
and the contact-driven rhythm core has been shown to transfer between independent
implementations with exact period agreement and trace correlations above 0.9999. In
hardware, the companion balance-platform studies demonstrated that a BPA-actuated
limb holds itself upright under pseudo-vestibular inertial feedback [Bolen et al., 2024b,a,c].
What remains unfinished is equally specific, and it organizes the future work below: the
balance and weight-bearing layers that unsupported bilateral walking requires, wider
transfer across reference cycles, the supraspinal sensorium, and embedded real-time
execution.

force model of Chapter 3 drive actuator selection; route design runs under the corrected

7.2 Future Work
The subsections that follow are options for the researchers who take this program up,
ordered from the actuator outward to the whole body. Each draws directly on the delivered
models, the database, and the simulation tools.
7.2.1 Expanding the BPA Force Characterization
The isometric characterization of Chapter 3 leaves one actuator curve unidentified and
one regime unmeasured, and both are tractable next experiments. The unidentified curve
is the maximum-contraction function: ε620 could not be pinned to a predictive relationship
with resting length, so each actuator’s free contraction is recorded individually before
use. Controlled tests designed for that relationship, across multiple product batches of the
10 mm, 20 mm and 40 mm diameters, would either produce general fit surfaces or establish
batch variation as the binding uncertainty. The 20mm actuators characterized here came
from a single product batch, so batch-to-batch variation is unquantified and those fits in
particular carry that limitation.
The unmeasured regime is everything off the isometric plateau. A biological muscle
model separates active force from passive force and couples both to activation and con-
traction dynamics [Thelen, 2003, Millard et al., 2013], and Appendix A, which collects the
Thelen and Millard formulations used as torque targets in this work, is the natural home
for a BPA counterpart: extending it with the remaining equations of those models, the
Gaussian active-force shape factors, the activation and deactivation time constants, and
the tendon force curves, would let the BPA model be decomposed into active pressurized-

PDF PAGE 231
208
braid force, passive resistance of the unpressurized actuator and sleeve, and contraction
dynamics. Isokinetic and isotonic or isobaric characterizations could then be assimilated
into the same framework rather than fitted separately, with the pressure–force dynamics
model of Elzein et al. [2025] supplying the actuator-side dynamics.
Two experimental programs would complete the mechanical picture. Instron tensile
testing of actuators with their Onyx and aluminum end caps, unpressurized and pres-
surized, would measure the end-effect region that the characterization identifies as the
mechanism behind the resting-length dependence of maximum force. A finite-element
program sketched from the construction disclosed in Festo’s patent literature would give
the actuator a first-principles model to check the empirical surfaces against [Festo AG &
Co. KG, 2026]: a symmetry half-model of the actuator, a half circle fixed at the outer and
inner diameter at one end with the diameter free to expand at the other and an optional
lateral force or fixity, assembled from thin-shell or membrane layers in inter-layer contact,
with the two handed aramid weaves of the braided sleeve represented as beam or truss
elements. Figure 7.2 shows the construction such a model would represent.
7.2.2 Expanding Attachment and Routing Design
Each routing tier of Section 7.1 leaves a named next step. The knee’s flexor redesign
assumes both actuators share one attachment route; making the mirrored pair explicit is
the next refinement. The bi-pulley sweep showed that reeved routes do not yet fit the
anatomical corridors, where geometry rather than torque was the binding constraint, so
clearance-relaxed attachment sites are where that freedom should pay. The whole-body

PDF PAGE 232
209

PDF PAGE 233
210
pass needs its route-builder failure fixed and either larger actuators or relaxed corridors
on the two routes that satisfy geometry but fall short of torque.
The design element this program has so far declined is a patella. The torque model
wraps the extensor BPA over the joint surface and accounts for the loss with χ3, which was
easier than adding a patella, deterministically designing its location as a function of knee
angle, and accounting for friction (Chapter 4). A designed sesamoid is the biomimetic
answer: an artificial patella, its location optimized over the range of motion the way route
points now are, would buy back moment arm at high flexion and shield the actuator from
the joint, at the cost of a friction model and a contact schedule the optimizer must respect.
The cam-profile solver is the proof that a moving routing surface can be a design variable;
a patella is its biomimetic limit case.
7.2.3 Expanding the Sensory Afferent Database
The database’s growth now has two engines: the monthly skimmer–curator workflow that
sweeps the recent literature, and machine-assisted drafting under human ruling. The next
step the corpus itself suggests is prompt-driven passes over the existing records, designed
to discover the notes and rules latent in the corpus, candidate pathway records, missing
cross-links, controlled-vocabulary amendments, and gaps in the afferent classification,
and to draft entries for the flagged stubs and for the vocabulary gaps that the extensions
of Section 7.2.5 will need first, vestibular and visual preparations among them. Two
constraints from the curation rules carry over unchanged. The passes must be economical
and their accuracy measured against human rulings rather than assumed, and a human still

PDF PAGE 234
211
rules on every write; the triage evaluation found acceleration with substantial oversight
required [Bolen and Hunt, 2024], and nothing in the pipeline removes the evaluator. What
the passes buy is coverage at the corpus’s edges, at a cost a laboratory can afford.
7.2.4 Closing the Loop in Simulation
The simulation laboratory of Chapter 6 is assembled, audited, and in active use. The
converted robot model carries all of its muscle-tendon actuators with audited geometry,
and the BPA force interface computes actuator forces from the validated model inside the
loop; both are documented as complete in the chapter’s methods (Section 6.2). The 410-
neuron SNS-Toolbox instantiation of the spinal network, with per-leg rhythm-generator
half-centers, phase-shifted pattern-formation groups, motoneuron pools for every actuator,
and Ia, Ib, and II afferent populations, couples to the converted model at a 2 ms neural
step, and production walking configurations have been tuned against a walking reference
and evaluated on the ground (Section 6.3.5). A drive-weaning study has since produced a
feedback-sustained bilateral configuration whose stepping persists with the hand-drive
withdrawn entirely, still in place under the support rig, and the robot-side model progresses
under its harness at weaned drive (Section 6.5.5). What those same evaluations localize
as missing defines the work remaining here: the balance and weight-bearing layers that
unsupported, bilateral walking requires; transfer from a single tuning reference to the
diversity of the locomotion reference library; and the systematic verification series that
follows.
The verification series comes first because it is cheap and decisive. The circuits’ signs,

PDF PAGE 235
212
gains, and phase structure are checked against the experimental literature with the stimulus
protocols the physiology itself uses: a tonic stimulus applied at the motoneuron, pattern-
formation, and rhythm-generator layers, with the resulting outputs compared against the
tonic central and sensory stimulation that facilitates involuntary air-stepping in humans
[Selionov et al., 2009] and the deletion and stimulation phenomena reproduced by the
two-level CPG models and their neuromechanical implementations [Rybak et al., 2006,
Markin et al., 2010, Shevtsova et al., 2016]. The first of these tests is complete at the rhythm-
generator level, where tonic drive evokes an approximately 0.9 s antiphase cycle in the
isolated network (Section 6.2); the motoneuron-level and pattern-formation-level series
remain to be run, together with the motoneuron skipping-deletion phenomenon of the
reference models.
The laboratory’s hypothesis-testing program is stimulus, deletion, and addition. The
stimulus and deletion families have their first sweeps (Section 6.3.6). Pathway addition
is their complement and the one the tools have not yet exercised: adding a candidate
circuit to a tuned configuration, a commissural class, a sensory gain, or a modulatory
layer, and re-tuning, which turns connectome hypotheses into falsifiable experiments.
The closed-loop comparison of spiking and non-spiking controllers of the same topology
belongs to the same program, with the spiking mirror’s untuned bilateral ground gait as
the standing lead (Section 6.5.1).
The tools also compose across programs, and the composition is now cheap enough to
be a standard workflow. A full-body OpenSim model with arms and trunk, of the kind
MyoSuite distributes [Caggiano et al., 2022], can be brought into the SNS-Simscape library
for graphical assembly and inspection of the controller, and the same network imported

PDF PAGE 236
213
back into SNS-Toolbox on MuJoCo [Nourse et al., 2023] for the speed that tuning and
long evaluations require. A controller built in that loop inherits the conversion audit, the
cross-engine checks, and the deletion apparatus of Chapter 6 without rebuilding any of
them.
7.2.5 Extending the Sensorium
Once stepping is stable, three supraspinal contributions will be added, and a fourth is
in line behind them (Figure 7.3). First, vestibular feedback will be simulated with IMU-
like sensing of body orientation and angular velocity, extending the balance-platform
work, in which an inverted pendulum with pseudo-vestibular IMU feedback maintained
balance on an oscillating platform [Bolen et al., 2024b,a,c] and analytic neural networks
reproduced human balance dynamics [Hilts et al., 2019]. Second, ocular feedback will be
modeled to investigate how visual stabilization cues can help reset IMU drift, a well-known
practical problem for inertial sensing on robots, by treating visual measurements of the
horizon and surroundings as a low-frequency correction to the vestibular estimate. Third,
a cerebellar layer will be explored as an adaptive gateway between higher-level locomotion
commands and the CPG: a structure that can gate between gaits or movement patterns
(e.g., walking, running, and turning) and tune the phase relationships among them, in
line with the cerebellum’s established role in adaptation and coordination. Multilevel
spinal-plus-cerebellar synthetic nervous systems have already been demonstrated for
insect-like legs [Grummer et al., 2024], and hierarchical cortico-cerebellar-spinal models
have been proposed for adaptive step-length control in bipedal locomotion [Chen et al.,

PDF PAGE 237
214
2025]. The fourth addition, basal-ganglia circuits realizing the inhibitory and excititory
gating that selects among actions and locomotor modes, would give the controller a
substrate for choosing between behaviors rather than only executing one. The database
already holds the entry points for this program: the human balance and posture cluster
and the multisensory recalibration cluster are curated and connected to the locomotor
core through the citation graph (Chapter 5), and the proprioceptive–vestibular interaction
literature will guide how the signals are weighted [Akay and Murray, 2021].
7.2.6 Real-Time Hardware Realization
The final stage returns to hardware, and its near version is a step-by-step integration
problem for which the laboratory’s existing equipment already stocks the parts. The
stack is fixed: Teensy-class microcontrollers handle the low-latency per-joint loops (valve
control, pressure sensing, and contact sensing), and an NVIDIA Jetson Orin serves as
the companion computer for SNS network evaluation and activation computation. The
integration steps then run in order. Execute the SNS-Toolbox network in real time on the
treadmill biped. Close feedback from the sensors the lab already uses: the joint encoders,
the actuator pressure sensors, the liquid-wire strain gauges, the pressure manifolds, and
the IMUs. Compute the afferent signals from those streams exactly as the simulation
tools compute them, Ia length and velocity and II length from the encoders, and Ib force
from pressure-based force estimates checked against the strain gauges. Tune computer-
in-the-loop, with the embedded stack executing the network against the simulated body
before it executes against the physical one, so that gain changes are tested at simulation

speed and validated on hardware only when they survive. Keep the serial connection
to the host computer for data logging and high-level commands only (e.g., run, walk,
or stimulate a named neural pathway), so the control loop stays fully embedded. The
wiring schematics, the neural-pathway schematics, and the step-by-step commissioning
procedure that this integration produces belong in a hardware appendix rather than in this
chapter; the connectome figures of Appendix B already serve as the pathway schematics
for the spinal network.
The precedent and the margins both support feasibility. Walking control of a physical
musculoskeletal biped with McKibben muscles has been demonstrated with spring-loaded
inverted-pendulum controllers [Li et al., 2024], and the AARL’s quadruped has already
traveled part of this road: a MuJoCo model of the BPA quadruped was coupled to SNS-
Toolbox, produced closed-loop coordinated stepping, and was transferred to hardware
with potentiometer and pressure feedback on a treadmill [Lutz, 2025]; the present pro-
gram extends that approach from the quadruped to a leg built on human anatomy. An
actuation-level design lever also remains open: the BPA’s limited contraction can force long
musculotendon routings, a burden visible in the extensor redesign bounds of Appendix C
and encountered in the author’s master’s routing work [Bolen and Hunt, 2019]. A block-
and-tackle “reverse pulley” arrangement, demonstrated on the AARL’s self-balancing
inverted-pendulum platform, addresses this by trading transmitted force for travel: paired
BPAs pulled through compound pulleys on a tendon coupled to the limb, increasing range
of motion at the cost of reduced transmitted force and increased reaction force on the
structure [Bolen et al., 2024b,a,c]. Because joint torque is computed in the body frame, con-
figurations that turn that reaction force to advantage may exist, making the arrangement a

PDF PAGE 240
217
candidate for joints whose optimized routes demand impractically long actuators.
Because the static force and torque models of Chapters 3 and 4 are computationally
inexpensive lookup-table candidates, they will serve as the feedforward model inside the
real-time controller, while the dynamic pressure–force behavior under pulse-modulated
control will draw on the state-space model of Elzein et al. [2025]. Embedded execution of
the complete CPG-plus-reflex network on this stack is the intended demonstration of the
dissertation project: a robot that walks with human-like biomechanics under a biologically
grounded neural controller, and whose nervous system, unlike a living animal’s, can be
instrumented, stimulated, and lesioned at will.
7.2.7 Toward Dynamic Whole-Body Maneuvers
Beyond steady walking, the long-term goal of this line of research is dynamic, whole-
body maneuvering: transitions between gaits, reactive balancing, and ultimately ballistic
maneuvers that require coordinating the full actuator set through brief, precisely timed
activation bursts. The author’s aspiration for this program is a robot that can perform a
kickflip, a maneuver that, like its skateboard namesake, demands explosive coordinated
extension, precise timing, aerial body orientation, and controlled landing. It is exactly the
kind of behavior that exercises every layer of the architecture described above, from contact
receptors and spinal pattern generation to vestibular orientation sensing and cerebellar
coordination, and it would demonstrate that biomimetic actuation and neuromechanical
control scale from steady locomotion to dynamic whole-body movement.
These subsections are options, not prerequisites; each can be pursued on the delivered

PDF PAGE 241
218
framework independently of the others. What they all draw on, the actuator and torque
models, the routing machinery, the afferent database, and the simulation laboratory, is
consolidated in Chapter 8.

PDF PAGE 242
219
8 Conclusion
This dissertation moved the biomimetic humanoid robot project from an unvalidated
design concept to a quantitatively characterized actuator set and a validated knee-level
biomechanical design, and it defined the path from those experimental foundations to
neuromechanical control.
At the actuator level, the analysis in Chapter 3 produced novel equations for calculating
isometric force in Festo 10 mm and 20 mm-diameter BPAs. The work elucidated the rela-
tionship between maximum BPA force at 620kPa and resting length, F620(l0), a previously
unreported characteristic of these actuators that the manufacturer’s tool predicts with
the opposite trend, and it produced a nondimensional force surface, F˚(ϵ˚, P˚), with only
three diameter-dependent coefficients. Taken together, these fits predict isometric BPA
force across resting lengths from a small number of measurements, correct the large over-
predictions of the manufacturer’s tool at short resting lengths, and outperform published
single-length models. In a design workflow, these equations make it possible to select
actuator diameter and resting length and to evaluate whether a proposed muscle routing
can meet force and range-of-motion requirements, before committing to a prototype.
At the joint level (Chapter 4), the improved actuator model was combined with a
compliance- and wrapping-corrected musculotendon kinematics model and validated
on two knee designs. The hybrid torque calculation method isolated the sources of error
between prediction and measurement, and the resulting correction terms (a constant length

PDF PAGE 243
220
offset, an equivalent-stiffness representation of series compliance, and a wrapping-loss
term) substantially improved torque prediction on the pinned knee and transferred to the
biomimetic four-bar knee. The biomimetic knee driven by optimized BPAs was shown to
meet or exceed human isometric knee torque over most of the range of motion, confirming
that the optimized muscle routing of the author’s earlier work is realizable in hardware
and identifying the high-flexion regime where additional flexor capacity is needed.
Beyond the specific models, the dissertation’s methodological contribution is the
actuator-to-joint design chain itself: characterize the actuator, correct for the compliance
and geometry of the transmission, validate against human biomechanics, and only then
close the loop with a controller. The neural layer that completes the chain is assembled
rather than absent: the converted MuJoCo model, the SNS-Toolbox coupling, and the
two-layer spinal network with its verified antiphase rhythm generator are already in
place. The portability of these neural primitives was verified directly: the contact-driven
rhythm core reproduces with exact period agreement and trace correlations above 0.9999
between independent NumPy and Simulink implementations, the archived AnimatLab
reference models reproduce their published rhythms headless, and a BPA-actuated walker
in MuJoCo air-steps under synthetic neural control, while the honest negatives of the same
evaluation series localize the remaining gap in the balance layer, not in the rhythm, the
actuators, or the engines.
Completing that layer is the future work scoped in Chapter 7: from balance and bilateral
coordination through vestibular, ocular, and cerebellar extensions to embedded hardware
controlling a treadmill-walking robot.
The biomimetic platform that results will permit neuromechanical experiments (neural

PDF PAGE 244
221
lesions, sensory deletions, and pathway stimulation) that are impossible in living animals,
closing the cycle between better robots and better biological understanding, and opening
the way toward dynamic whole-body maneuvers.

APPENDICES

PDF PAGE 264

Appendix A: Muscle Force and Joint Torque Calculations
This appendix documents the reference implementation of the force, torque, and
compliance equations used in Chapters 3 and 4. When present in MATLAB code, file
names are given so that any number in this dissertation can be traced to the code that
produced it.
A.1 Human muscle force calculations
A.1.1 OpenSim Thelen2003 and Millard2012 Formulations
OpenSim’s Thelen2003Muscle comprises a contractile element (first-order activation dy-
namics with distinct rise and fall time constants; active force–length curve; Hill-type
force–velocity curve) in series with a compliant tendon, plus a parallel passive fiber
element, with fiber pennation. Unlike the MuJoCo actuator, the fiber length isnot
the musculotendon length: at every instant the model solves the force equilibrium
Ftendon(Lmt ´L fiber cosα) =F fiber(Lfiber, ˙Lfiber, a), so tendon compliance filters and de-
lays force transmission and stores energy. The tendon is a quadratic-then-linear “strip”
curve anchored at the slack length Ls (force zero until Lmt stretches the tendon beyond Ls).
Millard2012EquilibriumModel, OpenSim’s newer default, retains the same equilibrium
structure but replaces the curve shapes (an asymmetric Gaussian active-force–length curve
with a plateau, an exponentially stiffening tendon) and adds fiber damping; it is numeri-
cally better behaved at extreme lengths. Both OpenSim models therefore differ from the

PDF PAGE 265
242
converted MuJoCo model in the same single respect: series-elastic compliance.
A.1.2 OpenSim Moment Arms and Human Torque Targets
The human torque targets of Section 4.1.5 are OpenSim tendon-excursion moment arms:
for each muscle, the moment arm about the knee is the partial derivative of muscle-tendon
length with respect to the knee coordinate,r(θk) =Bl MT/Bθk, evaluated across the range of
motion. The exported analysis tables archived with the test data (OpenSim Vasti MomentArm.txt,
OpenSim Vasti Results.txt, OpenSim Bifem MomentArm.txt, OpenSim Bifem Results.txt)
carry the per-muscle moment arms and the corresponding maximum-isometric torque
curves (moment arm multiplied by each muscle’s maximum isometric force) for vas-
tus intermedius, lateralis, and medialis, and for the biceps femoris short head; the op-
timization loads the vastus medialis and biceps femoris short-head columns as its tar-
gets (buildKneeExtContext20mm.m lines 119–140; Knee Flexor Data 20mm.m line 546). This
derivative definition and the geometric perpendicular-foot construction of Equation (A4)
below are equivalent for a straight muscle path crossing a planar revolute joint; the
derivative form remains correct for the wrapping and moving-path-point geometry of the
biological knee, which is why the human targets are computed in OpenSim rather than by
the robot-side construction.

PDF PAGE 266
243
A.2 Muscle Model Formulations and Cross-Platform Force Comparison
A.2.1 MuJoCo Muscle Actuator (as converted)
The conversion from OpenSim to MuJoCo re-expresses each Thelen2003 muscle as a
MuJoCo general actuator with the built-in muscle dynamics. Activation is a first-order
process with distinct rise and fall time constants (0.01 s and 0.04 s in the converted model).
Force is computed from the actuator’s normalized length ¯L= (L´L min)/(L max ´L min),
where the length range is taken from the converted geometry, as
F=F max
[︁
a f L( ¯L)f V( ˙L)´f P( ¯L)
]︁
, (A1)
with aP[ 0, 1] the activation, fL a force–length curve that peaks inside the normalized range,
fV a force–velocity curve, and fP a passive element. The peak force scale Fmax (actuator
parameter gainprm[2]) is set by the converter such that the peak force over the model’s
range of motion matches the OpenSim max isometric force; audited across all 84 active
muscles, the deviation is ď 15% (Sec. A.2.2). Two properties matter for interpretation:
(i) the tendon isrigid, so there is no series-elastic element and no tendon slack length,
and force at the joint equals the contractile-element force immediately; and (ii) all curve
parameters are shared defaults of the converted model rather than per-muscle fits.
A.2.2 Cross-Platform Force Comparison
Both models were driven with identical kinematics and identical activations along the sub-
ject01 walking trajectory (the same IK frames used for the static-optimization back-solve).

PDF PAGE 267
244
The OpenSim side is the StaticOptimization solution’s own muscle forces (full Thelen
dynamics with compliant tendon); the MuJoCo side replays the identical joint trajectory
with those activations. Table A1 reports, per muscle, the coefficient of determination R2
between the two force time series, the shape correlation, and the best-aligning lag [Prove-
nance: Code/MuJoCo SNS/spinal/ muscle force compare.py; per-muscle values for all
twelve analyzed muscles are archived inmuscle force compare.csvbeside Figure A1].
Table A1: MuJoCo vs. OpenSim muscle force along the subject01 walk at matched activa-

For eight of the twelve representative muscles the two formulations agree with R2 =
0.87–0.98 and mean-force ratios within 10%, withnosystematic phase shift (best-aligning
lag 0–2 frames, ď 34 ms); soleus and semimembranosus agree moderately (R2 = 0.74 and
0.57, respectively). The absence of a phase lead in the rigid-tendon model indicates that,
along physiological trajectories, missing tendon slack compliance does not desynchronize
force transmission; its signature is instead visible in waveformshape: for the soleus (the

PDF PAGE 268
245
largest series-elastic energy store in human gait), the OpenSim compliant tendon carries
substantial force through mid-stance while the MuJoCo rigid-tendon force follows the
activation dips. This is the expected smoothing of the series-elastic element, visible in the
soleus panel of Figure A1. The two remaining outliers are anatomically explained: rectus
femoris was rerouted in the conversion over the vastii’s patella-tracking via point, and
its moment-arm polyfit deviates from OpenSim’s path (the same coupler artifact noted
for the vastii); biceps femoris short head agrees in shape ( r2 = 0.87) but carries a scale
offset. We conclude that the converted model reproduces OpenSim muscle force generation
along physiological trajectories to a degree sufficient for neural-control studies, and that
if tendon-elasticity phenomena (energy storage, force smoothing) become material to a
claim, the appropriate upgrade is a compliant-tendon formulation rather than parameter
retuning of the rigid-tendon model.

Appendix B: Neuron Model Equations
This appendix documents the neuron and synapse models used by the synthetic
nervous systems in this project, both in the AnimatLab walker model and in the SNS-
Toolbox implementation of the MuJoCo pipeline (Chapter 6). The authoritative model state
is the working AnimatLab project file ( Biped 2xCPG wSubs/Biped 2xCPG wSubs.aproj);
after the network reorganization described in Section 6.2, a fresh standalone export
(Biped 2xCPG wSubs Standalone.asim) runs headless and carries the rhythm-generator
charts, and the earlier phase-1 instrumented export (walk new new tester added 2 axis phase1.asim)
records the run reported in Section 6.3.
B.1 Spiking Neuron Model
Each spiking neuron is a conductance-based leaky integrator with threshold and adapta-
tion,
Cm ˙V=´g leak (V´E rest) +I syn +I app, (B1)
where V is the membrane potential, Cm the membrane capacitance (gleak =C m/τm with
membrane time constant τm), Erest the resting potential, Isyn the summed synaptic current,
and Iapp any applied (tonic stimulation) current. A spike is emitted when V exceeds the
firing threshold, after whichVresets and an after-hyperpolarization (AHP) of amplitude
AAHP with time constant τAHP is applied; the threshold itself accommodates with the firing
history (relative accommodation racc, time constant τacc). Optional calcium channels are

PDF PAGE 276
253
defined but disabled in the current model (Gmax,Ca =0).
The parameter values shared by the neurons in the walker model are given in Table B1.
Table B1: Spiking neuron parameters in the AnimatLab bipedal walker model
(Biped 2xCPG wSubs). All neurons share these values except the motor neuron thresh-
old noted below.
Parameter Symbol Value
Resting potentialE rest ´60 mV
Membrane time constantτ m 5 ms
Initial firing thresholdV th ´55 mV (motor neurons:´59 mV)
Relative accommodationr acc 0.3
Accommodation time constτ acc 10 ms
AHP amplitudeA AHP 1 mV
AHP time constantτ AHP 3 ms
B.2 Non-Spiking (Graded) Chemical Synapses
Pattern-formation and motoneuron-drive connections in the walker use graded chemical
synapses, whose conductance is a clipped linear ramp of the presynaptic potential,
gsyn(Vpre) =g max clamp
(︃Vpre ´V th

, 0, 1
)︃

(︁

)︁

where Vth and Vsat are the synaptic threshold and saturation potentials andEsyn the reversal
potential. The walker model instantiates three non-spiking synapse types:
• Nicotinic ACh (excitatory drive): Esyn =´ 10 mV, gmax = 0.5 µS, Vth ÑV sat =
´65 mVÑ ´20 mV.

PDF PAGE 277
254
• Hyperpolarizing IPSP (inhibition): Esyn =´ 70 mV, Vth ÑV sat =´ 55 mVÑ
´40 mV.
•Depolarizing IPSP:E syn =´55 mV,V th ÑV sat =´60 mVÑ ´40 mV.
Spiking chemical synapses and electrical synapses (rectifying coupling weight 0.1,
non-rectifying 0.2) are also defined in the model and are available for the sensory afferent
pathways of the MuJoCo/SNS implementation.
B.3 Relation to the SNS-Toolbox Implementation
These are the standard AnimatLab firing-rate/conductance models. SNS-Toolbox [Nourse
et al., 2023] implements corresponding classes of non-spiking and spiking neurons with
conductance-based synapses, and the AnimatLab parameters above served as the physi-
cally motivated initialization for the first SNS-Toolbox instantiation of the spinal network:
a 410-neuron network with per-leg rhythm-generator half-centers, phase-shifted pattern-
formation groups, motoneuron pools for all 92 muscle-tendon actuators of the converted
model, and Ia, Ib, and II afferent populations. In that implementation the non-spiking mem-
brane and graded-synapse equations take the same forms as Equations (B1) and (B2) with
the spiking threshold and accommodation terms removed, and the afferent resting-tone
offsets are zeroed so that Ia and Ib signals are pure activity measures; the rhythm-generator
layer has been verified to oscillate at a human-like period with the two legs in antiphase
under tonic drive (Section 7.2.4). An explicit parameter-mapping and validation study
between the two implementations remains future work; the tonic stimulation tests of
Section 7.2.4 correspond to constant Iapp injections into Equation (B1) at the motoneuron,

PDF PAGE 278
255
pattern-formation, and rhythm-generator layers respectively.
B.4 Spiking-Mirror Implementation, Calibration, and Verification
This appendix documents the numeric details behind the spiking-neuron mirrors of Sec-
tion 6.2.8, in the same file-traceable form as Appendix A: the cell and synapse equations as
implemented, the calibration factors, the full verification-gate table of the MuJoCo mir-
ror, and the variant-by-variant AnimatLab conversion table. The artifacts live under
Code/MuJoCo SNS/spinal/reports spiking 20261002/ (reports, gate scripts, logs, and
metric files) andCode/MuJoCo SNS/spinal/ (the mirror builderbuild network spiking.py
and its calibration scriptcalibrate spiking.py); the Simulink blocks are built byCode/Matlab/SNS Simscape/sns build spiking.m
and tested bysns units test spiking.min the same folder.
B.4.1 Adapting Integrate-and-Fire Cells and Hybrid Synapses
Between spikes, every spiking cell of the MuJoCo mirror obeys the membrane equation
Cm ˙V=´g m (V´V rest) +I syn +I bias, (B3)
with a spike emitted whenV reaches the firing thresholdθ, after whichV resets to Vreset and
θ increases by the increment ∆θ; between spikes θ decays back toward its initial value with
time constant τθ, bounded by the adaptation floor. The tonic biasIbias parks the equilibrium
a few millivolts below threshold, reproducing the analog cells’ near-threshold operating
point. Table B2 lists the per-class values as coded (SNS-Toolbox’s SpikingNeuron class;

PDF PAGE 279
256
the toolbox’s membrane update is dimensionally consistent only atgm = 1 µS, which the
mirror therefore uses throughout). The rhythm-generator adaptation time constant reuses
the analog network’s persistent-sodium inactivation value, so that tuned adaptation values
transfer between builds; at the rhythm gate’s operating point it is 0.35 s. The mechanism
behind that value, recorded here rather than in the chapter body, is as follows. In the
analog rhythm cells of the contact-driven cores, burst termination is carried by a persistent
sodium current, a slowly inactivating inward current; in every port of this dissertation
its inactivation time constant is held fixed rather than voltage-dependent, because the
voltage-dependent form of the AnimatLab original quenches burst termination and leaves
the network latched in a static state, the failure documented with the archived-model
results of Chapter 6.
Table B2: Spiking cell parameters of the MuJoCo mirror (real millivolts;
build network spiking.py, SPIKE table, with the rhythm-generator increment su-
perseded by the calibration of record). All cells rest at ´70 mV with membrane
conductance 1 µS and the tonic bias of Table B3.

Spiking chemical synapses, in both the toolbox class used in the MuJoCo mirror and
the Simulink SpikingSynapse block, carry a conductance that decays between events and

257
jumps by a fixed increment per presynaptic spike, saturating at its maximum,
˙g=´g/τ syn between spikes,gÐmin (gmax,g+g inc) per spike, (B4)
with Isyn =g(E syn ´V post) as for the graded synapse of Equation (B2). The Simulink
HybridSpikingSynapse implements the same dynamics with the spike detected from
the presynaptic voltage crossing a detection threshold that must sit strictly between the
presynaptic resting and firing potentials (a detection threshold equal to the firing potential
fires nothing, because the presynaptic voltage resets at threshold). Reversal potentials are
0 mV (excitation, above rest) and ´70 mV (inhibition, shunting at rest) in the spiking frame,
and +8/´5 mV in the motor neurons’ 0–5 mV frame. The graded-to-spiking command
pathways (descending drive, posture, and bias surrogates) keep the graded synapse of
Equation (B2).
B.4.2 Calibration of Record
Table B3 lists every calibration factor produced bycalibrate spiking.py (spiking calibration.json),
with the measured residual of each. The spike-to-motor-neuron factors are bisected so that
a steady 40 Hz presynaptic train reproduces the non-spiking steady-state motor-neuron
voltage; the spike-to-spiking factors are rate-calibrated (a 40Hz source drives a relay cell at
40 Hz, and a calibrated inhibitory factor silences a 40Hz-driven cell); the readout-tap factor
maps a 50 Hz train to the full-scale analog level. The rhythm-generator threshold incre-
ment was set by an isolated-pair sweep followed by a full-network sweep over alternation
quality and period target.

maximum conductance max(5, 2ˆamplitude); and each connection’s own class tag is
flipped to the spiking enum. That last rule is a documented format trap: the per-connection
<Type> tag is stored independently of the synapse type, and converting only the type kills
the run at load with no diagnostic detail. Structural validation confirms for every copy
that neuron, connection, synapse-type, chart, and stimulus sets are identical to the original
and that every class tag matches its synapse type.
Figure B1 shows the ablation result at the joint level for the two-layer family: with
graded synapses kept, spiking-regime thresholds leave the joint oscillation essentially
intact, while the full conversion pins the joints. This is the visual form of Table 6.6’s
conclusion that the synapse conversion is the breaking change.
The spiking copies are headless-run artifacts only: none has been opened in the Ani-
matLab graphical environment, and graphical verification (or an equivalent acceptance
ruling) remains outstanding for every .asim listed in Table B5. The mirror work also
scoped, but did not attempt, two further conversions: the full 410-population Simulink
spinal network (whose every one of 1,392 gains is tuned for graded voltages, making a
spiking conversion a full re-tune rather than a parameter port) and the spiking mirrors
of the alternative MuJoCo network variants (separate architectures whose fresh tuning is
future work).

B.5 Connectomes of the Spinal-Network Models
The connectome of the production gait2392 spinal network (the 410-neuron SNS-Toolbox
instantiation of Section 7.2.4) is documented in Figures B2 and B3. Both schematics are
generated by Code/MuJoCo SNS/spinal/draw circuit.py from the compiled network at
the fitted walking configuration (fitted walk params.json, the IK/NNLS back-solve refit,
in which the pattern-formation and posture weights are refit against muscle activations
back-solved from recorded walking kinematics, Section 6.2.9; 410 neurons, 201 per leg plus
8 shared, and 376 inputs; the eight shared cells are the singleDRIVE and POSTURE descending
neurons and the six balance cellsBAL PF, BAL DF, BAL TRK EXT, BAL TRK FLX, BAL LAT R, and
BAL LAT L), with every motoneuron-pool membership pulled live from muscle map.py
rather than hardcoded, so the drawings cannot drift from the built network. The caption of
Figure B2 and its alt-text sidecar repeat these population counts, so prose, caption, and alt
text cannot drift apart either. Synapse markers follow the project convention throughout:
open circles are neurons, white triangles mark excitatory synapses, and solid black dots
mark inhibitory synapses.
Figure B2 draws both legs in full. A vertical dashed midline separates the sides and a
pale central corridor gathers the interleg commissural inhibition. Each side is organized in
labeled layer bands: descending DRIVE (MLR surrogate), POSTURE, and balance inputs;
the rhythm-generator half-centers with their mutual inhibition and adaptation cells; the
phase-window pattern-formation cells; the Ia, II, and Ib afferent populations with the
stance-gated Ib load-sharing interneurons; and the ten motoneuron pools per side (HIP-E
7, HIP-F 4, KNEE-E 4, KNEE-F 4, ANK-PF 9, ANK-DF 3, HIP-AB 6, HIP-AD 6, TRK-E

PDF PAGE 287
264
1, and TRK-F 2, that is 46 motoneurons per leg) driving their muscles. Figure B3 shows
the same network for one leg as a DRIVE/POSTURE to rhythm-generator to pattern-
formation to motoneuron hierarchy, with the solved standing bias injected into every
pool, the sensory column of Ia, II, and Ib afferents, and a boxed key listing every pool
abbreviation with its member muscles. The spiking mirror of Section B.4 replicates this
topology exactly (Table B4, topology-mirror gate), so these two figures also serve as the
connectivity reference for that model.

Appendix C: Model Geometry, Bracket Frames, and Compliance Parameters
This appendix documents the numeric geometry and compliance parameters behind
the force and torque models of Chapters 3 and 4 as they exist in the project’s MATLAB
code, with file names given so that any value can be traced to the code that produced
it. Route and bracket coordinates are coded in meters and reported here in millimeters;
stiffnesses are coded and reported in newtons per meter. The bracket reference points
are interpretation choices for the 3D-printed (Onyx FDM) attachment hardware, set from
CAD by the author; moving a reference point changes the identified stiffness values, so
the points are recorded here alongside the values they produced.
C.1 Static BPA Pathway Data
The static BPA model routes each actuator as a straight-segment polyline through via points
stored in a per-test Location array of dimensions Mˆ 3 ˆN , where N is the number of
joint frames in the motion sweep and M the number of via points; the first row in the
distal link frame is marked by a cross-point index. There are no binormal or surface-frame
quantities: at each frame the force direction is the normalized vector from the cross-point
row to the previous row transformed into the crossing frame,
ˆu9R ´1 pC´1 ´p C, (C1)

PDF PAGE 291
268
where pC is the cross-point row, pC´1 the previous row, and R the frame-crossing rotation
[Provenance: minimizeFlxPin.m lines 160 and 524–537 (UD) and 201–235 (computeForceVector);
minimizeExtX3.m lines 687–700; BPA struct fields, minimizeFlxPin.m lines 28–35]. The
pinned-knee test routes are archived as FlxPinBPASet.mat (5 flexor tests, M= 2, knee
angle ´120˝ to +10˝) and ExtPinBPASet.mat (9 extensor tests, M= 7, knee angle ´125˝
to+35 ˝).
Table C1 lists the five pinned-knee flexor tests; their routes are two points, femur-frame
origin p1 = [´75.0, 100.0, 32.8 ] mm and tibia-frame insertionp2 = [´50.1, ´45.0, 32.3] mm
(test 1; FlxPinBPASet.mat, kf(1), frame 1). Table C3 lists the extensor pinned-knee route
of test 1 at θk =´ 125˝; rows 1–6 are femur-frame and row 7 is tibia-frame, and as the
knee extends, rows 2–6 release onto p1 (wrap released at +35˝). Table C4 lists the per-test
constants of the nine extensor tests; the correction terms of Section 4.1.4 used tests {1,
2, 5, 6, 7, 8 }, of which the adopted fold trained on {5, 6, 7 } and held out {1, 2, 8 } for
validation; tests 3, 4, and 9 were not used in the evaluation [Provenance: fold columns of
minimizeExt10mmX3_results.mat;Dig_crossPredict.mline 46].
The biomimetic flexor route ( buildKneeFlexorContext20mm.m) is a three-point path
(femur-frame origin p1, a moving wrap point pwrap on the tibia, and tibia-frame insertion
pend), with the wrap point held on a standoff surface of radiusbpaRb about the joint and its
z placed on the projected p1–pend line; the optimizer seed is p1,0 = [´50.0, 35.0, 50.0 ] mm
andp 2,0 = [´12.24,´8.87, 27.87]mm withl 0 =415 mm and 25 mm tendon [Provenance:
buildKneeFlexorContext20mm.m lines 209–218; buildKneeFlexorRoute20mm.m lines 98–
186]. The biomimetic extensor seed route is given in Table C2, including the release
schedule of the route-elimination rule.

The knee transform is planar: every joint frame is a rotation about z by the knee an-
gle θk composed with a translation fitted to robot CAD tables of the migrating instan-
taneous center of rotation. With R=R z(θk), the robot (femur ÑICR) transform is

the pure translation [f 13(θk), f14(θk), 0]J (x: 29.66 to ´7.58 mm; y: 25.97 to 22.33 mm),

C.3 Bracket Reference Points and Frames
Table C5 lists the bracket reference points. Each compliant bracket carries an instantaneous
body-fixed frame anchored at its reference point, constructed by two rotations (yaw about
z, then pitch about the yawed y). With pb the vector from the reference point to the
bracket-to-BPA attachment target, the yaw and pitch angles are
θbr =atan2(p by,p bx),ϕ br =atan2(p 1
bz,p 1
bx)withp 1
b =R z(θbr)Jpb, (C3)
and the bracket-frame pose is
Rkbr =R z(θbr)R y(ϕbr)J,T kbr = [Rkbr,p br;01 ] , (C4)

PDF PAGE 296
273
so the frame’s x axis aims along the nominal load path. The angles are computed per
configuration from the bracket geometry, not fixed [Provenance: minimizeFlxPin2brk.m
lines 252–298; minimizeExtX3.m lines 461–483]. As executed, however, the flexor evaluator
of record assemblesbothof its bracket frames from the yaw rotation alone (the pitch-only,
one-transform pose Tkbr = [R z(θbr), pbr; 0 1]): insertion bracket at line 289 and origin
bracket at line 305 of minimizeFlxPin.m, with the matching pitch-only point reconstruc-
tions at lines 318 and 331; the two-rotation assembly of Eq. (C4) is computed but left
commented at lines 288, 304, and 330. For the pinned extensor the two-rotation assem-
bly is live (transMode default 2trans; minimizeExtX3.m lines 3–6 and 479–482). Force
is transformed into the bracket frame by RowVecTrans(T ´1
kbr, Fk) and deformed points
are mapped back by RowVecTrans(T kbr, ¨). Because the insertion array [X1, X2, X1] is
y-symmetric, the assembly choice is numerically immaterial there; for the origin bracket it
is not (see Table C6). The pitch-only (yaw-only) variant that the earlier one-transform fit
used survives inminimizeFlxPinX3.mlines 200–209.

C.4 Stiffness Arrays and Compliance
Every configuration models bracket compliance as a diagonal Hooke-law spring in the
bracket frame, Kbr =diag(k 1, k2, k3) newtons per meter. The scalar compliance along the
force path and its series combinations are
cb = ˆuJK´1
br ˆu,c eff =c b +c ten (+c b2 for the second bracket), (C5)
exactly as in Equations (4.8)–(4.18); when an artificial tendon is present its axial spring (ˆ2
cables, effective steel area 1.51ˆ 10´6 m2, E= 193 GPa) adds in series, and the force balance
of Equation (4.22) is solved per frame by fzero on the contraction interval [Provenance:
stiffness arrays: minimizeFlxPin.m lines 387 and 400; minimizeExtX3.m lines 575–584;
minimizeExt.m line 352; minimizeFlx.m line 314. fzero force balance: minimizeFlxPin.m
line 438; minimizeExtX3.m line 611; minimizeExt.m line 389; minimizeFlx.m line 352]. The
per-configuration stiffness arrays and their series combinations are given in Table C6.
The wrapping-loss term X3 (Equations 4.24–4.26) is evaluated as X3 R|∆θ|ζ 2 per
contact arc. On the pinned extensor the two physical contact arcs use R1 = 12 mm
(27˝ to ´23˝) and R2 = 40 mm, with arc-length normalizers 27.75 /(ang 1 ´ang 2) and
92.46/(ang 2 + 120˝); on the biomimetic configurations the bend measure iswrap|θturn| with
wrap = 25 mm [Provenance: minimizeExtX3.m lines 333–359; buildKneeFlexorRoute20mm.
m line 268; MonoPamDataExplicit_balanceX3.m lines 489–494]. Table C7 records the opti-
mizer parameterization and bounds of the adopted identification: the two-bracket flexor fit
solves all three terms, and the extensor refit locks (X1, X2) to the adopted flexor-front val-
ues (the extensor front was generated with the pair fixed to the adopted values of Table C8

C.6 Redesigned Extensor Route Geometry
This section documents the direct redesigned extensor route, the first closed redesign of
the extensor configuration. It remains the reference geometry for the route-elimination
machinery and the seed from which the later work proceeded, but it is no longer the
extensor design of record: when hardware testing of the direct route began, the printed and
machined parts started breaking under the route’s higher actuator forces, so the operating
force had to be lowered, and the cam-profile redesign of Section 4.3.2, which converts
the direct route’s thin torque margin into headroom at reduced force, was developed
in response and supersedes it as the current design. The geometry below is recorded
as saved by the optimization results file ( Vas_Pam_20mm_Result_20260925.mat, the run
cited in Table C2). Figure C1 reproduces the optimizer’s tiled route-geometry view from
that results file: eight pose panels sweep the 100-frame solution, drawing full flexion
(θk =´ 120.0˝), the solver frame immediately before each of the six contact-elimination
events, and full extension ( +10.0˝). The panel angles are the last poses at which the
released row is still active; the release angles of Table C2 are the first poses at which each
row is off, one pose spacing of 1.31˝ later. Beyond the release schedule, the results file also
carries a top-level removedText field reading ‘-p8’. This field is a plotting leftover rather
than a route statement: the plotting block of Opt_run_Ext.m (lines 730–736) computes
removedText only as part of each interior pose-panel title, naming the point released in the
next frame, and the value left in the results file is the one from the last interior panel, the
p8 release-event panel at +6.06˝ in Figure C1. The route of record does contain p8: like
p3, released at +6.1˝, p8 stays active from full flexion through 97 of the 100 design-grid

PDF PAGE 303
280
frames until its release at +7.4˝ near full extension (Table C2), so the seed table and release
schedule document the route as run. Each panel draws the active p1:p9 route in the femur
frame (tibia-side rows transformed through the ICR) over the femur-cylinder, femur-wall,
corrected-condyle, and tibia clearance envelopes of the route-elimination rule, with the
local bend-radius checks at p2 and p8 drawn dashed. The optimized endpoint values of
this design are p1 = [ 40.0, 137.0, 0 ] mm (femur frame; the square marker of Figure C1)
and pEnd = [34.4, ´197.1, 0] mm (tibia frame; the diamond marker), with resting length
827.7 mm and 36.8 mm of tendon.
Figure C2 places the same optimized route on the OpenSim bone-surface point clouds
(Open Sim Bone Geometry) at exactly zero knee angle: the full skeleton (femur, tibia, pelvis
and hip, sacrum, lumbar spine, and foot) is drawn behind the three human vastus reference
paths in light orange, the optimized p1-to-pEnd route is drawn in magenta with its
endpoints circled, and the optimized route carries a total BPA-plus-tendon path length
of 0.784 m at this pose. Animated sweeps of both redesigned routes over the full flexion
range are archived with the optimization results (Knee Extensor 20mm.gif, 2.3 MB, and
Knee Flexor 20mm.gif, 4.7 MB, inCode/Matlab/Mesh Optimization/Results).
C.7 Contact and Release Angles of the Wrapped Route
The route-elimination rule that schedules the wrapped extensor route is a route-topology
state machine rather than a shortest-path solver: the fully flexed route carries every
candidate contact, and each contact is released at the first more-extended knee angle at
which the straight chord between its surviving neighbors can replace it without entering

PDF PAGE 306

the exclusion envelopes. This section states the calculation in generic terms, so that it
applies to any wrapped actuator route on a rotating joint pair; the extensor route of
Table C2 is the worked example, and the release schedule the calculation produces is the
one quoted there.
The route is stored as a fixed number of rows p1, . . ., p9: rows p1 through p5 are femur-
frame points, rows p6 through p9 are tibia-frame points, and the segment from p5 to p6
crosses from one body frame into the other. The endpointsp1 and p9 are the actuator origin
and insertion, and they are always active. Each interior row is either active, a genuine
wrap contact, or eliminated; an eliminated femur-side row repeats the preceding active
femur row, and an eliminated tibia-side row repeats the neighboring active tibia row,
so the route keeps a fixed size and its segment chain crosses the joint exactly once. Let
A(θk)Ď t 2, . . ., 8u denote the active interior rows at knee angle θk. The sweep over the
design grid, here 100 poses from ´120˝ to 10˝ at a spacing of 1.31 ˝, proceeds from full
flexion toward extension, each pose inherits the active set of the previous pose, and rows
are only ever removed, soAshrinks monotonically as the knee extends.
At every pose, every active interior row is tested between its nearest active anchors,

a signed angle between the through-contact leg and the bypass chord, measured in the
native wrap sense of the candidate’s side of the crossing segment; the two cases differ only
in that sense of rotation, because the femur-side contacts turn the route one way around
the distal femur and the tibia-side contacts turn it the opposite way around the proximal
tibia. The rotation rule releases the contact once the margin turns positive, the orientation
at which the chord, rather than the contact, provides the outer boundary of the route on
that side. The gate is opened slightly early,
µj(θk)ą ´∆,∆=1.5 ˝, (C8)
as a release hysteresis: in the last poses before the contact becomes collinear with its chord,
the computed moment arm of the wrapping segment collapses, and releasing the contact
slightly before that orientation, with the collision gate below still controlling validity,
removes the artifact without changing the route family.
Opening the rotation gate is necessary but not sufficient. A removal additionally
requires the straight bypass chord AB to clear the exclusion envelopes of the candidate’s
side,

PDF PAGE 308
285
where dist is the planar clearance between the trimmed chord and the envelope and
tangency is allowed. The chord is trimmed by 2 mm at each end, capped at 45 % of its
length, because a legitimate contact departs from the clearance boundary itself. On the
femur side the envelopes Ej are the femoral-cylinder clearance circle, the condyle clearance
polygon, and the femoral-wall span; on the tibia side they are the two tibial-cylinder
clearance circles and the wall band between them. Every envelope is the normal offset of a
bone profile by the fully inflated BPA radiusR BPA,
q(ϑ) =e(ϑ) +R BPA ˆn(ϑ), (C10)
where e(ϑ) traces the physical profile and ˆn(ϑ) is its outward normal; the offset curve
shares the tangent of the physical profile, its radius of curvature isρ(ϑ) =ρ phys(ϑ) +R BPA,
and the candidate contact positions themselves are seeded at tangent points of this curve.
Because the envelopes are drawn at the fully inflated radius while the routed BPA is
contracted at the release angles, each envelope radius may be relaxed by ρ= 3 mm inside
the gate, floored at the bone profile plus 0.5mm; a rotation-gate opening that still fails the
relaxed collision gate indicates that the seed placement, not the gate, must move.
At the first pose of the sweep, full flexion, the seed route is settled to a fixpoint: every
contact whose two gates already open there is removed, and removals repeat until no
further gate opens, so that the sweep starts from a valid topology; a colinearity guard may
additionally remove a seed contact that starts the sweep already aligned with its neighbors,
and its tolerance is zero in the design of record, so every seed row of Table C2 starts active.
At every later pose at most one contact is released: if several rows open both gates in the

PDF PAGE 309
286
same pose, the row with the largest elimination margin, the one whose rotation rule is
most decisively open, is released first, and the remaining rows are retested at the next pose.
The release angle of row j is recorded as the first grid pose at which the row is inactive.
The activation angle is read from the same record with the sweep reversed, as the last pose
at which the row is active, which is the pose at which the contact re-engages when the
route is driven from extension toward flexion.
The same active-set record drives the geometric bend measure that scales the fictive-
strain wrapping loss of Equations (4.24) and (4.26) inside the stiffness-aware force balance.
Each wrap family, the femoral-cylinder contact, the femoral-condyle curve, the tibial
contact curve, and the crossing-point curve at the ICR, contributes an arc length over its
effective radius, and the total
b(θk) =
ÿ
f
R f (θk)α f (θk)(C11)
is the bend signal handed to the force model. Each family’s turn angle α f ramps with
knee angle between the two route-state events that bound it, holding zero before the first
event and its plateau after the second, so the topology state and the bending signal are
two readings of one record. The formulation is joint-agnostic: it requires only a fixed route
representation with one crossing segment, per-angle transforms between the two body
frames, and clearance envelopes on each side, so it carries unchanged to the other wrapped
routes of this program.

C.8 Supporting Route Searches and Transmission Sweeps
The following diagnostics describe earlier optimizer runs and integration status. The
five-site batch table concerns the original unity-transmission search, and it is distinct from
the subsequent size re-solves plotted below. Objective values from different formulations
are not directly comparable.
The route redesign of Section 4.3.2 placed both 20mm actuators on direct attachment
routes. The Mono/Bi-pulley algorithms extend that search space by allowing the artificial
tendon to be reeved over pulleys before insertion, and the flexor pulley run quantifies
what the added routing freedom is worth on the knee-flexor route. The pulley variant
converged to a collision-feasible layout with zero bound violations (pattern-search exit
flag 1) at an objective of 0.08465 under its own objective function, which differs from
the direct route’s objective of 74.47, so the two values are not commensurable. The best
configuration on file reeves the two actuators over a single tackle-line part at a gain fixed
at 1:1, so the pulley acts as a redirect rather than a force multiplier; even so, the redirect
alone deepened the peak predicted flexion torque from 81.23N¨m to 85.74 N¨m , a gain
of 4.51 N¨m (5.6 %), while holding the off-axis torque to at most 0.803 N m, under 1 % of
the peak on-axis value; the predicted peaks of the direct and pulley routes exceed the
human flexor torque scale of 23.16 N m by factors of 3.51 and 3.70. The Bi-pulley batch
establishes where that freedom runs out on anatomical routes. Across the five OpenSim
muscle sites attempted (soleus, tibialis anterior, rectus femoris, medial gastrocnemius, and
biceps femoris short head), every run closed with a nonpositive torque margin (objectives
of 0.929 for tibialis anterior, 0.997 for soleus, and 3.42 for rectus femoris with a residual

PDF PAGE 311
288
worst-constraint value of 0.242), and the medial-gastrocnemius and biceps-femoris-short-
head runs terminated at the infeasibility sentinel (objective 101, worst-constraint value 10,
exit flag ´2). For the four runs that report a finite route-to-route clearance, the binding
constraint was geometry rather than torque: minimum pair clearances spanned 4.26 mm
to 19.14 mm against the 25 mm separation required between adjacent routes (two 10mm
actuator radii plus a 5 mm bundle margin); the medial-gastrocnemius run records no finite
clearance, so its failure rests on the infeasibility sentinel alone. These clearance results
motivate evaluating attachment sites with more space for the reeved routes. The pulley
solutions remain optimizer-stage results and have not been propagated into the OpenSim
or MuJoCo model trees. Physical isometric tests of the direct redesigned flexor and cam
extensor are reported in Section 4.3.6.
The Bi-pulley batch applied the same reeving freedom to five muscle sites of the robot-
body model’s OpenSim leg, and all five runs failed the torque requirement, with the
medial-gastrocnemius and biceps-femoris-short-head runs terminating at the infeasibility
sentinel (Table C10). For the biceps femoris short head site, the pattern search exited with
flag ´2 and returned the sentinel objective of 101 from an initial objective of 1000; the
paragraph above records the per-run objectives and the clearance constraints behind these
outcomes.
The robot-body model itself carries one update from this work. The adductor magnus
attachment point P2 was restored to its full-precision location(´0.1357, ´0.0929, 0.0591) m,
displacing a rounded copy that was disqualified for producing a zero-length via point
that nullified the flexion torque; the regenerated mirrored model passed its validation
with zero non-mirrored pairs. At the restored location, the muscle delivers 34.28 N m of

flexion torque, 1.12ˆ the human target of 30.61 N m, and 27.62 N m of adduction torque,
0.82ˆ the human target of 33.60 N m. These redesigned routes remain optimizer-stage
results: the robot-body OpenSim model contains no pulley entities, and no via points or
markers were added to it for these routes (its eight pre-existing moving-path-point vias,
the rectus-femoris and vastii patella-tracking points, one per muscle per leg, belong to the
original muscle paths). The MuJoCo conversion used for the simulation work predates
the pulley batch and the adductor-magnus restoration, so it carries one stale path point,
the pre-restoration rounded adductor-magnus P2. Physical torque tests of the redesigned
routes are reported in Section 4.3.6.
The same redesign pipeline was then extended from the knee to all 27 muscle-actuators
of the robot-body model, and this whole-body extension is reported as work in progress
rather than a completed result. The first pass reached 19 of the 27 actuators before the run
stopped, and its chosen solutions showed that the penalty-based formulation itself was
the binding limitation: all but one of the chosen routes violated the feasibility constraints,
the sole exception being the biceps femoris long-head pair, whose worst-angle margin of
+0.03 % is indistinguishable from zero. The formulation was then restructured so that the
task envelope, the joint-angle window each route must serve, acts as a hard feasible set
rather than a penalty. In the restructured pass, twelve of the 27 actuators terminated on a
concatenation error in the route builder (the three lumbar-wall actuators, tibialis posterior,
the seven distal-foot actuators, and adductor magnus), leaving fifteen finalized routes.
Eight of the fifteen were chosen from constraint-feasible configurations: semimembranosus,
biceps femoris long head, tensor fasciae latae, soleus, lateral gastrocnemius, tibialis anterior,
iliacus, and psoas. Of those eight, six also met the torque requirement at every angle, with

PDF PAGE 314
291
worst-angle margins spanning +0.25 % (biceps femoris long head) to +250.7 % (tensor
fasciae latae); the semimembranosus and soleus routes satisfy the geometry but remain
short of their torque targets. The crash set and the two torque-short feasible routes define
the remaining engineering: the route builder needs a fix for the concatenation failure, and
the short routes need either larger actuators or relaxed corridors.
The unadjusted cam optimizer solution had minimum margins of +31.30 % against the
required envelope and +42.34 % against the plain human target; the adjusted design used
in Chapter 4 has margins of+30.83 % and+39.77 %, respectively.

per-test fits appear in Chapter 4 (Tables 4.3, 4.4, and 4.5).
